## Monologue

I’ll begin with a story.

The first thing Mara noticed was that the sea had returned the wrong boat.

Her father’s skiff had been blue, with a white stripe along the gunwale and a patch in the sail shaped roughly like a sleeping dog. The boat resting against the harbor steps was green. It had no sail at all. Its little brass bell was ringing, though there was no wind.

Nobody else seemed to hear it.

Along the quay, the fishmongers were opening their shutters. Old Tomas was arguing with a crate of crabs. Two gulls fought over something neither of them would have wanted alone.

The bell rang again.

Mara set down her basket.

There was a name painted across the boat’s stern, in careful gold letters:

*Tomorrow.*

“That’s a poor name for a boat,” Tomas said behind her.

“I thought you were busy with the crabs.”

“They’ve adopted a more reasonable position.”

“Do you know whose it is?”

Tomas leaned over the rail, and his expression changed. Not much; just enough that Mara noticed.

“Go home,” he said.

That was when she climbed down into the boat.

---

Inside was a wooden box, a coil of rope, and a loaf of bread still warm enough to steam in the morning cold.

Mara touched the bread first.

She would later wonder about that. Of all the strange things before her, the bread had been the one she needed to understand immediately. Boats could drift into a harbor. Bells could catch a breeze she hadn’t felt. But warm bread meant an oven, and an oven meant somebody had been nearby.

Under the loaf was an envelope.

Her name was written on it in her own handwriting.

From above, Tomas said something very softly.

“What?”

“I said don’t open that.”

She opened it.

There were only seven words.

*When he returns, ask about the lighthouse.*

Mara read the sentence three times.

Her father had been gone for nine years.

The lighthouse had burned down eleven years ago.

And as she stood in the green boat with the letter trembling between her hands, its bell began to ring so violently that people all along the harbor turned to look.

Not at her.

At the man walking across the water.

He came slowly, as though making his way across a field after rain. His coat was dark. His hair had gone almost white.

At the first harbor step, he stumbled.

Mara dropped the letter and ran to him.

For a moment, there was nothing strange in the world at all. There was only the scratch of his beard against her forehead, the smell of salt in his coat, and the sound he made when he said her name.

Then she remembered.

“The lighthouse,” she whispered.

Her father let go of her.

Behind them, the bell stopped.

“What did you say?”

She could already hear the answer he wanted. She had misheard something. She was tired. She was overwhelmed.

Instead she asked, “What happened at the lighthouse?”

His face changed in exactly the way Tomas’s had.

And from somewhere beneath the harbor, far below the boats and the pilings and the black roots of the tide, a light came on.

It was not a bright light. That frightened her more.

A bright light might have been sunlight caught in some strange current. This was a steady, deliberate glow, the color of a lamp in a window when everyone else in the house has gone to sleep.

Her father seized her wrist.

“How long has that boat been here?”

“I don’t know.”

“Did you read anything?”

She tried to pull free. His grip loosened at once.

“Mara. Did you?”

“A note.”

“Where is it?”

“In the boat.”

He looked toward the green skiff.

Tomas had come down the steps. He carried a long-handled boat hook, though Mara had never seen him need one in water so calm.

“You’re late, Eli,” he said.

Her father laughed once. There was no amusement in it.

“Am I?”

“Nine years.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

Tomas lowered the hook.

Above them, the harbor had begun to quiet. Shutters hung half-open. A fisherman stood with a cigarette burning untouched between his fingers. Even the gulls had settled on the roofs.

The glow under the water moved.

Mara saw, with a sickening twist of recognition, a circular sweep pass across the harbor floor. For an instant there were stones, chains, the ribs of an old wreck.

Then darkness.

Then the light came around again.

A lighthouse beam.

“The tower fell into the sea,” she said.

“No,” her father replied.

Tomas closed his eyes.

“It was put there.”

---

They took shelter in the chandlery because Tomas still had a key.

Mara remembered buying lamp oil there as a child, when the place had smelled of wax and hemp and oranges hidden beneath the counter. Now the shelves were empty. Fine white salt crusted the inside of the windows, though the shutters had been closed for years.

Her father bolted the door.

“That won’t help,” Tomas said.

“It might help with people.”

Mara stood between them.

“No one says another mysterious thing until somebody tells me what is happening.”

Her father turned.

There were details she had not seen on the steps. A thin scar crossed his chin. His left hand was missing its smallest finger. His coat was buttoned wrongly, the top button forced through the second hole.

He looked at her basket, still hanging from her elbow.

“You were going to market.”

“Yes.”

“What were you buying?”

She stared at him.

“Eli,” Tomas said.

“I need to know.”

“Onions,” Mara said. “Flour. Thread, if there was any blue.”

Her father nodded, and for a moment she thought he might cry.

“All right.”

“No,” she said. “It is not all right.”

He sat on an overturned barrel.

“The lighthouse wasn’t built to guide ships into our harbor.”

“I know. It warned them away from the reef.”

“That was one of its uses.”

Tomas made a weary sound.

Her father looked up at him. “You tell her, then.”

For the first time since his return, Mara saw something familiar pass between the two men: the old, quick irritation of people who had once known each other very well.

Tomas rested the boat hook against the wall.

“There are places,” he said, “where the sea is deeper than the bottom.”

Mara waited.

“Most people can live their whole lives without learning what that means. Ships pass over them. Fish swim through them. Nothing happens.”

“Until?”

“Until somebody shines a light down.”

Outside, a bell rang.

It was not the green boat’s bell. This was the deep harbor bell on the customs roof.

One stroke.

Tomas went pale.

Her father stood.

“What does that mean?” Mara asked.

Neither answered.

She crossed to the window and rubbed a clear patch in the salt.

At the far end of the quay, the customs officer had come out in his nightshirt. He was looking up at the bell with both hands held open.

It rang again.

Two strokes.

The officer backed away.

Mara turned.

“How many?”

Her father’s eyes met hers.

“How many strokes?” she repeated.

“Twelve,” he said.

“And then?”

“The tide changes.”

She looked through the glass again. The water was already climbing the lowest step.

“It changes every day.”

“Not this tide.”

---

Her father had been twenty-three when he first entered the lighthouse.

He told her quickly, with Tomas interrupting only to correct dates. The corrections annoyed Mara more than the story itself. The two men could agree that a light was turning beneath the harbor, but not whether the work had begun on a Tuesday.

The keeper then had been a woman named Iven, whom Mara remembered as a bent figure buying pears with silver coins too old for the shop scales.

Iven had taken Eli and Tomas to the lantern room after a winter storm. Three ships had disappeared near the reef. No wreckage had washed ashore.

“There was a second lamp,” Eli said. “Under the first. Built into the floor.”

“What did it illuminate?”

“Water.”

“We’re standing beside a great deal of water.”

“This water showed things.”

“What things?”

“Ships that hadn’t arrived. Storms that hadn’t formed.”

Tomas drew a stool out from behind the counter and sat down.

“At first,” he said.

The third stroke rolled across the harbor.

Mara flinched.

“At first,” she repeated.

Her father nodded.

“Then it began showing us people.”

He looked at Tomas.

Tomas studied his hands.

“I saw my brother,” he said. “He drowned when we were boys.”

“Alive?”

“On a beach. He was older than he’d ever been.”

Mara could hear small waves knocking against the quay outside.

“Iven said the lamp wasn’t showing the future,” Eli continued. “Not exactly. It was showing places the world might have gone.”

“And you could reach them.”

Neither man spoke.

“That’s why there was a boat.”

Her father looked toward the door.

“The first time, we brought back a sail needle. Just to see.”

Tomas gave a bitter smile.

“Just to see.”

“The second time, medicine. There’d been a fever that winter. We knew people were dying.”

“Did it work?”

“Yes.”

Mara frowned. She had been prepared for a warning, a tale of cursed remedies and worse bargains. The simple answer unsettled her.

“How many did you save?”

“Seventeen,” Tomas said.

“Twenty,” Eli said.

“Seventeen who would otherwise have died.”

Again the dates, the numbers. Again this careful bookkeeping at the edge of something impossible.

“What did it cost?” she asked.

Her father rubbed the place where his finger had been.

“We didn’t know there was a cost.”

The fourth stroke came, and every empty shelf in the chandlery shivered.

A bottle fell from somewhere in the back room.

It did not break.

Mara heard it rolling across the floor, slowly, for much too long.

---

“Iven closed the lamp,” Tomas said. “After she found out.”

“After she caught us,” Eli corrected.

“She understood the danger.”

“She understood some of it.”

“What cost?” Mara said.

Her father answered without looking at her.

“Things began to disappear.”

“Objects?”

“At first.”

“Stop saying that.”

He bowed his head.

“A lane behind the bakery. A fishing ground. A family no one could quite remember, though their house was still there.”

“And you kept going?”

“No,” Tomas said.

Eli said nothing.

Mara looked at him.

Her mother had died when Mara was six.

The lighthouse had burned when she was eight.

Her father had vanished when she was ten.

She had carried those facts all her life as separate misfortunes. Now she watched them move toward one another.

“You went back for her.”

Eli pressed his palms together.

“I thought I could find a place where she’d recovered.”

The fifth stroke sounded.

For a moment the room smelled strongly of oranges.

“She was there,” he said. “I found her.”

Mara gripped the back of Tomas’s stool.

“What happened?”

“She didn’t know me.”

“Because she had another life?”

He nodded.

“Did she have me?”

“No.”

It was a small answer, but it took something out of the room.

Tomas reached toward her hand, then stopped.

Eli looked up.

“I came back.”

“You came back, and then you left again.”

“I tried to close what we’d opened.”

“The lighthouse was already gone.”

“The tower was gone. The lamp wasn’t.”

“So you went into the sea.”

“I took the boat.”

Mara thought of its warm loaf, the gold letters on its stern.

“Why nine years?”

“I couldn’t find the right harbor.”

From outside came the scrape of wood on stone.

Someone knocked on the chandlery door.

Three polite taps.

Tomas rose so abruptly that the stool fell.

No one moved toward the door.

The person outside knocked again.

Then a woman said, “Eli? I know you’re in there.”

Mara did not remember her mother’s voice well enough to recognize it.

She remembered, instead, how her father used to stand when he heard it from another room.

He stood that way now.

---

Tomas took hold of the boat hook.

“Don’t,” Eli whispered.

The woman outside sighed.

It was an ordinary sound, impatient and tired.

“You can’t leave her to find all this out from you two.”

Mara stepped toward the door.

Her father blocked her path.

“That isn’t your mother.”

“You don’t know what I think.”

“I know what I thought.”

The sixth stroke shook dust from the rafters.

Under the door, a thin line of water appeared.

It was dark, but not dirty. Tiny points of light drifted within it, moving against the current.

The woman’s shadow crossed the gap.

“Mara,” she said.

Mara’s breath caught.

“Don’t answer,” Tomas said.

“Why?”

“Because we don’t know what answering does.”

That, at least, was an honest answer.

The water reached the toe of Mara’s boot.

It was warm.

She stepped back.

Outside, the woman said, more softly, “You’ve grown.”

Mara closed her eyes.

There were things she wanted to ask. Dozens, then hundreds, crowding so closely she could not distinguish them. Was there a version of the world in which her mother had stayed? Had she been kind there? Had they argued when Mara got older? Did she know the little song about the fox in the kitchen, the one Mara had forgotten halfway through?

Her father was crying silently.

That decided her.

Not against the voice. Not in favor of it. Only against standing in a locked room while everyone else made the shape of her life around her.

She went to the counter, found a stub of pencil in its drawer, and pulled a scrap of wrapping paper free from a nail.

“What are you doing?” Tomas asked.

“Making a list.”

“Of what?”

“What you know. What you think. What you’ve never bothered to test.”

The woman outside laughed.

Then, abruptly, she stopped.

Mara drew three columns.

“First question,” she said. “Who rang the harbor bell?”

---

Neither man knew.

Mara wrote that down.

“Who wrote the note?”

“What note?” Tomas asked.

“The one you told me not to open.”

“I saw the envelope.”

“You recognized it?”

“I recognized that there was an envelope addressed to you on a boat that shouldn’t have been here.”

Fair.

Her father was watching the water.

“The note was in my handwriting,” Mara said. “It told me to ask you about the lighthouse.”

Eli lifted his head.

“Exactly that?”

“‘When he returns, ask about the lighthouse.’”

For the first time, he seemed less frightened than puzzled.

“What?”

“I left no note.”

“I gathered.”

“And the bread?”

“You knew about the bread?”

He shook his head.

“I can smell it.”

So could she. Warm crust, fresh yeast. It was stronger than it had been aboard the boat.

The seventh stroke sounded.

The woman outside spoke again, but now her voice came from farther away.

“You must hurry.”

Mara crouched beside the water. The points within it were not stars, she realized. They were lamps. Tiny windows. Streets seen from a great height.

There was a village moving slowly across the chandlery floor.

She watched a square pass her boot. In it stood a tree with white ribbons tied to its branches.

Then the water shifted, and the village was gone.

“How do we stop the tide?” she asked.

“Put out the lamp,” Tomas said.

“How?”

“Break the lens.”

Eli’s head snapped toward him.

“No.”

“That was Iven’s answer.”

“Iven was wrong.”

“She died trying to do what you wouldn’t.”

“She died because she broke the housing. The lens survived, and everything around it went down.”

Mara wrote in the second column.

“You both think breaking the lens will stop this?”

“No,” Eli said. “He thinks that.”

“What do you think?”

Her father stared at the pencil.

“I think the lamp is looking for something.”

Outside, footsteps began to move along the quay.

Not one pair. Many.

Mara went to the window.

People were appearing at the water’s edge.

Some climbed the harbor steps. Others simply walked off the surface of the sea and onto the stones.

A boy in an oversized red coat.

A woman carrying a broken violin.

An old man barefoot, with a live hen tucked beneath one arm.

Farther down the quay, the customs officer stood face-to-face with someone who looked exactly like him, except for the nightshirt.

Nobody was shouting yet.

Mara knew that would not last.

---

The back door of the chandlery opened onto a narrow alley.

There were no strangers there, only a cat sitting on a rain barrel. It regarded them with the offended composure of an animal whose familiar world had become inconvenient.

Tomas went first.

Eli followed Mara, carrying the scrap of paper she had left on the counter.

“You forgot this.”

“I haven’t finished.”

“I know.”

At the alley mouth they stopped.

The harbor was full of conversations.

A woman had fallen to her knees before the boy in the red coat. He stood stiffly while she held him.

The old man with the hen was arguing with a baker over the location of a street.

Near the customs house, the two officers had started shouting identical questions at each other.

Not everyone who had come from the water was a ghost, or a lost love, or a temptation. Some looked cold. Some looked irritated. One young woman was limping badly, and a fisherman had brought her a chair.

That mattered.

Mara turned to her father.

“If we break the lens, what happens to them?”

Tomas said, “They go back.”

“Do you know that?”

He looked away.

She took the pencil from Eli and made another mark.

The eighth stroke rang.

This time, another bell answered.

It was higher, thinner, and came from under the water.

The green boat drifted against the harbor steps.

A woman was sitting in it.

Her hair was gray. Her left sleeve had been folded and pinned at the elbow. Across her lap lay a loaf of bread wrapped in cloth.

She looked up at Mara.

“Oh, good,” she said. “You asked him.”

Mara stopped walking.

The woman’s face was older by thirty years, perhaps more. But Mara knew the small white scar above her eyebrow. She knew the habit of pressing her lips together before speaking.

Most of all, she knew the eyes with which the woman was looking at Eli.

Not because they were her own.

Because she had looked at an empty place at the kitchen table with them for nine years.

Her father made a low, startled sound.

The woman in the boat smiled at him, briefly.

“Not yet,” she said. “We haven’t time for that.”

---

Mara did not climb aboard.

“Did you write the note?”

“Yes.”

“Why not write something useful?”

The older woman glanced at Tomas.

“You’d have thrown it away if it began with the truth.”

Mara could not entirely deny that.

“Are you me?”

“I’m a Mara.”

“From the future?”

“From a future. The distinction becomes exhausting, but it matters.”

She broke the bread and offered half.

Nobody took it.

“Suit yourselves.”

“Where did you get that?” Eli asked.

“A bakery.”

“Where?”

“A city you’ve never heard of, on a morning that’s no longer attached to this one.”

Tomas tightened his hold on the boat hook.

“Enough of this.”

The older Mara looked at him.

“You tried the hook before.”

“What?”

“On the lens. It doesn’t work.”

“I wasn’t—”

“I know what you weren’t going to say.”

Mara stepped down one stair.

“What happens at twelve?”

“The harbor becomes a crossing.”

“For how long?”

“If we’re lucky, an hour. If we’re not, it stays one.”

“And if it stays?”

The woman folded the cloth over the remaining bread.

“Eventually, no one can agree where home is. Streets lead to the wrong towns. Winter arrives in a house but not its garden. People go looking for someone and return to find that the person waiting for them has never met them.”

“How do you know?”

“I lived here.”

The ninth stroke began.

All along the quay, people looked up.

The older Mara held out her hand.

“We need to go to the lantern room.”

“It’s underwater,” Tomas said.

“Yes.”

Mara looked at the boat.

“How many can it carry?”

“Four comfortably.”

“There are four of us.”

Her older self looked past her, toward the quay.

For the first time, she seemed uncertain.

The woman from outside the chandlery was standing beside a mooring post.

Mara had a miniature portrait of her mother in a drawer at home. The face was younger in the portrait, and less tired, but it was the same.

Eli turned.

The woman lifted one hand.

“Hello,” she said.

---

Her name was Anja.

That was Mara’s mother’s name, too.

She seemed to understand that this was both important and insufficient.

“I’m not the one you lost,” she told Eli.

He nodded without speaking.

“I had a daughter,” she said to Mara. “You aren’t her.”

Mara found herself grateful for the plainness of it.

“What happened to her?”

Anja looked toward the sea.

“She grew up.”

That answer was harder than death would have been.

“Where is she?”

“I don’t know.”

The older Mara shifted in the boat.

“You shouldn’t have come.”

“You shouldn’t have shut the western door.”

“I shut it because—”

“I know why. I was there.”

Mara looked between them.

“You know each other.”

Anja almost smiled.

“Not well enough.”

The ninth stroke faded. Somewhere on the quay, glass broke. A child began to cry, and someone soothed him.

Mara counted the seats, then the faces.

“The boat carries four comfortably,” she said. “What about five?”

Her older self looked at her for a long moment.

Then she moved the box beneath the forward seat.

“Uncomfortably.”

Tomas grunted and climbed aboard.

---

They pushed away from the harbor without oars.

The bell on the bow rang once.

Around them, the sea lay smooth as poured oil. People still walked across it, though they did not appear to see the boat. One man passed so close that Mara could make out the stitching on his cuffs. He carried a rolled carpet on his shoulder.

“Where are they coming from?” she asked.

“Nearby,” the older Mara said.

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It is, just not a useful one.”

“Try another.”

Her older self gave a small sigh. Mara recognized that, too, and disliked it.

“The lamp brings adjacent worlds close enough to cross. Sometimes they’re very similar. Sometimes they’ve drifted a long way.”

“What decides?”

“The person looking.”

Eli sat hunched in the stern.

“I was looking for Anja.”

“I know,” the older Mara said.

“And I brought this?”

“You widened it.”

Tomas looked over the side.

“Who opened it first?”

“Iven’s predecessor. Perhaps someone before him. It was old when any of us learned about it.”

Below them, the beam swept through the water.

Mara saw the lighthouse.

It stood upright on the harbor floor.

Its white stone walls were blackened on one side. The iron balcony still circled the lantern room. Seaweed trailed from the railings like laundry.

Beyond it were other towers.

Dozens.

Some lay broken. Some stood taller than the one she remembered. One was made of red brick. Another looked like a skeleton of iron beams.

All their lantern rooms were dark.

“Hold on,” the older Mara said.

The green boat tipped forward.

Mara grabbed the gunwale.

Water rose around them, clear and silent. It closed overhead without entering the boat.

For a moment the harbor appeared above them: inverted hulls, ropes hanging into brightness, the black soles of people walking on the surface.

Then they descended beyond it.

The tenth stroke arrived as a tremor through the wood.

---

The boat settled against the lighthouse balcony.

There was no door in the opening, only a rectangular darkness.

Tomas tested it with the boat hook.

“Still fond of that thing,” the older Mara murmured.

He ignored her.

Eli stepped through first. His boots struck dry stone.

Inside, the lantern room smelled of hot metal.

The great lamp that had once warned ships away from the reef was gone. Its supports bent upward from the floor, empty.

In the center of the room sat a smaller apparatus, no taller than Mara’s knee.

A lamp.

A circular lens.

A brass handle polished by many hands.

There was nothing elaborate about it. Mara had expected something large enough to justify the damage it had done.

Anja stood in the doorway, looking at the lamp as one might look at an animal that had bitten before.

The older Mara knelt beside the mechanism.

A thin beam rose through the lens, struck a tilted mirror, and passed outward through the broken windows.

On the far wall, just above shoulder height, someone had scratched a sentence into the stone.

Mara stepped closer.

The letters were uneven.

*DO NOT ASK IT FOR WHAT YOU CANNOT LOSE.*

“Who wrote that?”

“I did,” Eli said.

She looked at his missing finger.

He tucked his hand into his coat.

“What is it looking for?” she asked.

The older Mara adjusted the brass handle.

The beam slowed.

Outside, shapes passed through the water: roofs, trees, a long white road. They were not sinking. They moved as reflections move when a mirror turns.

“The lamp isn’t looking,” she said. “The person using it is.”

“Then Father was wrong.”

“Yes.”

Eli accepted that without protest.

“Why hasn’t it stopped?”

“Because no one has finished.”

---

Tomas set down the boat hook.

“Explain.”

The older Mara rested her hand on the brass handle.

“To open a crossing, you ask for something. Not in words. The lamp finds the shape of it in you.”

“A person,” Eli said.

“A person. A cure. A day you wish had gone differently.”

“And to close it?”

“You must let the search end.”

Tomas laughed sharply.

“We tried that. We put it out. We bricked up the lower chamber.”

“You stopped looking at it. That isn’t the same thing.”

Mara watched the beam turn.

A kitchen crossed the window.

For a moment she saw herself at ten, sitting at a table while her father cut an apple with a small knife. Her mother stood by the stove, a dishcloth over one shoulder.

Then the kitchen was gone.

Mara’s hands had moved toward the light without her noticing.

Her older self caught her wrist.

“Careful.”

Mara pulled away.

“You brought me here to do it.”

“Yes.”

“Why can’t you?”

The woman’s gaze flicked toward Anja.

Mara understood too much at once.

“You haven’t finished either.”

“No.”

The eleventh stroke came.

Dust fell from the ceiling. Beyond the windows, the other towers flickered with brief, scattered lights.

Tomas crouched beside the lamp.

“If Eli began this search, Eli can end it.”

“He can end his part,” the older Mara said.

“My part?”

“Everyone who has used the lamp left something moving in it. Iven. Her predecessors. You. Tomas. Me.”

Tomas sat back on his heels.

“Your brother,” Mara said.

He nodded.

“Do all of you have to let go?”

“All of us who are still alive.”

“And the others?”

The older Mara did not answer quickly enough.

Anja crossed the room.

“She doesn’t know,” she said.

Mara took out the scrap of paper.

It was damp, and the pencil marks had blurred.

Still, there was room in the third column.

---

For a little while, no one spoke.

Mara found the silence helpful.

She had been given a father, a mother who was not her mother, a warning from herself, and a disaster measured in bell strokes. Every explanation had come with someone leaning on it, trying to make it support the thing they already wanted to do.

Now she looked at the apparatus.

The lamp burned inside a little glass chamber. There was fuel in a reservoir underneath. The lens sat in a brass frame with three adjustment screws. The handle turned the mirror, not the lamp.

“What happens if we cover the lens?”

“The pressure builds,” Tomas said.

“You tried?”

“Yes.”

“What happened if you turned the mirror away from the sea?”

Eli answered. “It opened into the wall.”

“Why is there fuel?”

No one replied.

Mara knelt.

There was a gauge on the reservoir. Its needle stood just below half.

“What is this?”

“Oil,” Tomas said.

“What kind?”

“The kind Iven used.”

“Where did she get it?”

He frowned.

“I don’t know.”

Mara leaned closer. The fluid behind the gauge was clear.

She had bought lamp oil often enough to know its smell. There was none here.

Along the reservoir’s lower edge, almost hidden beneath the mounting bracket, was an inscription.

She wiped it with her sleeve.

It was not a warning.

It was a maker’s mark.

**VALE & DAUGHTERS  
OPTICAL AND SURVEY INSTRUMENTS**

Beneath that, in smaller lettering:

**RETURN ASSEMBLY — MATCH TO RECEIVING BEACON**

Mara read it aloud.

The older woman stared at her.

“What?”

“That wasn’t there.”

“When?”

“When I was here.”

“Perhaps you didn’t look.”

“I looked.”

Mara pointed to the bracket.

“The words were covered.”

Her older self knelt awkwardly and peered beneath it.

For the first time since arriving, she looked old.

Not wise, not mysterious. Just tired and caught by something she had missed.

Eli moved closer.

“A receiving beacon,” he said.

Outside, one of the other towers lit.

Then another.

The beam from their lamp swept past them both.

Mara grasped the handle.

“Not a lighthouse,” she said.

Tomas looked at the instrument.

“A signal.”

---

The twelfth stroke began before anyone was ready.

It sounded far away, then inside the tower, then inside Mara’s teeth.

All the dark lighthouses beyond the windows came alight.

Beams crossed the water in white lines. Where they intersected, scenes opened: a forest under snow, a market beneath red awnings, a shore of black pebbles. The tower trembled.

The older Mara caught the lamp housing with her remaining hand.

“Don’t turn it!”

“Why?”

“Because I did.”

Mara stopped.

“What happened?”

The woman’s pinned sleeve shifted as she leaned forward.

“Oh,” Mara said.

“Not only that.”

Anja braced herself against the doorway.

“The western crossing.”

The older Mara nodded.

For a moment Mara hated her. Not for what she had done, whatever it was, but for arriving with a note and a loaf of bread when she could have arrived with an account of the mistakes.

Then she saw how tightly the woman held the housing.

She was afraid of being there again.

“Tell me exactly,” Mara said.

The twelfth stroke continued, stretched beyond the length any bell should have managed.

“I turned toward the brightest tower,” her older self said. “I thought that must be the receiving beacon. The light joined. Then the crossing behind us closed.”

“Where was Anja?”

“On the other side.”

“And her daughter?”

The woman nodded.

Mara looked at Anja.

“How long?”

“Seventeen years.”

The answer struck the room harder than the bell.

Mara released the handle.

“All right,” she said. “We won’t do that.”

The older woman’s shoulders dropped a little.

Outside, the lights continued to cross.

“But we will read the rest of the instrument.”

---

There were instructions on the underside of the base.

They found them only because Tomas, who had grown up moving sacks twice his size, knew how to lift something heavy without putting his hands where it could crush them.

Eli held the lamp upright. Anja wedged a piece of broken railing beneath one foot. The older Mara kept the mirror steady.

Mara lay on the floor and read.

The lettering was small, tarnished, and infuriatingly practical.

**ALIGN RECEIVING MARKS BEFORE RETURN.**

**CLEAR ALL UNREGISTERED PASSAGES.**

**DO NOT EXTINGUISH WHILE OCCUPIED.**

**IN EVENT OF SIGNAL LOSS, USE MANUAL ATTENDANT.**

Below the final line was an arrow.

It pointed toward a rectangular plate on the side of the base.

“Manual attendant,” Mara said.

Tomas looked at Eli.

Eli looked at the older Mara.

She shook her head.

None of them knew.

The plate had a slot and, beside it, a button.

Mara pressed the button.

A little brass bell rang inside the machine.

The twelfth stroke stopped.

The sudden silence made them all sway.

Then a voice said, with considerable irritation, “At last.”

It came from the lamp.

---

The voice was thin and dry.

“Station designation?”

No one answered.

“Station designation,” it repeated. “Usually written on the outer casing. Unless you’ve removed that as well.”

Mara looked around the apparatus.

“There’s no outer casing.”

“Yes. I had inferred.”

“Who are you?”

“An attendant. Has there been an incident?”

Outside, an entire field of yellow flowers drifted past the window.

“Yes,” Mara said.

“How many open passages?”

“We don’t know.”

“How many travelers?”

“We don’t know.”

“Is the return assembly intact?”

Mara looked at her older self.

“Mostly.”

The voice was silent for a moment.

Then it said, “Please do not touch anything you have not already touched.”

Tomas sat down on the floor.

It was such a sensible reaction that Mara nearly joined him.

The voice continued.

“I’ll need a local operator.”

“Here,” Mara said.

“Name?”

“Mara Vale.”

A pause.

“Please repeat.”

“Mara Vale.”

The little gauge on the reservoir swung sharply to the right.

Somewhere within the base, gears began to turn.

Eli was staring at her.

“It’s your grandmother’s name,” he said.

“I know my own name.”

“No. Vale. Your mother’s mother.”

Anja had gone very still.

The attendant spoke again.

“I’m going to ask a question which may seem irrelevant. Does your family still manufacture optical instruments?”

Mara closed her eyes.

“Apparently,” she said, “we’re about to.”

---

The attendant’s name was Sen.

He was not inside the lamp, he explained. He was speaking from a receiving station so distant that naming the distance would be unhelpful.

“Distant in which direction?” Mara asked.

“Several.”

She decided to leave that for later.

He could hear them, but he could not see them. The instrument had been assembled for an older signaling protocol, and half its proper components were missing.

“Your lighthouse keeper appears to have mounted a survey return assembly beneath a navigational lamp,” he said. “A creative arrangement.”

“Dangerous?” Tomas asked.

“Extremely.”

“What is the liquid in the reservoir?” Mara said.

“Calibration medium.”

“Not fuel.”

“Certainly not.”

“What does the flame burn?”

“The flame is a display.”

Eli looked toward the light he had chased for nine years.

“And the things it shows?”

“Possible destinations, selected by operator association. This model predates numeric routing.”

Mara’s older self made a small, incredulous sound.

Sen paused.

“Is someone injured?”

“Not recently,” she said.

Mara sat on the floor at last.

“People have died.”

The attendant’s voice changed.

The irritation left it.

“I’m sorry. Tell me what happened.”

So they did.

Not everything. There was not time for everything. But enough.

The medicine. The missing lane. Tomas’s brother. The fire. Eli’s years on the boat. The crossing that separated Anja from her daughter.

Sen did not interrupt.

When they finished, the lamp’s display dimmed slightly.

“Can you fix it?” Mara asked.

“I can help you close the unregistered passages.”

“Will people disappear?”

“Not if we do it properly.”

“And the ones already lost?”

“I don’t know.”

Mara looked at her blurred list.

It was becoming the most useful answer anyone had given her.

---

First they had to stop the beam from turning without stopping the instrument.

The distinction would have saved a great deal of trouble eleven years earlier.

Sen directed Tomas to a little lever behind the mirror. Tomas moved it with the same care he used when lifting a crab by the back of its shell.

The beam steadied.

Outside, the drifting scenes stopped.

A field stood vertically beyond one window. Beyond another, an empty street ran up through dark water toward a sky none of them could see.

“Now,” Sen said, “we establish a receiving mark.”

“How?”

“Open the wooden box.”

Mara looked toward the doorway.

The box was still under the green boat’s forward seat.

Her older self laughed once, softly and unhappily.

“You never opened it?” Mara asked.

“I thought it was ballast.”

It was, in fairness, very heavy.

Eli and Tomas carried it inside.

The lid was held by two brass catches. Neither was locked.

Inside were six flat tiles of dark glass, a coil of thin copper wire, and a booklet swollen with damp.

On the booklet’s cover, beneath the same maker’s name, someone had written:

*Read this first.*

Mara said nothing.

It took considerable effort.

---

They placed five tiles in a ring around the lamp and left the sixth in the boat.

The copper wire joined them.

As each tile touched the wire, a small point appeared in its surface. Different colors: amber, green, blue, white, red.

The sixth was dark.

“Stand on a tile,” Sen said. “One person on each.”

“What will it do?”

“Read your place of departure.”

“Mine?” Eli asked. “Which one?”

“The earliest stable origin retained in your route.”

He looked at Mara.

“Your home,” she said, hoping she was right.

They took their places.

Mara stood on amber.

Her father on green.

Tomas on blue.

Anja on white.

The older Mara on red.

The lamp flickered.

In its glass chamber, shapes formed and cleared.

Mara saw her kitchen. The onion skins she had left beside the stove. A cracked bowl. Morning sunlight on the floor.

Her father inhaled sharply.

“What do you see?” she asked.

“The quay.”

“When?”

“The day I left.”

On Tomas’s tile, the light stayed blue.

His mouth tightened.

“A beach,” he said.

“With your brother?”

“No. After.”

Anja closed her eyes.

Her older self stared at the lamp without moving.

“What does it mean?” Mara asked.

Sen answered carefully.

“The instrument has mistaken several departure points for unfinished requests. We can separate them now.”

“Can we go to them?”

A silence.

“Yes.”

Mara looked at her father.

He was gazing at a morning nine years gone.

If he stepped into it, would he stay? Would another Mara grow up with him? Would that make this one’s empty kitchen less empty?

She almost asked Sen.

Instead she asked Eli.

“What will you do?”

He turned toward her voice.

The image in the lamp trembled.

“Stay here,” he said.

She believed that he meant it.

She did not yet know whether believing was enough.

---

Anja’s destination was a room with a green door.

There was a woman inside, perhaps twenty-five, sitting at a table piled with books. She was asleep with her cheek against her hand.

A cat lay across the papers.

Anja covered her mouth.

“She was eight,” she whispered.

Sen said nothing.

The older Mara stepped off her tile.

The red light went out.

“I’m sorry.”

Anja looked at her for a long time.

“I know.”

“I thought if I closed the crossing—”

“I know what you thought.”

The younger Mara watched them.

There was no accusation loud enough, no apology elaborate enough, for seventeen years. The two women seemed to understand this better than she did.

“What happens if she goes?” Mara asked Sen.

“We register the passage, guide her through, then close it behind her.”

“Can she come back?”

“Not through this assembly once it is decommissioned.”

Anja kept watching her daughter.

“Can she hear me?”

“Not yet.”

“Please.”

The lamp’s light shifted.

In the room beyond it, the cat lifted its head.

Anja spoke her daughter’s name.

“Nell.”

The woman at the table woke.

She frowned, listened, and turned toward the green door.

“Nell,” Anja said again.

The book fell from the woman’s lap.

Mara looked away.

Some things could be witnessed without being watched.

---

Tomas refused the beach.

He did it quickly, before Sen had finished explaining the route.

“No.”

The blue light steadied.

“You needn’t decide immediately,” Sen said.

“Yes, I do.”

He stepped off the tile.

For a moment he looked older than either Mara had ever seen him.

Then he picked up the boat hook.

“I’ve got a shop full of crabs.”

Mara touched his sleeve.

He patted her hand twice and moved to the window.

Her father’s green tile went dark next.

He did not make a speech.

He only stepped down and came to stand beside her.

The older Mara remained on the edge of her red tile, one foot touching it.

“What’s yours?” Mara asked.

“A house.”

“Who’s there?”

“No one now.”

“Why would you go back?”

“My things are there.”

Mara waited.

The older woman smiled faintly.

“And my life. What’s left of it.”

“Could you stay?”

“Perhaps.”

“Do you want to?”

Her older self looked at Eli.

The desire on her face was so naked that Mara felt ashamed of having asked.

Eli held out his hand.

The woman took it.

He said her name.

Both of them answered.

It startled a laugh out of Tomas at the window.

For a brief moment the room was not a machine for sorting loss. It was a cramped room containing people who had been awake too long.

The lamp chimed.

Sen cleared his throat.

“I’m sorry. There is another matter.”

---

The open passages led into the harbor.

All of them.

Every person who had crossed would need a choice of destination before the assembly could be shut down safely.

“How many?” Mara asked.

Sen hesitated.

“Forty-three confirmed travelers.”

Tomas lowered the hook.

“We need a bigger boat.”

“The harbor itself is now inside the receiving field,” Sen said. “You can operate from there.”

“How do we get the lamp up?”

“Carefully.”

They all looked at the green skiff.

The older Mara folded her remaining sleeve higher on her arm.

“Uncomfortably,” she said.

---

They came up through the water just after noon.

At least, the sun said it was noon. The customs clock had stopped at nine minutes past six, and the baker’s pocket watch insisted it was Thursday.

The harbor had changed.

Someone had set up trestle tables.

There were blankets on the quay. Kettles steamed over two small braziers. The injured woman sat with her ankle bandaged, giving instructions to three fishermen who appeared relieved to have something ordinary to do.

Near the customs house, the two customs officers had divided a stack of forms between them.

The old man’s hen was pecking crumbs beneath a bench.

Mara stared.

Tomas sniffed.

“People do manage.”

They brought the lamp into the chandlery, where the floor was dry again.

The village in the water had gone.

Outside, the harbor bell hung still.

Mara found more paper.

This time she made four columns.

Name.

Place of departure.

Desired destination.

Uncertainties.

The last column was the widest.

---

The boy in the red coat came first.

His name was Oren.

He had been fetching water from a well when he saw the harbor at the bottom of the bucket. He thought this an excellent reason to climb in.

“How old are you?” Mara asked.

“Eleven.”

The woman who had embraced him stood behind his chair.

Her son had died at eleven.

They had already discovered that Oren was not him.

Her hand still rested lightly on the chair back.

“Do you want to go home?” Mara asked.

“Yes,” he said, then looked over his shoulder. “Can she come?”

The woman shut her eyes.

Mara waited.

After a moment, the woman knelt beside him.

“No, love. I have people here.”

“Your son?”

“No.”

“Oh.”

“My sister. And a rather stupid dog.”

He considered this.

“Mine’s stupid too.”

“What’s his name?”

They talked about the dogs for a while.

Then Oren stepped onto the amber tile, and the lamp showed a stone well in a yard full of yellow hens.

A woman was shouting his name.

He grinned with relief.

“I’m in trouble.”

“Yes,” Mara said. “Probably.”

The passage opened.

Before he crossed, he turned and hugged the woman beside his chair.

She held him for one breath, then let him go.

Mara marked the first line complete.

---

The old man with the hen did not want to return.

“My street’s gone,” he said.

“Here or there?”

“There. It had been gone for years. I thought perhaps you still had it.”

“Which street?”

“Bell Lane.”

Tomas, leaning against the doorway, straightened.

“Behind the bakery?”

“Yes.”

Mara set down her pencil.

“What was your name?”

“Jory Pell.”

Tomas sat on a crate.

The old man studied him.

“Do I know you?”

“Perhaps.”

Jory shook his head.

“Too many faces. They all run together now.”

The lamp found his home, or what remained of its route: a narrow room with a sloping ceiling and a window looking over an unfamiliar city.

He considered it without affection.

“Can I stay?”

Mara looked toward the lamp.

Sen said, “The route can be registered as a permanent arrival, provided there is a local sponsor.”

“What does that mean?”

“Someone accepts responsibility for helping him settle.”

“I’m eighty-three,” Jory said. “I can settle myself. I want a room and a bit of ground for this fool.”

The hen blinked.

Tomas cleared his throat.

“There’s a room over my shop.”

“Does it smell of fish?”

“Yes.”

“Cheap, then.”

They began bargaining.

Mara wrote *staying* beside Jory’s name.

In the uncertainties column, she added *Bell Lane*.

There would be time, she hoped, to learn what had happened to it.

---

By midafternoon, the chandlery smelled of bread and wet coats.

The baker had come with a tray of rolls, found the window into another world mildly distressing, and asked whether anyone needed butter.

They did.

Mara’s older self ate standing beside the shelves.

Eli had found her a chair three times. She kept forgetting to sit in it.

They spoke in fragments while Mara worked.

Questions she could not help hearing.

“Did you marry?”

“Yes.”

“Were you happy?”

“Sometimes. Quite often, for a while.”

“What was his name?”

“Her name was Sella.”

“Oh.”

“You would have liked her.”

“Would she have liked me?”

The older Mara considered.

“Eventually.”

Eli laughed, then covered his eyes with one hand.

Mara bent over the ledger.

The next traveler was the woman with the broken violin.

She laid it on the table as though it were a sleeping animal.

“I can’t go back,” she said.

“Why?”

“There’s a war.”

Mara waited for Sen to explain some restriction.

He did not.

“Do you know anyone here?” she asked.

“No.”

Tomas said from the doorway, “The inn needs a fiddler.”

“The instrument’s broken.”

“Can it be fixed?”

“I think so.”

“Then we’ll start there.”

Mara wrote her name down.

Leth.

Then she asked how it was spelled.

---

The two customs officers presented a difficulty.

Both were called Deren Hask.

Both could recite the same childhood address, identify the same scar on the mayor’s chin, and complain with equal conviction about the state of the harbor accounts.

One had a wife called Brin.

The other had a husband called Hal.

This resolved which world each belonged to, but did not resolve which one had arrived by sea.

“I was wearing my nightshirt,” one said.

“So was I,” said the other. “Until I dressed.”

“You took my clothes.”

“They were in my house.”

Mara pressed the attendant’s button.

“Sen.”

“Yes?”

“Can the instrument identify which one crossed?”

“It can identify both departure routes.”

“Good.”

One Deren folded his arms.

“I am not stepping on that thing.”

The other folded his arms.

“Neither am I.”

Mara stood.

She had been awake since dawn. Her father had returned from the sea. Her mother had briefly existed in the same room as a more exhausted version of herself. She had discovered that the instrument threatening her town bore her family’s name.

The two officers looked at her.

They stepped onto the tiles.

---

Near sunset, Anja went home.

She waited until the queue was shorter, until the old man and his hen had moved their belongings into Tomas’s upper room, until Leth had found someone willing to examine the violin.

Mara suspected she was delaying.

She could understand that.

Nell had been given time to prepare, though what preparation could mean for the return of a mother gone seventeen years, Mara could not imagine.

In the lamp’s light, the green door stood open.

Beyond it were two chairs at a table.

Anja turned to Eli.

He held her for a moment. Neither mistook it for a reunion.

Then she came to Mara.

“You have her hands,” Anja said.

“Your daughter’s?”

“Your mother’s.”

Mara looked down at them.

Anja smiled.

“I’m sorry. That was a foolish thing to say. I don’t know what belonged to whom.”

“It’s all right.”

“Is it?”

“No,” Mara said. “But you can say it.”

Anja nodded.

They embraced awkwardly, then less awkwardly.

When she stepped back, Mara asked, “The fox in the kitchen. Do you know that song?”

Anja’s face softened.

“Yes.”

“Could you write it down?”

“Of course.”

So, while a doorway to another world waited in the chandlery, Anja borrowed the pencil and wrote six verses on the back of an old invoice.

She crossed out one line and put it right.

“That part matters,” she said. “Otherwise the rhyme doesn’t work.”

Mara folded the paper carefully.

Then Anja went through the light.

Nell rose from the table.

The passage closed before either woman reached the other, at Anja’s request.

Mara was glad of that.

She had enough of an ending to imagine the rest.

---

The older Mara stayed until the last traveler had decided.

By then, lamps were burning along the quay.

Forty-three names filled the pages.

Thirty-one returns.

Eleven arrivals.

One undecided.

Mara looked at her older self.

“You don’t count,” the woman said.

“You’re on the list.”

“I was hoping you wouldn’t notice.”

“You wrote to me in my own handwriting.”

“That did make anonymity difficult.”

Eli had gone to fetch tea.

Tomas was negotiating breakfast arrangements with Jory upstairs.

For the first time all day, the two Maras were alone.

“Will I become you?” the younger asked.

“I don’t think so.”

“Because of what we changed?”

“Yes.”

“Does that mean you disappear?”

The older woman shook her head.

“Sen says I retain my route.”

“You asked him.”

“While you were dealing with the two customs officers.”

Mara sat beside her.

“What is waiting in the house?”

“A garden that’s probably suffered terribly. An unfinished repair to the roof. A neighbor who’ll have been feeding my cat and resenting me.”

“And Sella?”

“Died four years ago.”

“I’m sorry.”

“I know.”

Mara leaned her elbows on her knees.

“Tell me something useful.”

“About what?”

“My life.”

The older woman looked at her hands.

“I can tell you how mine went.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“No.”

From the quay came the sound of Leth testing her violin. One string was badly out of tune.

“Learn to mend your own roof,” the older Mara said.

“That’s it?”

“And don’t wait until you’re certain you’ll be good at something before you begin.”

Mara glanced at her.

“That sounds rehearsed.”

“I’ve had years to think of advice I wouldn’t have taken.”

“What would you have taken?”

The older woman smiled.

“A little money. A warm coat. Someone to watch the shop when I was tired.”

Mara stood and went to find a coat.

---

Eli came back with tea and found both of them crying.

He set the tray down very carefully.

“I can go away.”

“No,” they said together.

He sat.

They drank from mismatched cups.

The older Mara told him about a winter when the harbor froze hard enough to walk across. About Sella, who repaired clocks and was late for everything. About a dog she had never intended to keep.

She did not tell him everything.

The younger Mara was learning that this, too, could be a kindness rather than a concealment.

When the tea was gone, the older woman stood on the red tile.

The lamp showed a house with pale walls and a garden crowded with weeds.

A gray cat sat on the doorstep.

“I thought you said the neighbor was feeding it,” Mara said.

“Feeding isn’t the same as opening the door on demand.”

Eli took his older daughter’s face between his hands.

“I’m sorry.”

She closed her eyes.

“I needed you,” she said.

“I know.”

“No. You don’t.”

He bowed his head.

After a moment she touched his wrists.

“But I wanted you to know.”

He kissed her forehead.

Then she embraced the younger Mara.

The borrowed coat smelled faintly of cedar.

“Open boxes,” she whispered.

Mara laughed into her shoulder.

The older woman stepped through.

The gray cat stood at once and began to complain.

The passage closed.

---

Sen guided them through the shutdown.

There were more steps than Mara expected and fewer than Tomas feared.

They checked each route. They secured the mirror. They drew the calibration medium into a sealed chamber beneath the base.

The flame display went out.

Nothing exploded.

The harbor did not vanish.

No one forgot anyone’s name.

Tomas looked faintly disappointed, then ashamed of himself.

“Is that all?” Mara asked.

“For tonight,” Sen said.

She rested her hands on the table.

“For tonight?”

“The instrument should not be operated again in its current condition.”

“We can manage that.”

“It also needs to be collected.”

“By whom?”

“A qualified technician.”

“When?”

A pause.

“Our routes are complicated.”

Mara looked at the old maker’s mark.

“Sen.”

“Yes?”

“Who founded Vale and Daughters?”

“I’m not authorized to disclose personal records across unverified historical routes.”

“That sounds like an answer.”

“It is a policy.”

“Those aren’t always different.”

“No,” Sen admitted.

The little brass bell inside the base rang once.

“I have to release the connection now.”

“Will we hear from you?”

“If the collection route succeeds.”

“And if it doesn’t?”

“Please keep the booklet.”

Then the voice was gone.

Mara put the booklet in the driest drawer.

She locked it.

After a moment, she unlocked it again and took the key out of the lock.

---

The walk home was shorter than she wanted and longer than she remembered.

Her father carried her basket.

It still contained nothing from the market.

At the corner by the bakery, they stopped.

There was a narrow gap between two buildings.

Mara had passed it every day for years. She had always thought it was only an awkward bit of unused ground.

“Was Bell Lane there?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“How many people?”

“I don’t know.”

She nodded.

He did not offer an excuse.

That helped, though not much.

At her door, he hesitated.

She took the key from her pocket.

“You can sleep in the little room.”

“Thank you.”

“The window sticks.”

“I can fix that.”

“Not tonight.”

“No.”

Inside, the kitchen was exactly as the lamp had shown it.

Onion skins beside the stove.

The cracked bowl.

Now the light on the floor was from the moon.

Mara filled the kettle.

Eli stood just inside the doorway.

She wanted him to move. She wanted him to sit in his old chair. She wanted him nowhere near it.

After a while she said, “There’s wood in the box.”

He knelt by the stove.

His hands remembered how to lay the fire.

Mara watched without speaking.

When the kettle boiled, she took two cups from the shelf.

They would need more flour in the morning.

And onions.

Blue thread, if there was any.

---

Three days later, a crate arrived at the harbor.

No ship admitted carrying it.

It was addressed to Mara Vale at the old chandlery, though she had not yet decided to rent the building.

Inside were three replacement lenses, a pair of thick gloves, and a letter.

The letter was not in her handwriting.

That was a relief.

*Ms. Vale,*

*Collection has been delayed.*

*In the meantime, please replace the damaged housing using the enclosed components. Instructions are included. Read them before beginning.*

*Your predecessor’s adaptations have rendered the assembly unsuitable for unattended storage. We recommend a minimum of two trained operators remain available until transfer can be arranged.*

*An introductory course begins when you press the blue button.*

*There is no need to press it immediately.*

Below the signature, Sen had added one line by hand.

*I thought you would appreciate that last point.*

Mara examined the crate.

There was a blue button.

She did not press it.

Instead she carried the letter to Tomas’s shop, where her father was repairing the loose step beneath the door.

Tomas read it twice.

“Absolutely not,” he said.

Jory, seated by the window with the hen asleep in his lap, said, “What sort of course?”

Eli wiped his hands on a rag.

Mara looked across the quay at the chandlery.

Its windows needed cleaning.

The roof had a shallow dip near the chimney. There would be rot under the slates; there was always rot somewhere in an empty building. She had no money for a sign, and the drawer behind the counter still smelled of salt.

She would need another table.

And better paper.

Before she could say anything, Leth appeared in the doorway with her repaired violin tucked under one arm.

“Is this a bad time?”

Tomas sighed.

“It appears to be the beginning of one.”

Leth held out a folded page.

“I found this in the violin.”

Mara took it.

On the paper was a drawing of a bridge.

Underneath, in precise lettering, were two words:

**PLEASE ANSWER.**

---

The bridge in the drawing was not especially remarkable.

It had three stone arches and a low parapet. Someone had sketched a row of houses on the far bank and a tree leaning out over the water. The sort of place, Mara thought, that people visited on a Sunday because there was nothing urgent to do there.

“What do you mean, in the violin?”

Leth turned the instrument over.

“When the repairer opened the back, it was tucked beside the lining.”

“Old?”

“The paper isn’t.”

Mara held it toward the window. A faint watermark crossed the page: a small circle divided by three lines.

She showed it to Tomas.

He shook his head.

Jory lifted the hen from his lap and placed her on the floor.

“That’s not a bridge.”

Everyone looked at him.

“It is plainly a bridge,” Tomas said.

“Yes, and your lamp was plainly a lamp.”

Jory held out his hand.

Mara gave him the page.

The old man studied the arches.

“Here,” he said.

He pointed to a tiny shape beneath the middle arch.

Mara had taken it for a boat.

Now she saw that it was a door.

A rectangular door, standing upright in the water.

“Do you know this place?”

Jory ran a thumb along the paper’s edge.

“I’ve dreamed about it.”

“How often?”

“Not for years.”

“What happened in the dream?”

He looked toward the gap beside the bakery.

“Someone kept knocking.”

---

Mara went home for her notebook.

Her father followed her halfway across the quay before she turned.

“You don’t have to come.”

“I know.”

She waited.

He stood with the rag still in one hand.

“I thought I might make lunch.”

It was such a small offer that she nearly refused it automatically.

Then she remembered the older Mara’s advice: not the part about learning roofs, but the warm coat, the money, the person watching the shop.

“There’s barley in the jar.”

“I saw.”

“And carrots.”

“I can find them.”

She nodded and continued walking.

He went toward the market.

At the chandlery, Tomas was testing the roof beam with the end of his boat hook.

“That,” Mara said, “is not a structural inspection.”

“It’s more than the building’s had in ten years.”

“Will you help me clear the back room?”

He lowered the hook.

“For the course?”

“For a table.”

“Mm.”

“We don’t have to do anything else today.”

Tomas looked relieved.

They opened the back door.

Light fell across a room stacked with empty jars, broken shelving, and old sailcloth.

The bottle that had rolled during the bell strokes lay against the far wall.

Mara picked it up.

It was green glass, stoppered with a piece of dark wood.

Inside was a folded strip of paper.

She stared at it.

“Oh, come on,” Tomas said.

---

The message in the bottle was an invoice.

This improved Mara’s opinion of it.

It was dated forty-one years earlier and addressed to the keeper of the north light.

Three gallons of whale oil.

Six replacement wicks.

Two pounds of polishing chalk.

One smoked ham.

At the bottom, in different handwriting, was a note:

*Iven—your brother came asking again. I told him nothing. He says the knocking has moved upstairs.*

Mara carried it to the front room.

Jory had drawn the bridge again on a fresh sheet. His version had four arches.

“Are you certain?”

“No.”

“That’s useful.”

“Being uncertain?”

“Telling me.”

He gave her a dry look.

“Then I shall be useful all afternoon.”

Leth stood at the window, watching the green boat.

It had not left the harbor.

Children had begun throwing pebbles near it to see whether it would ring. So far, it had declined.

Mara laid the invoice beside the drawing.

“Did Iven have a brother?”

Tomas stopped in the doorway.

“Yes.”

“What was his name?”

“Corren.”

“Where is he now?”

Tomas read the note.

Then he sat down.

Mara brought another sheet of paper.

---

Corren had built things.

Not fine things, according to Tomas. Useful ones. Roof trusses, sluice gates, a cart with an ingenious brake that worked well except on hills.

“He wasn’t a carpenter?”

“He was whatever needed building.”

Corren and Iven had not spoken much. Their disagreements were old enough that no one remembered their beginning, and both siblings preferred them that way.

“He lived inland,” Tomas said. “Near the river.”

“By a bridge?”

“By several.”

“What happened to him?”

“He came to the lighthouse one winter. Iven sent him away.”

“Why?”

“I wasn’t there.”

“But you heard.”

“Heard isn’t knew.”

Mara looked up.

Tomas tapped the paper.

“Put it in the right column.”

She almost smiled.

“What did you hear?”

“That he’d found a room in his house that hadn’t been there before.”

Leth lowered herself into a chair.

“And the knocking?”

Tomas looked at the invoice.

“I heard that too.”

“Then?”

“Then Iven went to see him.”

“Did she return?”

“Yes.”

“Did he?”

“No.”

Outside, the green boat’s bell rang.

Just once.

The children scattered.

Mara rose and went to the window.

A second boat had entered the harbor.

It was yellow.

---

The yellow boat was larger than *Tomorrow* and considerably less elegant.

It had a square cabin, two fenders made from old tires, and a crooked nameboard that read *Authorized Personnel*.

A man stood on its deck, holding a clipboard.

He wore a raincoat despite the clear weather.

As the boat drew alongside the quay, he looked up at Mara.

“Vale?”

“Yes.”

“Collection.”

Relief moved through the room so quickly that Mara felt foolish.

Tomas was already at the door.

The man glanced at his clipboard.

“One legacy return assembly. Damaged housing. Unregistered installation. Associated auxiliary components.”

“That’s it,” Mara said.

“Any livestock?”

She looked back at Jory’s hen.

“Not associated.”

“Good.”

The man stepped onto the quay.

He had a narrow face and a patient expression that looked practiced rather than natural.

Mara noticed the boots first.

They were perfectly dry.

That meant little. Everyone who had walked across the water three days earlier had arrived with dry boots.

Then she noticed his shadow.

It fell toward the sun.

She held the door without opening it wider.

“Name?”

The man looked at the clipboard.

“Technician.”

“Your name.”

A pause.

“Corren,” he said.

Behind Mara, Tomas made no sound at all.

---

Mara did not move.

The man’s expression remained patient.

“Do you have a family name?” she asked.

“Does the collection require one?”

“It does now.”

He glanced beyond her shoulder.

His gaze moved across the empty shelves, the table, the crate of replacement parts.

When it reached the drawing of the bridge, the clipboard bent in his hands.

Only slightly.

Paper should not have bent that way.

“Where did you get that?”

Mara closed the door.

Not all the way. An inch remained.

“Wait here.”

“Ms. Vale.”

She shut it and turned the key.

Tomas had the boat hook in both hands.

Mara pointed at him.

“No.”

“He said—”

“I heard.”

Jory was standing beside the table.

The hen had vanished under it.

Leth held her violin as though she might need to defend herself with it.

Mara went to the crate.

She found the blue button.

There was no need to press it immediately.

She pressed it.

A cheerful voice said, “Welcome to introductory field operations.”

Mara leaned over the speaker.

“Sen.”

“During this course, you will learn—”

“Sen.”

“—the safe handling of associative optical—”

Outside, the man knocked.

Three polite taps.

Mara pressed the button again.

The cheerful voice stopped.

A less cheerful one said, “You’ve reached instructional support. Please state the nature of your—”

“Sen.”

There was a click.

Then, faintly, hurried footsteps.

---

“Do not let him take the assembly,” Sen said.

Mara looked at the locked door.

“Is he Corren?”

“I don’t know.”

“What do you know?”

“No collection was dispatched.”

“What can we do?”

“The replacement housing should include a shielding hood.”

Mara opened the top compartment.

There were the gloves, the lenses, and a folded sheet of dull gray material.

“This?”

Sen could not see it.

She described it quickly.

“Yes. Put it over the assembly.”

Tomas moved before she finished.

The material settled over the lamp without touching its edges, holding a shape of its own.

Outside, the man stopped knocking.

For several seconds there was silence.

Then he said, in a different voice, “Thank you.”

Mara looked at the door.

The new voice was older. Exhausted.

Tomas lowered the hook.

“Corren?”

“Tom?”

Tomas’s face drained.

“Iven’s dead,” he said.

Mara turned sharply toward him.

It was not how she would have chosen to begin.

Outside, someone sat down heavily against the door.

“I know.”

Sen spoke from the crate.

“Do not remove the shielding.”

Mara knelt beside it.

“What happened?”

“The assembly was acting as a relay.”

“For what?”

“I’m still checking.”

On the other side of the door, Corren began to weep.

The sound was quiet and unmistakably human.

Mara remained where she was.

That did not prove anything.

It also did not mean she could ignore him.

---

They spoke through the door.

Corren said he had been on the yellow boat for a very long time.

Sometimes he could remember where he was going.

Sometimes he found himself carrying a clipboard with instructions he had never read.

“I’m not alone,” he said.

Mara’s pencil stopped.

“On the boat?”

“Not exactly.”

“Then where?”

A long silence.

“In the parts between.”

Sen made a sound over the connection.

Recognition, perhaps. Mara did not like it.

“Tell us,” she said.

“Some early systems used human attendants for route stabilization.”

“You’re a human attendant.”

“I’m a trained operator working from a station. This was different.”

“How different?”

“They placed a person inside the route.”

Tomas sat on the nearest chair.

Corren said, through the door, “I built a room.”

Mara waited.

“I was asked to build a room. Measurements were wrong, but the pay was good. I thought it was a cupboard, or some sort of cold store.”

“Who asked you?”

“I don’t remember.”

The door creaked under his weight.

“When I finished, it was bigger inside.”

Leth closed her eyes.

“I went to get my sister. She told me to burn it. I told her the wall was part of the house.”

“Did you burn it?”

“No.”

The brass bell inside the covered lamp rang.

Once.

Corren fell silent.

---

Sen told them the hood was holding.

He repeated it twice, as though someone at his end had asked.

Mara could hear other voices now, too distant to understand.

“Is this still an introductory course?” she asked.

“No.”

“Good.”

Corren shifted outside.

“The bell means it’s trying to find me.”

“What is?”

“I don’t know what to call it.”

“Describe it.”

He breathed slowly.

“It gives me somewhere to go. Then it gives me the words to say. If I do what it wants, I can think about other things.”

“And if you don’t?”

He did not answer.

Mara wrote *coercion* in the uncertainties column, then crossed it out and moved it into the first.

“Can you leave the door?”

“Yes.”

“Can you walk to the bakery?”

“I think so.”

“Go there. Ask for a cup of water. Tell them Mara will pay.”

Tomas stared at her.

“What?” she said softly. “We can’t leave him on the step.”

Outside, Corren stood.

His footsteps crossed the quay.

Mara went to the window and watched him enter the bakery.

The yellow boat remained motionless against the stones.

Its shadow lay beneath it like spilled ink.

No sun could have put it there.

---

They kept the door locked.

Sen consulted people who did not introduce themselves.

Jory made tea, because it turned out he had been making tea for difficult situations longer than anyone else in the room had been alive.

Leth laid her violin on the table and examined the paper from its lining.

“There’s more,” she said.

Mara joined her.

The drawing had changed.

There were now four arches, as Jory had remembered.

Under the middle of the bridge, the rectangular door stood open.

Beyond it, someone had drawn a staircase.

At the bottom of the page, beneath **PLEASE ANSWER**, new words appeared.

Not written by a moving pen. They darkened slowly into the fibers.

**HE IS NOT THE ONLY ONE.**

Mara called Sen.

For once, he had nothing immediate to say.

Tomas looked toward the bakery.

Eli stood outside it, speaking with Corren.

He had returned with vegetables tucked beneath one arm and found a stranger shaking so badly he could not hold his cup.

Mara watched her father set the vegetables down.

Then he sat beside the man.

He did not try to bring him anywhere.

He stayed.

Mara looked back at the changing page.

“Start with what you know,” she told Sen.

---

Sen knew about abandoned infrastructure.

There were routes no one used anymore, older than the office from which he spoke. Some had closed. Some had been sealed. Some remained on maps as warnings without explanations.

“The systems were designed to preserve passage,” he said.

“Even when passage was no longer wanted?”

“Yes.”

“Who designed them?”

A hesitation.

“Several companies.”

“Vale and Daughters?”

“Not all of them.”

“That wasn’t my question.”

“No.”

Mara sat.

The maker’s name, which had seemed almost funny three days earlier, was becoming heavy.

“Can you locate the bridge?”

“We’re trying.”

“Can you help Corren?”

“We may be able to separate his route from the relay.”

“May?”

“I’m not going to give you certainty I don’t have.”

She let out a breath.

“Thank you.”

From the bakery, Eli looked across the quay.

Mara opened the chandlery window.

“Keep him there,” she called. “Food, if he wants it.”

Her father nodded.

Tomas lifted the invoice again.

“What was Iven doing all those years?”

No one answered.

He turned it over.

The reverse side was blank except for a small stain near one corner.

Then he held it against the light.

There were indentations in the paper.

Writing from a page once laid on top.

Mara found a piece of soft charcoal in the old stove.

They rubbed it lightly across the back.

A map emerged.

---

The map showed the lighthouse, the harbor, and the river running inland.

At the northern edge of town, where the river forked, a small square marked Corren’s house.

Beyond it, in faint dotted lines, was a bridge.

Four arches.

Jory touched the paper.

“Yes.”

“Have you been there?” Mara asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Dreams only?”

He shook his head slowly.

“I’m not sure they were dreams.”

Tomas read a cramped note beside the bridge.

**ATTENDANT ACCESS — DO NOT ENTER ALONE**

Below it, in a firmer hand:

**CORREN REMEMBERS THE WAY WHEN ASLEEP.**

Mara looked toward the bakery.

Across the quay, Corren had put his head on his folded arms.

Eli sat beside him, not speaking.

The cup of water stood between them.

Leth laid her hand flat over the drawing.

The paper was warm.

“Do we wake him?” she asked.

Mara pressed the support button.

“Sen. We have a map.”

---

They did not wake him immediately.

They watched.

His breathing steadied. His hands stopped shaking. Once, his lips moved, but Eli could not make out the words.

The yellow boat’s cabin door opened.

No one came out.

Mara could see darkness inside it, though the sun shone directly through the opposite window.

Tomas stood beside her.

“I’m going to say something,” he murmured, “and you aren’t going to like it.”

“Then say it.”

“We should sink that boat.”

“Do you know what would happen?”

“No.”

“Neither do I.”

He nodded.

“I said you wouldn’t like it.”

Sen’s voice came from the crate behind them.

“Do not damage the vessel.”

Tomas looked offended.

“Does he hear everything?”

“Only enough,” Sen said.

Mara almost laughed.

Then Corren began to speak in his sleep.

Eli rose and beckoned.

Mara left Tomas with the covered lamp and hurried across the quay.

The bakery was warm and smelled of flour.

Corren had not moved.

His voice was very clear.

“Fourth arch,” he said. “Wait for the river to stop.”

Mara opened her notebook.

“Who are you speaking to?”

Corren’s brow furrowed.

“Don’t count the steps. It changes them if you count.”

Eli pulled out a chair for her.

She sat.

Corren’s fingers tightened against his sleeves.

“The woman at the desk will ask what you’ve lost.”

Mara waited.

Outside, the yellow boat’s bell rang.

Corren whispered, “Don’t tell her.”

---

They woke him when he began to struggle.

It took only a touch on the shoulder, but he came upright with such terror that Eli stepped back.

For a moment, Corren stared at the table as though he had never seen wood before.

Then he recognized the cup.

“Water,” he said.

“Yes.”

He drank.

Mara waited until his breathing settled.

“Do you remember the dream?”

He shook his head.

She showed him what she had written.

As he read, his face grew still.

“I know the desk.”

“Where is it?”

“At the bottom.”

“Of the stairs?”

“Yes.”

“Who is the woman?”

“I don’t know.”

“Does she send you places?”

“No.”

He touched the line about what had been lost.

“She keeps the doors shut.”

“Against what?”

Corren looked toward the harbor.

The yellow boat’s cabin door slowly closed.

“She says we ought to be grateful.”

---

By late afternoon, Mara had learned three things.

First, the bridge existed on no current map.

Second, Corren’s house had burned down thirty-seven years earlier, though the boundary wall still stood.

Third, Sen’s organization was far less certain of its authority than its stationery suggested.

“We can send an assessment team,” he said.

“When?”

“Access depends on the route.”

“How long?”

“I can’t determine that yet.”

Mara looked at the blue button.

“Can you determine whether the thing sending Corren will keep trying?”

“Yes.”

“And?”

“It will.”

“Can the hood stay on the lamp?”

“Yes, but the relay may seek another assembly.”

“The bridge.”

“Possibly.”

“Or the violin?”

“The paper may be a passive signal rather than an active route.”

She wrote that in the second column.

Tomas entered with a plate of bread and cheese.

“Eat.”

“I’m not hungry.”

“Then disappoint your stomach with a surprise.”

She ate.

Sen waited.

When she had finished half the bread, she asked, “Can you teach us enough not to make it worse?”

The pause this time was long.

“Enough,” Sen said, “to recognize several ways of making it worse.”

It was not a stirring answer.

Mara trusted it more for that.

---

They spent the evening learning.

Not how to open doors into other worlds.

How to mark a rope.

How to test whether a voice came from a person, a recording, or an instrument imitating either.

How to establish a fixed point so that a returning traveler would not mistake a similar room for the one they had left.

Much of it was tedious.

Mara was delighted.

Tomas learned the knot for a route line on the third attempt, then complained that the name was wrong because sailors had used it for generations.

Sen asked what sailors called it.

Tomas told him.

There was a pause while Sen wrote it down.

Leth practiced reading the dark glass tiles. Her fingers, trained to tiny variations in pressure and sound, were better at the work than anyone else’s.

Jory remembered pieces of the bridge.

A bench under the leaning tree.

A stone worn hollow where people had sharpened knives.

A bell that rang when the river stopped.

Eli prepared a meal on the chandlery’s little stove.

Corren slept in the back room with the door open.

Sometimes someone checked on him.

Sometimes he woke and asked where he was.

They told him each time.

By midnight, the first page of Mara’s new notebook was full of corrections.

On the second, she wrote:

**No one goes alone.**

Underneath, Tomas added:

**Bring lunch.**

---

They went to Corren’s house the following morning.

Not to enter the route.

Only to look.

Mara repeated this at least four times, which made Tomas increasingly suspicious that she was reassuring herself.

The house stood three miles inland.

Its stone walls were blackened, its windows empty. Grass grew through the hearth. A young ash tree had rooted in what must once have been the pantry.

Corren stopped at the gate.

“I had a blue door,” he said.

No one hurried him.

Leth carried a bag of instruments from the crate. Tomas had the rope. Eli brought food, water, and a spade, though no one could say whether a spade would be needed.

Mara had the map.

Jory had insisted on coming and had been given the least heavy bag in a manner that fooled nobody.

They left the hen with the baker.

The green boat, despite remaining tied in the harbor, had somehow put its bell in Mara’s bag.

She had found it on the kitchen table that morning.

She had put it in the bag because leaving it there felt less sensible.

Now it rang.

Corren looked up.

In the doorway of his ruined house stood a woman made entirely of shadow.

She held a broom.

“Oh,” Jory said.

Everyone turned toward him.

“I remember her.”

---

The shadow swept the threshold.

It moved with the brisk irritation of someone cleaning a floor that others kept making dirty.

Mara stayed outside the gate.

“Good morning.”

The broom stopped.

The woman lifted her head.

Her face was not blank. It had features, but they were as difficult to see as a face reflected in dark glass.

“You’ve brought him back.”

Corren stepped behind Tomas.

“We’re visiting,” Mara said.

“No one visits.”

“Then this will be new.”

The woman looked at the ruined walls.

For a moment she seemed confused.

“Where’s the roof?”

“It burned,” Corren said.

She turned toward him.

“You were meant to mend it.”

“I was meant to do a great many things.”

The shadow tightened.

Mara reached into her bag, felt the little brass bell, and left it there.

“Who are you?” she asked.

The woman looked at the broom as though it might tell her.

“I keep the house.”

“What’s your name?”

“I keep the house.”

Behind Mara, Leth quietly opened the instrument bag.

The shadow noticed.

“Don’t bring those in here.”

“We won’t,” Mara said.

“Don’t measure it.”

“We won’t.”

“Don’t write down the dimensions.”

Mara closed her notebook.

The woman’s grip on the broom loosened.

“Thank you.”

---

They learned more by staying outside than Eli and Tomas had learned in years of entering places.

The woman could not leave the threshold.

She did not know whether she was alive.

She knew how many rooms the house had, but the number changed each time she tried to answer.

She remembered Corren leaving.

She remembered Iven arriving.

She remembered a fire.

When Mara asked about the bridge, the broom fell from her hand.

It made no sound on the stone.

“Is she still there?”

“Who?”

“The woman at the desk.”

“We think so.”

The shadow stepped backward.

The doorway deepened behind her.

For an instant Mara saw a corridor instead of the ash tree.

Doors on both sides.

A carpet worn thin down the middle.

A wall lamp with a red glass shade.

Then the ruin was only a ruin again.

“I won’t go back,” the woman said.

“No one is asking you to.”

“They always begin by not asking.”

Mara took a breath.

“All right. We’ll stay here.”

Jory unfolded the little stool he had insisted was necessary.

He sat beside the gate.

After a moment, Eli passed around bread.

The shadow watched them eat.

“Do you want some?” he asked.

She looked at her hands.

“I don’t know.”

He placed a piece on the gatepost and stepped away.

They waited.

---

By noon, the bread was gone.

No one had seen her take it.

The shadow’s name was Bera.

She remembered it while Jory was telling a pointless story about a mule.

He had reached the third repetition of the same incident when she interrupted.

“Bera.”

Jory stopped.

“Is that the mule’s name?”

“No. Mine.”

Mara did not reach for her notebook.

“Hello, Bera.”

The shadow touched the edge of the doorway.

“I had a room above a shop.”

“What kind?”

“Shoes.”

“Did you make them?”

“No. I kept accounts.”

Her outline grew a little clearer.

A narrow shoulder. A pin at the collar. Hair pulled back too tightly.

“How did you come here?”

“A door.”

“Did someone ask you to go through?”

“No.”

Bera looked toward the road.

“I was tired.”

No one spoke.

“I had worked all day. There was a door in the passage. I thought it led to a yard.”

Corren sank onto the low wall beside the gate.

Bera saw him.

“You didn’t know.”

He covered his face.

“I built it.”

“You didn’t know.”

Mara could feel the shape of the day changing.

They had come to look at a place.

There was a person in it.

---
