They returned to the harbor before dusk, as agreed.

Bera stayed at the house.

Mara left bread, water, and a blanket on the gatepost, though none of them knew whether she could use the last two.

She promised to come back.

Then she qualified the promise.

“Tomorrow, if the road is safe. If not, we’ll send word if we can.”

Bera nodded.

It seemed to reassure her more than a grander promise would have.

At the chandlery, Sen had news.

His station had found a record.

The bridge was part of a discontinued exchange called the Low Water Office.

“It processed returns,” he said.

“Of people?”

“People and goods.”

“Why was it discontinued?”

“Accounting discrepancies.”

Mara stared at the speaker.

“That is a terrible phrase.”

“Yes.”

“How many people?”

“The records don’t say.”

“Why not?”

A pause.

“Because the discrepancy was in what counted as an arrival.”

Tomas swore.

Sen let him finish.

“An assessment team has been authorized.”

“When?”

“Sooner.”

Mara opened her notebook.

“Tell them we found Bera. She kept accounts in a shoe shop. She wants to leave the house.”

“I’ll add her.”

“Not as a discrepancy.”

“No,” Sen said. “As a person.”

---

That night, Mara dreamed of the bridge.

She knew it was a dream because her mother was beside her, and because the sky had the texture of a ceiling she remembered from childhood.

The river beneath the four arches did not move.

At the center of the bridge stood a desk.

Behind it sat a woman in a dark coat.

She had a ledger open before her.

“What have you lost?” the woman asked.

Mara looked at the ledger.

Its pages were crowded with names.

“Sleep,” she said.

The woman’s pen stopped.

“That is not an acceptable answer.”

“It’s the current one.”

Her mother squeezed her hand.

Mara did not look at her.

The woman behind the desk smiled.

It was not an unkind smile.

That made Mara more cautious.

“We can restore what belongs to you.”

“Then you won’t need me to list it.”

The woman turned a page.

“Everyone lists it.”

“Not tonight.”

The bridge shifted beneath her feet.

Her mother’s hand grew cold.

Mara reached into her pocket.

The brass bell was there.

She rang it.

---

She woke sitting upright in bed.

The bell was on the bedside table.

It was still vibrating.

From the little room down the hall, her father called, “Mara?”

“I’m awake.”

He came to the doorway but did not enter.

For a while, neither spoke.

Then she said, “I dreamed about Mother.”

His hand tightened on the doorframe.

“Was it her?”

“I don’t know.”

“What happened?”

“There was a woman asking what I’d lost.”

He closed his eyes.

Mara waited.

At last he said, “I know that question.”

“You heard it?”

“In the boat. Sometimes. When I couldn’t find the harbor.”

“What did you answer?”

He looked at her.

“The wrong thing.”

She did not let him leave it there.

“What did you answer?”

“Anja.”

The room was quiet.

“And then?”

“The light showed her to me.”

“Again and again?”

“Yes.”

Mara picked up the bell.

She turned it in her hands until its clapper came to rest.

“Tomorrow,” she said, “you tell me all of it. Even the parts you think are only dreams.”

He nodded.

“Will you sleep?”

“Eventually.”

He started to go.

“Father.”

He turned so quickly that she realized she had not called him that since the harbor steps.

“You can leave the door open.”

---

In the morning, the chandlery’s sign had changed.

It had previously read, in faded letters:

**KELL’S MARINE SUPPLIES**

Now, underneath, in smaller fresh paint, were the words:

**RETURN INQUIRIES — KNOCK FIRST**

Mara stood on the quay with her hands on her hips.

Tomas came out carrying a brush.

“What?”

“You painted my building.”

“Have you rented it?”

“No.”

“Then I painted Kell’s building.”

“Kell’s been dead for twelve years.”

“He hasn’t objected.”

Mara examined the lettering.

It was surprisingly good.

“You might have asked.”

“You might have said no.”

“Yes.”

Tomas nodded.

“That is a difficulty.”

She tried not to smile.

Inside, the ledger lay open on the table.

There was a new name on the first blank line.

No one admitted writing it.

**Bera Venn.**

Under desired destination:

**Somewhere with a window that opens.**

Mara read it twice.

Then she fetched the ruler and drew a fifth column.

**Next step.**

For Bera, she wrote:

**Ask what she remembers of home. Confirm the route. Find another option if home is gone.**

The brass bell rang softly in her bag.

Not a warning this time, she thought.

Then she crossed that thought out in her mind.

She did not know yet what it meant.

---

The first lesson of the second day concerned invitations.

Sen had found a way to speak through a small handpiece that no longer required the return assembly. It sat on the table like a flattened black pebble.

“An invitation can establish a route,” he said. “Not always. Not by itself. But avoid accepting terms you do not understand.”

Tomas looked at the sign through the open door.

“Does ‘knock first’ count?”

“Probably not.”

“Comforting.”

“What about a dream?” Mara asked.

Sen was quiet.

She told him.

When she finished, he asked, “Did you give the clerk a name?”

“No.”

“Did you accept anything?”

“No.”

“Did you cross the center of the bridge?”

“I don’t think so.”

“Good.”

“That sounds less like a lesson and more like checking whether I’ve already been eaten.”

“That is not an accurate description of the risk.”

“What is?”

“Misrouting under assumed consent.”

Tomas put down his cup.

“You people need better words.”

Sen said, after a moment, “Yes.”

Mara took the handpiece with her when they left for the ruined house.

At the top of the page, beneath the date, she wrote:

**A person saying yes must know what they are saying yes to.**

She underlined it once.

---

Bera was waiting in the doorway.

The blanket was folded neatly on the gatepost.

The water jug was empty.

Beside it sat a small pair of shoes.

They were made of green leather, with brass buckles no bigger than Mara’s thumbnail.

“Where did those come from?” Mara asked.

“The house.”

“Do you recognize them?”

Bera nodded.

“My son’s.”

Mara stopped outside the gate.

“What was his name?”

Bera’s outline blurred.

She pressed both hands against her head.

“I had it.”

“You don’t have to remember now.”

“I had it this morning.”

Leth set down the bag.

“May I play something?”

Bera looked up.

“Why?”

“It might help. Or it might not. We can stop if you don’t like it.”

After a moment, Bera nodded.

Leth took out the violin.

The repaired wood was a shade lighter than the rest.

She played a tune Mara did not know, slow and simple, with a turn in the third phrase that sounded like someone changing their mind.

Bera sat on the threshold.

By the time the tune ended, she was crying.

Not shadows of tears.

Water darkened the stone.

“Perrin,” she said.

Mara waited until Bera looked at her.

Then she opened the notebook.

---

Perrin had been five.

Bera had left him with her sister while she worked late at the shoe shop.

It had been raining.

She remembered being angry about the rain because the soles of her own boots were worn thin, and there was something humiliating about wet feet in a room full of shoes she could not afford.

Then the door in the passage.

The yard she expected.

The staircase she found.

The woman at the desk.

“What did she ask?” Mara said.

Bera looked at the green shoes.

“What I’d lost.”

“What did you tell her?”

“My way.”

Sen’s voice came quietly from the handpiece.

“Oh.”

Mara looked down.

“What?”

The attendant did not answer immediately.

Then he said, “That may explain why she cannot leave the house.”

Bera’s face turned toward the sound.

“Can you give it back?”

“I don’t know yet.”

She nodded, once.

Mara was beginning to recognize how much courage that little movement could contain.

“Does the house have another door?” she asked.

Bera looked behind her.

When she turned back, her features were almost clear.

“Yes.”

“Where does it lead?”

“I’ve never opened it.”

“Why?”

“It has my name on it.”

---

They did not open the door that day.

They found it.

That was enough.

Bera guided them around the outside of the ruin to a section of wall hidden by brambles. Eli cut the growth away with a borrowed billhook.

The door was small, made of pale wood untouched by fire.

A brass plate read:

**BERA VENN — PENDING**

Below it was a narrow slot.

Mara leaned close enough to see through.

On the other side lay a room full of shelves.

Each shelf held boxes.

Each box had a name.

She stepped back.

Leth looked next.

Then Tomas.

Corren refused.

“No,” he said.

No one asked twice.

Sen directed them to place a glass tile on the ground and tie the route line around the door handle without turning it.

They did this slowly.

When the knot was secure, the tile showed a single white point.

“Good,” Sen said.

“What have we done?”

“Marked the route for the assessment team.”

“When are they coming?”

“Tomorrow morning.”

For the first time, the answer had a time in it.

Mara leaned against the wall.

Behind her, from within the room of shelves, someone knocked.

Not three polite taps.

A rapid, frightened pounding.

Bera went still.

Then a child called, “Mama?”

---

Mara’s hand moved toward the handle.

She stopped it with the other one.

Bera had already crossed the space between them.

“Perrin?”

The pounding ceased.

“Mama, it’s dark.”

Tomas closed his eyes.

Leth gripped the violin case so tightly her knuckles whitened.

Sen’s voice was urgent but controlled.

“Do not open the door yet.”

Bera looked at Mara.

The look would stay with her for a long time.

“Yet,” Mara repeated. “Not never.”

“He’s in there.”

“We heard a voice.”

“My son’s voice.”

“I know.”

“You don’t.”

“No,” Mara said. “I don’t.”

The child called again.

Bera pressed her forehead to the wood.

Mara crouched beside the tile.

The white point had divided.

Now there were three.

“Sen.”

“Describe it.”

She did.

“Ask the voice a question.”

“What question?”

“One whose answer Bera knows, but has not said aloud here.”

Mara looked at her.

Bera’s lips trembled.

Then she asked, “What did you call the dog next door?”

Silence.

“Mama?”

“What did you call him?”

The answer came too quickly.

“Perrin.”

Bera stepped back.

She did not scream.

She put one hand over her mouth and stood very straight.

Inside the door, something struck the wood hard enough to shake dust from the wall.

---

They left the rope tied.

They left the tile.

They took Bera away from the threshold as far as she could go.

It was farther than before.

To the gate.

Then one step beyond.

She stared at the road beneath her feet.

Mara did not congratulate her.

Bera was looking back at the little door.

“I almost opened it.”

“Yes.”

“You almost did too.”

“Yes.”

The handpiece lay silent in Mara’s palm.

From the house came another blow.

Then a voice, no longer a child’s, said something none of them understood.

Corren flinched.

“I’ve heard that.”

“Where?”

“On the boat.”

The green bell in Mara’s bag rang.

The sound was clear and hard.

The pounding stopped.

Eli looked at the bag.

“May I?”

Mara handed him the bell.

He held it by its leather loop.

As he stepped toward the ruin, the brass warmed from dull yellow to a pale, shining gold.

Sen spoke.

“Where did you get that?”

“From the green boat.”

“Bring it back.”

Eli stopped.

“What is it?”

“A station bell.”

Mara waited.

Sen sounded, for once, astonished.

“That boat isn’t a vehicle,” he said. “It’s an office.”

---

Tomas stared at the bell.

“Of course it is.”

Mara took the handpiece closer to her ear.

“What does that mean?”

“The old mobile stations carried an attendant, a route assembly, and a bell. The bell marks a jurisdictional boundary.”

“Better words,” Tomas said.

Sen drew breath.

“It tells other systems that someone responsible is present.”

“And they listen?”

“They’re supposed to.”

The small door shuddered again.

Eli rang the bell.

The wood went still.

Mara looked toward the ruined doorway.

Bera stood in sunlight now.

A faint shadow fell behind her.

Not toward the sun.

Away from it.

She noticed Mara looking.

“What?”

“Nothing,” Mara said, then corrected herself. “You’re casting a shadow.”

Bera looked down.

For a moment she seemed to be waiting for instructions.

Then she lifted one hand.

The shadow lifted its hand too.

Corren began to laugh.

It was an unsteady sound, close to crying.

Jory reached for his arm and held it.

Mara turned back to the handpiece.

“Sen. How do we tell the thing behind the door that Bera is leaving?”

There was a pause full of distant voices.

Then Sen said, very carefully, “I am going to read you the wording. Do not add to it.”

---

The wording was disappointing.

Mara had expected something commanding.

Instead she was required to state her name, the local station’s provisional designation, the name of the person requesting release, and the fact that the original agreement was disputed.

She stumbled over *retrospective custodial assumption*.

Tomas muttered that he would have liked to be present when someone decided that phrase would help.

Mara began again.

This time she finished.

The brass plate on the door changed.

**BERA VENN — REVIEW**

Bera’s knees gave way.

Eli caught her before she struck the ground.

For the first time, his hands met the weight of an ordinary person.

She gripped his sleeve.

“Don’t let go.”

“I won’t.”

Mara looked at Sen’s handpiece.

“Is she free?”

“Not yet.”

“Can we take her to town?”

“Yes.”

“Then we’re taking her.”

No one objected.

They wrapped her in the blanket she had folded so neatly.

Corren carried the little green shoes.

Jory walked beside him.

Leth took Bera’s arm on one side, Eli on the other.

Tomas gathered the bags.

Mara remained by the gate long enough to look back at the small door.

From behind it came the scrape of a chair.

Then a woman’s voice.

“Ms. Vale.”

Mara held the bell.

The voice was calm.

“You have made an administrative error.”

Mara rang it once and went after the others.

---

Bera slept at the inn.

The room had two windows.

Both opened.

She asked Leth to check them three times, then apologized.

Leth said she had once spent a winter in a room where the window had been painted shut, and had no objection to checking.

Downstairs, Mara sat at a table with Sen’s handpiece, the ledger, and a bowl of soup going cold.

Her father brought a spoon.

“You’ll need that.”

She took it.

Tomas sat opposite her.

“Well?”

“Well what?”

“Was it an error?”

Mara looked at the line she had read.

“I don’t know.”

The handpiece clicked.

Sen said, “It was valid.”

Tomas leaned back.

“Good.”

“It may still be contested.”

“Of course.”

Mara ate a spoonful of soup.

It had carrots in it.

For some reason, that nearly undid her.

She set the spoon down and pressed her palms against her eyes.

No one told her to be brave.

No one reminded her that Bera was upstairs.

Eli sat beside her.

Tomas watched the door.

After a while, she picked up the spoon again.

---

The assessment team arrived at dawn in a boat that looked completely ordinary.

This made Mara immediately suspicious.

There were two people aboard: a broad woman in a wool coat and a young man carrying so many cases that he could barely see over them.

The woman stepped onto the quay.

“Mara Vale?”

“Name first.”

The woman smiled slightly.

“Renn Aster. This is Colm.”

“Who sent you?”

“Sen.”

“What did he say about Tomas’s knot?”

Renn blinked.

Colm, behind his cases, said, “That the nautical name was more useful and considerably less dignified.”

Tomas nodded.

“That’s them.”

Renn produced a document.

Mara did not know whether the seals were real.

She asked Sen.

He confirmed them through the handpiece.

Only then did she unlock the chandlery.

Renn looked at the covered return assembly, the crate, the ledger, and the fresh sign visible through the window.

“How long have you been operating?”

“We aren’t operating.”

Colm looked at the list of names.

“Right.”

“We had an emergency.”

Renn removed her coat.

“Most stations begin that way.”

Mara did not find this comforting.

---

Renn examined Bera first.

She did not use the lamp.

She asked questions, took notes, and checked whether Bera could remember where she had slept.

Then she placed a small instrument against Bera’s wrist.

It clicked softly.

“Do you consent to a route examination?” Renn asked.

“What happens?”

“I look for the connection holding you to the house. It should not hurt. You may stop me at any time.”

“Will I go back?”

“Not during the examination.”

Bera nodded.

Renn waited.

“Yes,” Bera said.

The instrument glowed.

Mara watched from the doorway.

She had expected the arrival of experts to make her feel foolish about the ledger, the questions, the improvised caution.

Instead, Renn kept asking to see her notes.

At the end of the examination, she sat back.

“The review notice was properly entered.”

“Can you remove the connection?”

“I think so. I want to inspect the originating door first.”

Bera looked at Mara.

“Do I have to come?”

“No,” Renn said.

The answer was immediate.

Bera began to cry again, quietly.

Colm, who had been checking the window latches at her request, found a clean handkerchief and set it beside her.

---

At the ruined house, the small door was gone.

The rope remained.

It lay in a loose circle on the ground, still knotted around a handle that had detached itself cleanly from the wood.

The glass tile was black.

Tomas stared at the wall.

“That wasn’t us.”

“I believe you,” Renn said.

She crouched beside the tile.

Colm unpacked three instruments and arranged them in a line.

Corren stayed at the gate.

Mara held the station bell.

From somewhere in the house came the sound of sweeping.

Renn looked up.

“Is there another person here?”

“Bera said she kept the house.”

“Yes.”

“Perhaps the house found someone else.”

No one spoke for a moment.

Then a man appeared in the ruined doorway.

He was thin, young, and wearing a coat several sizes too large.

In his hands was the broom.

He looked at the group outside the gate.

“Have you come to fix the roof?”

Corren sat down on the wall.

Mara opened the ledger.

The young man watched her pencil.

“What is your name?” she asked.

He frowned.

“I keep the house.”

Behind Mara, Renn said something under her breath.

It was not administrative language.

---

They spent the next hour establishing that the young man could not remember his name.

He remembered a red scarf.

He remembered a train, though no railway had ever come within a hundred miles of the harbor.

He remembered someone promising to take him home.

Renn placed two markers at the gate and one beside the doorway.

Colm tested the wall where the small door had stood.

“It has moved,” he said.

“Where?” Mara asked.

“Not far.”

“Can you find it?”

“Yes.”

He did not sound pleased.

The man with the broom looked at them anxiously.

“Am I in trouble?”

“No,” Mara said.

Renn glanced at her, then nodded.

“No.”

Corren stood.

“I know where the room is.”

Mara turned.

“You said you didn’t remember.”

“I didn’t.”

He looked at the young man.

“Now I do.”

Renn began to ask a question, but he raised his hand.

“I’m not going in. I’m telling you.”

“Good,” she said.

Corren stepped along the outside wall and stopped by the old hearth.

“The door wasn’t built into the house,” he said. “The house was built around it.”

He pointed at the fireplace.

“Under there.”

---

Colm found stone steps beneath the hearth.

There should not have been room for them.

The house stood on solid rock; Corren said he had cut the foundations himself.

Renn sent a little wheeled device down first.

Mara watched its lamp shrink into the dark.

On the screen of a box in Colm’s hands, the staircase appeared in shades of gray.

It descended between smooth walls.

At the bottom was a door.

No nameplate.

No handle.

Only a letter slot.

The device stopped.

Colm adjusted a dial.

A sheet of paper slid through the slot and landed on the floor.

Renn leaned closer.

The device’s camera lowered.

The paper read:

**ONE RELEASE AUTHORIZED.  
ONE REPLACEMENT REQUIRED.**

Mara looked toward the young man in the doorway.

His broom scraped softly across the stone.

“No,” she said.

Renn straightened.

“No,” she agreed.

For the first time since they had met, Mara felt they were speaking exactly the same language.

---

Renn did not kick down the door.

She did not send anyone down the stairs.

She asked Colm for a different case.

Inside were six heavy metal wedges, a roll of red cord, and a stack of forms sealed in a waterproof sleeve.

Tomas looked at the forms.

“Will those help?”

“Possibly more than the wedges.”

“I hate that.”

“So do I.”

They marked the hearth.

They marked the gate.

They marked the young man’s broom, with his permission, after explaining that the marker might help them understand what held him there.

Mara sat on the wall beside him while the others worked.

“Can you remember the scarf?”

“Red.”

“Wool?”

“I think so.”

“Yours?”

His hand moved to his neck.

“No.”

“Someone you knew?”

He nodded.

A tear ran down his cheek without changing his expression.

“I was following her.”

“Onto the train?”

“I don’t know.”

Mara waited.

The broom had stopped moving.

After a while he said, “She called me Kit.”

Mara opened the ledger.

“May I write that down?”

He nodded.

This time, when the pencil touched the paper, he did not flinch.

---

At midday, the woman from Mara’s dream appeared on the staircase screen.

She stood behind the door, visible now through a pane of glass that had not been there before.

Her dark coat was buttoned to the throat.

She held a ledger.

Renn spoke into Colm’s box.

“This is a contested custodial installation under emergency review.”

The woman looked toward the camera.

“You have removed an attendant.”

“We have removed a person whose consent was not established.”

“Consent was given.”

“Produce the record.”

The woman opened her ledger.

Pages turned too quickly to count.

Mara stood behind Renn.

The clerk’s eyes lifted.

“Ms. Vale.”

Mara did not answer.

“You wished to know what had become of your mother.”

Eli, beside the hearth, went rigid.

Renn did not look away from the screen.

“Produce the record.”

“I can show you.”

“Produce the record of Bera Venn’s informed consent to indefinite service.”

The pages stopped.

The woman’s smile faded.

“There was an agreement.”

“Then show it.”

On the screen, the room behind the clerk darkened.

Mara heard a distant bell.

Not hers.

Not the harbor’s.

Something much larger.

---

The record was six words long.

**I HAVE LOST MY WAY HOME.**

Below it, in a different hand:

**RETURN GUIDANCE ACCEPTED. SERVICE IN EXCHANGE.**

No signature.

No terms.

No end date.

Renn read it.

Then she asked Colm to make three copies.

The clerk watched without speaking.

“Do you dispute the record?” she asked.

“Yes,” Renn said.

“On what grounds?”

“Misrepresentation. Absence of informed consent. Indefinite term. No demonstrated route assistance. Coercive retention.”

The woman’s gaze moved to Mara.

“Your family wrote the original schedule.”

The sentence struck harder than Mara expected.

Renn kept her voice level.

“Not relevant to Bera Venn’s claim.”

“It is relevant to the station’s authority.”

Mara looked at the name on the crate in her memory.

Vale and Daughters.

A family business not yet founded, perhaps. Or founded elsewhere. She did not know.

The clerk smiled again.

“Would you like to see your signature?”

Mara gripped the bell.

“No,” she said. “I’d like you to show us everyone you’re holding.”

The clerk closed the ledger.

Behind her, something began knocking from the inside of a cabinet.

---

The connection failed.

Not in a flash.

The picture dimmed, broke into bands, and went gray.

Colm adjusted two controls.

Then he shook his head.

“The office has withdrawn.”

Tomas stared at the staircase.

“Can it do that?”

“It just did.”

“What about Kit?”

Renn turned toward the doorway.

The young man stood motionless, his hands empty.

The broom lay at his feet.

“Kit?” Mara said.

He looked at her.

Then at the gate.

He took one step.

Nothing stopped him.

He took another.

When he reached the road, he began to shake.

Mara put down the bell and went to him.

Behind her, Colm’s instrument started clicking rapidly.

Renn crouched beside the hearth.

“What is it?” Eli asked.

She looked up.

“The office is shedding its attendants.”

“All of them?”

“I don’t know.”

From inside the ruined house came footsteps.

Then a woman emerged carrying an armful of folded towels.

Behind her came a boy with a bucket.

Then an old man in a uniform so faded that Mara could not make out its color.

Each stopped in the doorway, confused by the sun.

Mara turned to Tomas.

He had already picked up the lunch bag.

“How many cups?” she asked.

“Not enough.”

---

They made space in the orchard.

Corren’s apple trees had survived the fire, though thirty-seven years without pruning had left them tangled and tall.

People sat beneath them.

Some could name themselves.

Some could not.

A woman spoke a language no one recognized. Leth sat beside her and offered water, then waited while she drank.

A man asked whether it was still winter.

A child asked for a person called Renna.

Jory remembered another piece of Bell Lane and fell silent for several minutes.

Renn worked without stopping.

Colm sent messages through his box.

Eli went back to town for help and returned with a cart, blankets, bread, and the baker, who had decided to close for the afternoon.

Tomas looked at the growing crowd.

“People do manage,” Mara said softly.

He glanced at her.

“Don’t quote me at myself.”

She smiled, briefly.

By dusk, twenty-six people had come out of the house.

Then the doorway showed only the ash tree again.

The staircase beneath the hearth had become a shallow cellar full of rainwater.

The small handle in the rope turned to rust.

Renn sat on the wall.

For the first time all day, she looked uncertain.

Mara opened the ledger.

“Twenty-six,” she said.

Renn nodded.

“How many were there?”

“We don’t know.”

Mara turned to a new page.

At the top, she wrote:

**Still missing.**

---

That evening, the harbor filled with lights.

The inn took nine people.

The baker’s sister took three.

Tomas complained loudly about the stairs to his spare room and then carried bedding up them himself.

The customs officers—only one of them now, though it still seemed necessary to specify—opened the old inspection hall.

Leth played while people ate.

Kit remembered his surname halfway through a bowl of stew and said it so suddenly that everyone at his table went quiet.

“Arlen.”

Mara wrote it down.

Then he remembered that the woman with the red scarf was his aunt.

Then he cried.

Nobody asked him for more.

Bera came downstairs and sat beside the woman with the towels.

They looked at one another for a long time.

“I know you,” Bera said.

The woman nodded.

“Laundry.”

“Third floor.”

“Before they moved the stairs.”

Their conversation continued in fragments too small for Mara to understand.

She did not interrupt.

Across the room, Renn spoke quietly with Sen through the handpiece.

Mara caught only one sentence.

“No, not an isolated installation.”

She closed the ledger.

Outside, the yellow boat had vanished.

The green boat remained.

Its brass bell was back on the bow.

Mara could not remember carrying it there.

---

Renn left two days later.

Colm stayed.

He said he had been assigned to assist with route verification.

Tomas said he looked too young to be assigned anything heavier than laundry.

Colm showed him the calluses on his hands.

They began getting along after that.

The return assembly remained under its hood.

A second, safer instrument arrived in a locked case with instructions Mara could actually understand.

Before Renn boarded her boat, Mara asked about the clerk’s accusation.

“Could I have written that schedule?”

Renn took a moment.

“A version of you could have.”

“That doesn’t help.”

“No.”

“Could this version?”

Renn looked toward the chandlery.

The sign.

The ledger visible on the table.

The people waiting outside, some anxious, some merely tired.

“That depends on what you do.”

Mara almost objected to the simplicity of it.

Then she thought of the older Mara, certain enough to close a crossing and wrong enough to spend seventeen years trying to reopen it.

“Will you send me the old schedules?”

“Yes.”

“All of them you can find.”

“Yes.”

“And the names.”

Renn nodded.

“The names first.”

---

On the first quiet morning, Mara repaired a shelf.

It took longer than expected.

The wood had warped, the nails were poor, and she was not good at holding the board level while reaching for the hammer.

Her father offered to help.

She accepted.

He held the board.

She drove the nails.

For a while, that was all.

Then he said, “I’m going to the reef tomorrow.”

Mara stopped.

“In Tomas’s boat,” he added. “With Tomas. To fish.”

She looked at him.

“I should have led with that.”

“Yes.”

He smiled faintly.

She did not.

The smile faded.

“What time will you return?”

“Before four.”

“If you’re late?”

“We’ll send word if we can.”

She set another nail.

He held the shelf steady.

After a moment, she said, “Bring something for supper.”

“I’ll try.”

“No promises about fish, then?”

“Only about trying.”

That was small enough to believe.

She struck the nail cleanly.

---

The first letter from the records office arrived a week later.

It contained twelve names.

No dramatic warning.

No plea hidden in a violin.

Just twelve names, each followed by a place and an estimated date of disappearance.

Mara read them at the table while Colm checked the new instrument.

Leth sat by the window, teaching Kit to tighten the bow without splitting it.

Bera was upstairs, where she had begun keeping the station’s accounts.

She had asked for paid work.

Mara had said there was almost no money.

Bera had looked at the books and said that was apparent.

They were working on it.

At noon, Tomas brought soup.

At one, Jory remembered a grocer in Bell Lane and drew the shopfront.

At two, the green boat bumped gently against the steps.

No one was aboard.

On its forward seat lay a parcel wrapped in plain cloth.

Mara looked at Colm.

He looked at the parcel.

“Gloves?” he suggested.

“Gloves.”

They carried it inside.

Under the cloth was a book.

Its cover read:

**VALE & DAUGHTERS  
FIELD MANUAL, FIRST EDITION**

Mara did not touch it.

Bera came down the stairs.

“What is it?”

“I don’t know.”

The book opened by itself.

On the first page, in Mara’s handwriting, was a sentence.

*If this has reached you before the contract, do not sign.*

---

They left the book open on the table.

No one turned the page.

Mara contacted Sen.

He asked her to read the sentence aloud twice.

Then he said, “Has anyone offered you a contract?”

“No.”

“Station accreditation?”

“Renn mentioned it.”

“Funding?”

“Not yet.”

“Manufacturing rights?”

Mara stared at the cover.

“No.”

Sen was silent.

Bera folded her arms.

“Well,” she said, “we know what we’re doing this afternoon.”

Tomas had just arrived at the door.

“Not signing?”

“Reading.”

He sighed.

“Worse.”

Colm brought another lamp.

Leth closed her violin case.

Mara sat.

Before touching the first page, she wrote the date in her notebook.

Then the people present.

Then where the parcel had been found.

It felt slow.

It felt almost absurd.

The book before her might contain decades of her own work, a warning about the institution that had helped them, or a trap designed by the clerk who knew her name.

She wrote down what she knew.

Then she put on the gloves and turned the page.

---

The second page was blank.

So was the third.

The fourth contained a diagram of the return assembly.

Several components were crossed out in red.

Beside the reservoir, someone had written:

*Never let them call this fuel.*

Mara looked at Colm.

He leaned closer.

“That’s correct.”

“Why would anyone call it fuel?”

“Because then they can charge by consumption.”

Bera sat down.

“Ah.”

The next page held a table of fees.

Not for equipment.

For arrivals.

For departures.

For route retention.

For suspended passage.

For a category called *custodial continuity*.

Mara felt cold.

Bera tapped the final line.

“That’s us.”

Sen said quietly, “Send me a copy.”

Mara turned another page.

This one bore a heading:

**PROPOSED EXCLUSIVE LICENSE**

Below it, in her own handwriting:

*They will say the network cannot run without this. Ask which network. Ask who pays when no money changes hands.*

Tomas pulled up a chair.

Outside, the tide rose against the harbor steps.

Inside, Bera found a clean sheet of paper and began checking the sums.The sums did not add up.

This, Bera said, was the first encouraging thing that had happened all week.

Mara leaned across the table.

“Encouraging?”

“Someone expects no one to check.”

Bera had arranged three sheets beside the manual. One held the printed fees. One held the arithmetic. The third was covered with questions.

She pointed to a column headed **CONTINUITY RESERVE**.

“Every arrival pays into this.”

“Pays what?”

“It doesn’t say.”

Colm brought his chair closer.

“That should be a reserve of calibration medium.”

“Should be,” Bera repeated.

“Yes.”

“And how much does an arrival use?”

“Depends on the route.”

“Does it ever use a negative amount?”

“No.”

“Then why do these six routes show negative consumption?”

Colm took the pencil.

For several minutes, no one spoke except Sen, who asked for the figures one at a time.

When Colm finished, he looked at the book with an expression Mara had not seen on him before.

“What?” she asked.

“These aren’t fees charged to travelers.”

Bera nodded.

“They’re payments to the station.”

“For what?”

“For agreeing,” she said, “that some travelers haven’t arrived.”

---

There was a margin note beside the longest column.

It had been pressed into the paper so hard that the nib had almost torn through.

*Count people at both ends.*

Mara traced the words without touching them.

“Sen?”

“I’m here.”

“Who runs the records office?”

“A board.”

“Who appoints the board?”

“Member stations.”

“Who counts the member stations?”

The handpiece clicked.

Sen did not reply.

Bera picked up another sheet.

“We’ll need the charter.”

Tomas rubbed his face.

“I preferred the part with the haunted boat.”

“The haunted boat had accounts,” Bera said.

“Yes, and I preferred not knowing that.”

Mara looked at the open doorway.

Three people waited on the bench outside. A woman whose destination they had found but could not yet reach. A man who did not want to return until someone had checked that his brother was still alive. Kit, who had come to help carry water and had fallen asleep in the sun.

These were not abstractions.

They were not a reserve.

She pressed the handpiece again.

“Sen. Please don’t make inquiries yet.”

“I already have.”

“To whom?”

“My supervisor.”

“What did you ask?”

“What you asked me.”

Bera closed her eyes.

On the table, the field manual turned its own page.

A single line appeared.

*By now, he has probably told them.*

---

They disconnected the handpiece.

Not because Sen had betrayed them.

Because they did not know who else might be listening.

Colm showed Mara how to remove the little contact plate without damaging it.

The resulting silence was uncomfortable.

“I don’t like this,” she said.

“Neither do I.”

“Can we contact Renn another way?”

“Yes.”

“Privately?”

Colm looked at the handpiece.

“More privately.”

“That will have to do.”

He took a pencil and wrote an address on an ordinary piece of paper.

It was a street name, a house number, and a city.

Mara waited for the extraordinary part.

There was none.

“You can send a letter?”

“Not through the post here.”

“How?”

“There’s a courier station two days south.”

“Two days?”

“By road.”

Tomas brightened.

“A road. Finally.”

Colm folded the paper.

“We may not have two days.”

The harbor bell rang.

One stroke.

Everyone at the table froze.

Then the customs officer leaned through the doorway.

“Meeting at the hall in an hour,” he said. “Someone’s sent us a charter.”

---

The charter was on thick cream paper.

It had arrived in a leather case with a brass lock, carried by a woman who had come ashore from a merchant vessel and showed no sign of being made of shadow, water, or administrative displeasure.

Her name was Dalia Fen.

She wore travel-stained boots.

Her shadow behaved properly.

Mara disliked how relieved she felt about both things.

At the customs hall, Dalia laid the charter on a table.

“The provisional harbor station has attracted substantial attention,” she said.

“Apparently,” Mara replied.

Dalia glanced at her notes.

“You’re Ms. Vale.”

“Yes.”

“I understand you’ve had a difficult beginning.”

Tomas made a sound from the back of the room.

Dalia did not react.

“We can provide training, replacement equipment, emergency personnel, accommodation grants, and a stable supply of calibration medium.”

Bera sat beside Mara with a blank notebook.

“And in exchange?” she asked.

“Membership in the network.”

“Terms?”

“In the charter.”

“We’ll read it.”

“Of course.”

Dalia pushed the document toward them.

The customs officer cleared his throat.

“As this concerns the harbor, the council will need to be consulted.”

“Certainly.”

“And property rights.”

“Naturally.”

“And tariffs.”

Dalia’s expression changed very slightly.

Mara noticed.

Bera noticed too.

---

The charter was forty-eight pages long.

Mara read the first.

Bera began at the back.

Tomas asked whether there was a version for people who had other things to do.

Dalia said there was an executive summary.

“No,” Bera said.

She did not raise her voice.

Dalia set the summary aside.

Colm stood by the window, holding the courier letter in his pocket.

Halfway through the third page, Mara found the word *exclusive*.

She underlined it.

Dalia watched her pencil.

“Exclusive access ensures reliable routing.”

“Exclusive to whom?”

“Accredited member stations.”

“Can a nonmember request help?”

“Of course.”

“On the same terms?”

“There are differences in service priority.”

Mara looked up.

“What happens to someone already in transit if a station leaves the network?”

“Their route remains subject to the existing agreement.”

“For how long?”

“Until resolved.”

Bera stopped writing.

“And who may resolve it?”

Dalia did not look at her notes this time.

“The originating office.”

Mara thought of a little pale door.

Of a brass plate changing from *pending* to *review*.

“We’re going to need longer than an hour,” she said.

---

Dalia did not object.

This made the room more uneasy than an objection would have.

She offered to return the next morning.

She left the leather case.

She asked for a receipt.

Bera wrote one that described exactly what had been received and promised nothing else.

Dalia read it.

For the first time, she smiled with something like genuine appreciation.

“You’ve done this before.”

“Accounts,” Bera said.

“Then you know there are costs.”

“Yes.”

“Someone has to meet them.”

“Yes.”

Dalia waited.

Bera handed her the receipt.

“That’s why I’d like to know what they are.”

After she left, the customs officer sat down heavily.

“Well?”

“No,” Tomas said.

“You haven’t read it.”

“I heard enough.”

Mara kept reading.

At the bottom of page seventeen, beneath a paragraph about emergency route stabilization, she found a reference number.

She compared it with the field manual’s fee table.

It matched.

Bera leaned over.

“Which clause?”

Mara showed her.

Bera’s mouth tightened.

**A station may elect to retain an unresolved arrival in continuity status pending sufficient local capacity.**

“How long?” Mara asked.

Bera turned to the definitions.

There was no maximum.

---

They worked through the evening.

The customs officer fetched the town’s old property deeds because he said one set of obscure documents might help illuminate another.

It did not.

But he stayed.

The baker brought a lamp.

Jory fell asleep in a chair and woke occasionally to offer a devastatingly sensible question.

“Why don’t they ask the traveler?”

“Why is that word capitalized?”

“If the money is for beds, where are the beds?”

At midnight, they found a schedule of payment.

Stations received support according to the number of arrivals they could document as *continuing* rather than *complete*.

Completed arrivals ended the payment.

Bera sat back.

“There.”

Mara stared at the page.

“They’re paid to delay.”

Dalia, had she been present, might have called that unfair.

The agreement did not require anyone to delay.

It did not ask them to hide a traveler behind a door.

It simply paid more when a person remained unresolved.

Bera turned the paper toward the customs officer.

“If you paid fishermen for keeping fish in their nets, what would they bring to market?”

He looked offended.

“That’s not the same.”

“No,” she said. “Fish have the advantage of not being told it is for their own good.”

---

Mara went home at one.

Her father was asleep in a chair by the stove.

There was food under a cloth.

A note beside it read:

*Back before four.*

Under that, in smaller writing:

*Three fish. One very small. Tomas blames the moon.*

She stood looking at the note for a long time.

Then she ate.

When Eli woke, she was still at the table with the charter.

“What is it?”

“A way to make the station possible.”

“That sounds good.”

“It might also be a way to make Bera impossible to release.”

He came to sit opposite her.

She showed him the clause.

He read slowly.

“Can you cross it out?”

“Perhaps.”

“Then start there.”

“They may not accept it.”

“No.”

“We need the equipment.”

“Yes.”

She rubbed her eyes.

He waited.

At last she said, “I don’t know enough.”

“About contracts?”

“About any of it.”

Eli looked at the shelf they had repaired.

Neither nail was quite straight.

“Who does?”

Mara almost said *they do*.

Then she thought of the missing people.

The discrepancies.

The phrase *until resolved*.

She drew a line through the clause.

---

In the morning, they presented amendments.

Forty-three.

Dalia read the first ten without speaking.

At the eleventh, she asked for tea.

At the eighteenth, she removed her coat.

At the twenty-sixth, she looked up.

“You want travelers to retain the right to refuse a destination after route expenditure has begun.”

“Yes,” Mara said.

“That could be costly.”

“Then explain the cost before you begin.”

“And if circumstances change?”

“Explain the change.”

“And if they still refuse?”

Bera tapped the amendment.

Dalia read on.

At the thirty-second, she stopped again.

“No exclusive license.”

“No.”

“No assignment of future inventions.”

“No.”

“No retention of identity-bearing calibration data without separate consent.”

Colm nodded.

Dalia looked at him.

“That provision is standard.”

“It’s also unnecessary for routing,” he said.

“Some systems use it.”

“Then they should stop.”

The customs officer, who had slept poorly and was beginning to enjoy himself, added, “We also require a public annual account.”

Dalia leaned back.

“You are asking for a different institution.”

Mara looked at the crossed-out clauses.

“Perhaps.”

---

Dalia did not threaten them.

She explained what would happen if they refused.

No guaranteed calibration supply.

No priority emergency response.

No indemnity against claims from other stations.

No access to restricted route records.

No funding.

She said these things plainly.

Mara appreciated that, though it did not make them less frightening.

“What about the people here now?” she asked.

“The network will accept their cases.”

“Under the existing terms.”

“Yes.”

“Bera too?”

“If she chooses.”

“Can she choose without signing?”

Dalia’s gaze shifted to Bera.

“No.”

Bera folded her hands.

“I have signed enough things by saying I was lost.”

Dalia looked down at the charter.

For a moment, Mara thought she might apologize.

Instead she said, “I can request a review of your amendments.”

“How long?”

“I don’t know.”

“Will you leave the equipment?”

“The equipment is not mine to leave.”

“The replacement housing was sent before this offer.”

“That remains yours.”

“And the new instrument?”

“Loaned.”

Colm put a hand on its case.

“Assigned to me.”

Dalia looked at him.

“You should speak to your supervisor.”

“I intend to.”

---

After Dalia left, Sen returned.

Not through the handpiece.

He arrived in person in a narrow gray boat with a cracked windshield.

He was younger than Mara had imagined and considerably taller. His hair stood in several directions. He carried a satchel, a coat, and a sandwich wrapped in paper.

At the top of the harbor steps, he stopped.

“Mara?”

She recognized the voice before she accepted the face.

“Sen.”

“I thought it best not to use the line.”

“What happened?”

“I’ve been placed on leave.”

“Because of us?”

“Because I accessed records outside my assigned function.”

“Because of us.”

“Partly because I kept doing it after being told to stop.”

Tomas took the satchel from him.

“Have you eaten?”

Sen held up the sandwich.

Tomas examined it.

“Have you eaten anything fit to eat?”

The attendant smiled, tiredly.

Inside the chandlery, Bera cleared a chair.

Sen sat as though he had only just discovered he was allowed to.

Then he took a packet from his coat.

“The charter,” he said, “was revised twenty-eight years ago.”

Mara opened the packet.

The original document was only nine pages long.

---

The old charter had no continuity payments.

It had no exclusive license.

It did require stations to contribute equipment, labor, or money toward shared rescue work.

It also required two counts for every passage.

One at departure.

One at arrival.

Mara found the sentence and felt the room settle around it.

“Why did they stop?”

Sen unwrapped his sandwich, looked at it, and wrapped it again.

“The counts disagreed.”

“They were meant to reveal that.”

“Yes.”

“So?”

“There were too many unresolved cases. Stations were arguing over who had failed. Some withdrew. Others couldn’t afford the required searches.”

Bera read the amendment history.

“Then they changed the definition of arrival.”

Sen nodded.

“What did that solve?”

“The reporting problem.”

Tomas stood very still by the door.

“And the people?”

Sen looked at the floor.

For a while, the only sound was Leth playing softly upstairs for someone who could not sleep.

Mara turned the page.

At the bottom of the old charter was a list of founding stations.

The sixth was called Low Water.

The seventh bore a name she had never seen before.

**VALE MOBILE OFFICE — TOMORROW**

---

They went down to the green boat.

All of them.

This made it sit so low in the water that Tomas ordered half of them back onto the steps.

The boat had three benches, a little storage locker, and a brass bell.

Mara knew this.

She had sat in it, descended in it, and carried a return assembly aboard it with five people who had no reason to fit.

Now she looked more carefully.

Under the stern seat was a small metal plate.

Not hidden.

Simply placed where no one had yet thought to read.

**STATION 7  
MOBILE INQUIRY AND RETURN OFFICE  
LOCAL AUTHORITY RETAINED**

Below that was a keyhole.

Mara held out her hand.

Sen searched his satchel.

“No.”

Colm shook his head.

Tomas said, “I have a hammer.”

“No,” three people replied.

Bera leaned over the rail.

“What key do you already have?”

Mara thought of the chandlery.

The kitchen.

The little drawer.

She took her key ring from her pocket.

The smallest key had come with the building when Tomas first let them in.

It fit.

She turned it.

The stern seat opened onto a staircase.

Tomas closed his eyes.

“I am going to need a different understanding of boats.”

---

The stairs were narrow and dry.

Mara stood at the top with the bell in one hand.

“No one goes alone,” she said.

“No one has suggested it,” Bera replied.

They took the route line.

Sen tested the first step.

Colm marked the entrance.

Tomas remained above with Jory and a firm instruction not to let anyone casually wander aboard.

Then Mara, Bera, Sen, and Colm descended.

There were eleven steps.

Mara did not count them.

Sen did, then looked embarrassed when she glanced back.

At the bottom was an office.

An ordinary office, if one ignored its location beneath a bench in a boat barely long enough to hold it.

There were two desks.

A stove.

A wall of filing drawers.

Three coats on pegs.

A kettle.

On the far wall hung a framed notice.

**THE PERSON AT THE COUNTER IS NOT THE CASE.**

Bera read it.

Then she inspected the desk.

There was dust on everything.

“How long?”

Sen touched the kettle.

“Years.”

Mara went to the filing drawers.

Each bore a letter.

The drawer marked V was locked.

Above it, someone had pinned a photograph.

Mara was in it.

Not the older woman who had brought bread.

Another Mara.

Younger than that one, older than this.

Beside her stood a child with a severe expression and a missing front tooth.

On the back, in blue ink:

*First inspection. She failed us on biscuits.*

---

Mara set the photograph down.

She did not ask anyone what it meant.

There would be time to be frightened by the child later.

Bera opened the top desk drawer.

Inside lay a pencil, a packet of tea, and a ledger.

The ledger was not magical.

Its pages did not turn by themselves.

Most were blank.

On the first page was a list of operating expenses.

Bread.

Lamp oil.

Copper.

Wages.

Roof repairs, with an angry line beneath the total.

On the second was a note.

*If you are reading this because the key finally worked, check the stove pipe before lighting a fire. Also, I am sorry about the state of the filing.*

Colm looked upward.

“Where does the pipe go?”

“An excellent question,” Sen said.

Mara turned to the next page.

This note was longer.

*You are not obliged to make my choices.*

*The office will try to recognize you. That does not mean you owe it service. It means I was careless about succession.*

*If you want no part in this, ring the bell three times from the shore and tell it to find another keeper.*

*If you stay, read the charter before the histories. The histories are full of people explaining why they had no alternative.*

Bera read over her shoulder.

“Someone learned.”

Mara looked at the photograph again.

“Eventually.”

---

There was a window behind the second desk.

Outside it, rain fell on a street of tall buildings.

Mara could not see the harbor.

Sen drew the curtain without touching the latch.

“Don’t open that until we’ve checked the address.”

“Agreed.”

A notice beside the window read:

**CURRENT VIEW DOES NOT INDICATE CURRENT LOCATION.**

Tomas would hate that, Mara thought.

Colm found the station’s technical log.

It was full of neat diagrams interrupted by increasingly impatient handwriting.

The last entry was dated in a calendar none of them recognized.

**External routing suspended. Office held in local berth pending correction of charter dispute. Bell active. Manual access restricted to consensual succession.**

Below that:

*Yes, consensual. I have learned something.*

Sen sat at the second desk.

“The office didn’t appear because you founded it.”

Mara looked at him.

“It was waiting for you to decide whether you would.”

Bera closed the ledger.

“Well, that was presumptuous of it.”

A floorboard creaked.

All four looked down.

“Don’t start,” Bera told the room.

The board did not creak again.

---

They found money.

Not a fortune.

Enough that Bera stopped speaking for nearly a minute.

It was in a tin labeled **OPERATING RESERVE — NOT FOR CATASTROPHIC IDEAS**.

Inside were coins from three countries, several promissory notes, and a small stack of local bank certificates.

The certificates were current.

Mara sat on the edge of the desk.

“How?”

Sen examined the dates.

“Someone planned.”

“Someone had access to time.”

“Those are not the same skill.”

Bera began counting.

The reserve would pay for food, rent, and modest wages for six months.

Perhaps eight, if they did not buy anything foolish.

“What counts as foolish?” Mara asked.

“We’ll discuss it before you buy it.”

“There’s an emergency repair fund,” Colm said from the technical log.

Bera looked up.

“How much?”

He showed her.

She shut her eyes.

“Four months.”

“Because of repairs?”

“Because none of you are allowed to work for nothing while pretending it doesn’t count.”

Mara had no argument ready for that.

Above them, Tomas called down the stairs.

“There’s someone here.”

“Who?”

“He says he’s come about the contract.”

Mara rose.

Then she looked at the notice above the desks.

The person at the counter was not the case.

“All right,” she said. “Ask his name.”

---

The visitor’s name was Olan Merrow.

He was not from the network.

He represented a company that made lenses.

Mara met him in the chandlery because she was not ready to invite anyone else into the boat.

Olan placed a sample case on the table.

Inside lay six discs of beautifully polished glass.

“We’ve heard about your work.”

“From whom?”

“Trade correspondence.”

“Whose?”

He smiled.

“People notice innovation.”

Bera sat beside Mara.

“People sign names to correspondence.”

The smile weakened.

Olan closed the case.

“I can provide you with materials. Manufacturing capacity. Distribution.”

“In exchange?”

“A minority share in any instruments developed using our facilities.”

“How much of a minority?”

“Forty-nine percent.”

Tomas, from the doorway, laughed loudly enough to interrupt.

Olan ignored him.

“The initial offer is negotiable.”

Mara thought of the field manual.

“Does your agreement include exclusive manufacturing rights?”

“For a limited term.”

“Assignment of future designs?”

“Only within the relevant class.”

“Who defines the class?”

Olan looked at Bera.

Bera did not help him.

Mara folded her hands.

“We aren’t signing anything today.”

---

After Olan left, Mara returned to the manual.

The next page had filled with writing.

*Good.*

She stared at it.

Then she took the pencil.

In the margin, she wrote:

*If you can read this, stop being cryptic.*

The words remained.

No answer appeared.

She waited.

Tomas put a cup beside her.

“Anything?”

“No.”

He sat.

A minute later, beneath her line, new handwriting darkened.

*I cannot read it yet.*

Mara stood so abruptly that the chair scraped.

The writing continued.

*But I remember wanting to write that. If you have written something else, this is going to look foolish.*

Tomas leaned over.

“Well?”

“She knows herself.”

“That can be useful.”

A final sentence appeared.

*The important pages are not hidden. They are unfinished.*

Mara turned back through the book.

The warnings.

The fee tables.

The diagrams.

Then blank paper.

Dozens of blank pages.

Waiting, apparently, for something she had not yet done.

She closed the cover.

“We need ordinary notebooks,” she said.

Tomas looked at the one already in her hand.

“More ordinary notebooks.”

---

Six months was not security.

It was time.

Bera insisted they spend the first part of it deciding what work they could actually do.

Not every missing person.

Not every broken route.

Not every promise made by a clerk with a ledger.

They began with the people already in town.

Each chose a contact person.

Each received an explanation of what was known, what remained uncertain, and what a search might require.

Some wanted to leave immediately.

Some wanted to stay.

One man refused to answer any further questions until someone found him boots that fit.

Tomas approved of him.

Mara and Colm spent three days verifying a route to a city built around seven canals.

When they finally opened the passage, the woman waiting to cross looked through it and said no.

Mara felt the effort in her shoulders.

The wasted calibration.

The three days.

Then she remembered the charter.

“All right,” she said.

The woman began to apologize.

“You don’t have to.”

“But you worked—”

“Yes.”

“I thought I’d be ready.”

“We can close it.”

The woman nodded.

They closed it.

Bera entered the expense under **SERVICE COMPLETED**.

Mara noticed.

Bera looked up.

“She received what she needed to decide.”

---

The next search took them to the river.

Not the bridge from the drawing.

An actual river, cold enough to hurt Mara’s feet through her boots when she slipped off a stepping stone.

The person they were seeking was Kit’s aunt.

Her name was Renna Arlen.

She had worn a red scarf because it had belonged to Kit’s father, and because she said railway stations were full of people in gray coats who expected children to find them by instinct.

Kit remembered that much.

The new instrument found three possible routes.

One led to a station in ruins.

One led to a platform crowded with soldiers.

The third led to a riverbank with no railway in sight.

Kit chose to inspect the third, from a safe distance, with Colm.

Mara came because he asked.

They set a fixed point beside a willow.

The air smelled of wet leaves.

Somewhere nearby, metal struck metal.

A hammer.

They followed the sound to a low building beside the water.

A woman in a red scarf was repairing a hinge.

Kit stopped.

Mara did not move past him.

The woman looked up.

For several seconds, neither spoke.

Then she said, “Where have you been?”

Kit began to laugh and cry at once.

“I don’t know.”

She crossed the yard so fast she nearly fell.

---

They did not close the route until both had agreed.

Renna wanted explanations.

Kit wanted lunch.

They gave him lunch first.

The building by the river was a ferry house. Renna had lived there for six years.

The train had vanished before reaching the next station.

She had searched every stop on the line.

Then every town near it.

Then places that were not on the line at all.

“How did you get here?” Mara asked.

“A man at a ticket office told me there was another service.”

“What did he ask you?”

Renna looked at Kit.

“What I’d lost.”

Mara set down her cup.

“What did you say?”

“My nephew.”

“And?”

“He told me to wait.”

“For six years?”

“No.” Renna’s mouth hardened. “For three days. Then I broke his window.”

Tomas, who would hear the story later, liked her immediately.

After that, she had worked her way along rivers and roads, keeping the red scarf even when it grew too thin to warm her.

“Someone might know it,” she said.

Kit held the frayed end between his fingers.

“I did.”

Mara wrote down the location of the ticket office.

Not during the reunion.

Afterward, outside, while Colm checked the route.

On the next page of the ordinary notebook, she added another name to the list of places.

---

When Kit left, the chandlery became quieter.

His mug remained on the shelf because he said he might visit if that was possible.

Mara did not promise it would be.

She promised to keep the address.

He accepted that.

A week later, a letter arrived through the green boat’s office.

It was ordinary paper.

There were no warnings.

Kit wrote that his aunt snored, that the ferry leaked in one corner, and that he had found work helping a carpenter.

At the bottom, he had drawn a broom.

Then crossed it out.

Mara pinned the letter above the desk.

Not as proof that they were good at the work.

As proof that something had happened after a name left the ledger.

Bera stood beside her.

“We should ask whether he wants it displayed.”

Mara took the pin out.

“You’re right.”

They wrote back.

The answer came three days later.

*Yes, but not the part about snoring. She’ll deny it.*

They folded that line under.

---

By autumn, the station had three regular routes.

None led to Low Water.

That remained missing.

The bridge drawing changed occasionally.

Once, the river was full of reeds.

Once, there was snow on the parapet.

Once, a figure stood beside the desk, but the drawing was too small to show whether it was the clerk or someone else.

Mara kept it in a glass frame.

Leth refused to put it back in the violin.

“The instrument has enough to carry,” she said.

The yellow boat did not return.

Corren began repairing actual houses.

He worked slowly.

Sometimes a room’s dimensions frightened him, and he had to step outside until his breathing settled.

No one called him unreliable.

Tomas said anyone who objected could repair their own roof.

Bera found her sister’s city.

Not the route.

Only a record that it existed.

That was enough to keep her at the desk late into the evening, sorting scraps of testimony and old station reports.

Mara asked once whether she wanted someone else to take over.

Bera shook her head.

“I want to work on it.”

“Not because you have to?”

“No.”

Mara believed her.

Then she reminded her about supper.

---

The contract returned in winter.

Not the charter.

Olan Merrow’s agreement.

He sent it with better terms.

Twenty percent.

No assignment of unrelated inventions.

A defined manufacturing period.

A generous advance.

Mara laid it beside the field manual.

The book stayed blank.

Bera read the agreement.

“So?”

“So it’s better.”

“Safe?”

“Better.”

Mara looked at the advance.

It would fund the station for two years.

It would pay for a larger house for the arrivals.

It would buy properly made instruments instead of repaired remnants.

It would pay Leth enough to stop teaching in three places.

It would give Sen a wage that did not require him to pretend his savings were larger than they were.

“What’s the danger?”

Bera pointed.

“Control of supply.”

“We retain the designs.”

“They make the parts. Exclusively for seven years.”

“We could build our own.”

“Could we?”

Mara looked at the little workshop behind the chandlery.

Corren had installed a bench.

Colm had burned one section of it already.

No, she thought.

Not at scale.

Not quickly.

Outside, sleet struck the window.

Someone upstairs coughed.

Mara read the amount again.

---

She took the agreement to the boat.

The office window looked onto a field under summer sun.

She kept the curtain closed.

The drawer marked V remained locked.

They had tried every key.

They had not tried the hammer.

Mara sat at the desk and read the manufacturing terms aloud, hoping their sound would reveal something her eyes had missed.

It did not.

At the third page, the stove made a quiet metallic tick.

She looked up.

The room felt occupied.

Not by a visible person.

By attention.

She put down the contract.

“If you know something, say it.”

The office did not reply.

Mara took out the photograph of the woman and child.

The child’s expression seemed familiar.

Not because she had seen the face before.

Because the child looked as though she had just asked a question adults were trying to avoid.

“Did you sign?” Mara asked the woman in the photograph.

The stove ticked again.

She turned the photograph over.

The old inscription remained.

Beneath it, in pencil so faint she had not noticed it before, was another line.

*She says the biscuits are not the problem.*

Mara laughed despite herself.

Then she went back upstairs.

---

They invited Olan to the station.

Not to sign.

To visit.

He arrived in a good coat and spent the first ten minutes trying not to get it wet.

Mara introduced him to everyone.

Bera showed him the accounts.

Colm showed him the workshop.

Sen explained the maintenance burden.

Leth showed him the room where travelers waited while the instrument searched.

Olan asked intelligent questions.

He listened.

That complicated things.

At supper, he said, “You think I’m trying to trap you.”

Mara put down her spoon.

“I think your agreement could trap us.”

“That isn’t my intention.”

“I’m glad.”

He looked dissatisfied.

She understood why.

He wanted intention to settle the matter.

“I need a return on the investment,” he said.

“Yes.”

“I can’t give you a factory for nothing.”

“We haven’t asked you to.”

“You want nonexclusive rights.”

“Yes.”

“Then a competitor can use our development work without paying for it.”

Bera leaned forward.

“Can we pay for the development work separately?”

Olan opened his mouth.

Closed it.

“How?”

“With a fixed contract. Defined costs. Shared specifications. No control over who may return home.”

He stared at her.

“That last part is not in my agreement.”

“No,” Bera said. “It is in ours.”

---

Negotiation took six weeks.

It was dull in ways Mara had not known dullness could be.

There were arguments over tolerances, replacement parts, testing standards, delivery dates, and what exactly constituted a defect in glass meant to show places that were not nearby.

Olan’s engineers wrote that the term *nearby* needed definition.

Sen agreed.

Tomas suggested everyone involved go outside for an hour.

They did.

The final agreement did not give either side everything it wanted.

Olan received payment for tooling and a guaranteed initial order.

The station retained the designs.

Safety specifications would be public.

Replacement parts could be made elsewhere after independent testing.

No one could sell a route record as part of the equipment.

The advance was smaller.

Much smaller.

Bera recalculated the budget.

“Ten months,” she said.

Mara felt the disappointment.

Then the relief.

They signed.

Nothing happened.

No bell.

No new words in the manual.

No photograph changing on the wall.

Olan sanded the ink, folded his copy, and asked whether there was more soup.

There was.

---

The field manual filled its next page that night.

Mara found it in the morning.

There was no approval.

No warning.

Only a diagram of the lens housing they had agreed to manufacture.

At the bottom, a note:

*Second screw tends to strip. Use a wider head.*

Colm examined it.

“That’s useful.”

Mara sat down.

For a moment she felt absurdly cheated.

She had wanted the book to tell her whether they had done the right thing.

It had told her about a screw.

Bera looked over her shoulder.

“Good.”

“Why?”

“Because the screw can be checked.”

They checked it.

The screw stripped on the seventh test.

Colm changed the design.

Mara added the result to the margin.

The book did not answer.

She found she preferred it that way.

---

The first new instrument went to a station called Reedmarket.

It was a town built on long wooden walkways over shallow water.

Its operator, Nessa, had been repairing the same lamp for forty years.

She wrote that she distrusted equipment without obvious dents.

Colm offered to make one.

She said she would manage.

Two weeks later, Reedmarket sent a complete departure and arrival count.

Both numbers matched.

A month later, another station asked for the specifications.

Then another.

The network issued a notice warning that unlicensed systems might lack continuity support.

Sen read it twice.

Then he wrote a response asking what support had been provided to Bera Venn.

He included dates.

Bera checked them.

The network did not answer directly.

It announced a review.

Renn wrote privately to say that reviews could be made useful if enough people kept asking specific questions.

Mara began sending copies of the old charter.

Not to every station.

Only to the ones that asked.

There were more than she expected.

---

Spring brought a ship with broken masts, an early run of silver fish, and a letter addressed to Bera.

The envelope had traveled through six stations.

Its corners were soft from handling.

Bera held it for almost an hour before opening it.

Mara worked at the other desk.

She did not ask.

At last, paper tore.

Bera read.

Then read again.

“Perrin,” she said.

Mara looked up.

“He’s alive?”

“Yes.”

The word barely came out.

“How old?”

“Forty-two.”

Bera was thirty-one.

Or had been.

They had never settled what age meant after the house.

She put the letter down.

Mara came to sit beside her.

“He has children.”

“Did he write?”

“His daughter.”

Bera laughed once, breathlessly.

“My granddaughter.”

Mara waited.

The letter contained an address and a photograph.

Perrin stood beside a woman and two young people in a garden.

He had Bera’s mouth.

There was no mistaking it.

Bera touched the image.

“I won’t know him.”

“No.”

“He won’t know me.”

“He might want to.”

“What if he doesn’t?”

Mara thought of her father in the doorway.

The repaired shelf.

The note about fish.

“Then we find out carefully.”

---

They did not open a route that day.

They wrote.

Bera’s first letter was nine pages.

She tore it up.

The second was three.

She tore that up too.

The third began:

*I do not know how to ask whether you want to hear from me.*

She kept that one.

Mara delivered it to the courier slot in the boat’s office.

The reply took twelve days.

Bera kept working.

She balanced the accounts.

She argued with Olan about a damaged shipment.

She reminded Tomas that fish donated to the station still needed to be entered in the stores ledger.

On the twelfth day, she was upstairs checking a window latch when the bell rang.

Mara brought the letter to her.

Bera read it standing.

Then she sat on the bed.

Perrin had written only one page.

He wanted to see her.

He was angry.

He did not know whether the anger belonged to her.

He wanted to see her anyway.

Bera folded the letter.

“Good,” she said.

It did not sound like happiness.

It sounded like ground underfoot.

---

The route opened into Perrin’s garden.

There was no table arranged for a grand reunion.

No crowd.

Only two chairs beneath a tree.

Perrin stood beside one.

His wife waited near the house, close enough to come if asked.

Bera looked through the passage for a long time.

Then she turned to Mara.

“Keep it open?”

“As long as you need within the safe interval. I’ll warn you before it changes.”

“Not until I say?”

“Not without warning.”

Bera nodded.

She understood the difference.

She stepped through.

Perrin did not run to her.

Neither did she.

They looked at one another.

At last he said, “You’re younger than I remember.”

Bera laughed, and it broke halfway through.

“You’re older.”

“Yes.”

“I’m sorry.”

He looked down at his hands.

“I know.”

Mara lowered her eyes to the instrument.

Colm checked the timer.

The passage remained steady.

Beyond it, two people sat beneath a tree and began with the weather because everything else was too large.

---

Bera returned before the safe interval ended.

She was exhausted.

Her son had asked why she had taken the strange door.

She had told him about the rain.

The wet boots.

The tiredness.

He had stared at her.

“All that,” she told Mara, “because I wanted to get out of the rain.”

Mara did not tell her it could have happened to anyone.

She did not know whether that would help.

Instead she asked, “Will you go again?”

“Yes.”

“Will you stay?”

“I don’t know.”

They sat in the office below the boat.

Outside the window, snow fell on a city neither had visited.

Bera opened the curtain halfway.

“Do you ever wonder about the woman in the photograph?”

“Yes.”

“And the child?”

“Yes.”

“Do you want to know?”

Mara looked at the locked drawer.

“Some days.”

“What stops you?”

“Other days.”

Bera nodded.

She rested her head against the chair.

For once, she did not ask about the accounts.

---

The locked drawer opened during a storm.

No key.

No announcement.

Mara was securing the office before going ashore when she heard the latch click.

She stood still.

Rain hammered the harbor above.

The window showed the same storm for the first time since she had found the room.

She opened the drawer.

Inside were letters.

Not records.

Not instructions.

Letters addressed to people whose names she did not recognize.

At the top lay an envelope marked:

**FOR THE MARA WHO FINDS THIS**

She brought it upstairs.

Tomas was waiting on the steps with a lantern.

“What is it?”

“A letter.”

“Of course it is.”

She smiled.

They went to the chandlery, where everyone had gathered because the roof was newer than the inn’s.

Mara did not open the envelope in front of them.

She waited until the storm had eased and the room was quiet.

Then she sat by the stove.

The letter began:

*I was tempted to leave you an account of your life.*

*I decided against it. Mine is not yours, and there are already too many people trying to sell certainty.*

*The girl in the photograph is Ada.*

*She is not a promise.*

Mara put the paper down.

For a while, she watched the fire.

---

Ada had arrived at the station at seven years old.

The letter did not say where she came from.

It said she had stayed.

It said she hated porridge, loved dismantling clocks, and had once attempted to inspect the inside of the bell by filling it with flour.

It said the office’s biscuit policy had improved under her influence.

It said nothing about whether Mara should look for her.

At the bottom of the second page:

*If you meet her, meet her. Do not go searching for a child because I loved one.*

Mara read that twice.

The third page concerned the drawer.

The letters were for former travelers whose routes had been lost during the charter dispute.

Some might be dead.

Some might still be waiting.

The other Mara had not been able to finish the work.

*There is no obligation here either,* she had written. *Only addresses, and what I know.*

Mara looked at the stack.

No obligation.

That was true in one sense.

In another, there were names in her hands.

She did not decide that night.

She put the letters back in order.

Then she went home.

---

The first letter they delivered was to a gardener.

It took four days to find the route.

The gardener was alive.

He had been ninety when the letter was written and was now ninety-four.

He read it under a fig tree.

Then he asked Mara to wait while he fetched a tin.

Inside were seeds.

He gave her six packets.

“For the station.”

“What are they?”

He named plants she had never heard of.

“Will they grow in salt air?”

“Some of them.”

“Which?”

He smiled.

“You’ll find out.”

Mara took the seeds.

On the way home, Colm asked what the letter had said.

“I don’t know.”

“You didn’t read it?”

“It wasn’t addressed to me.”

He looked at the packets.

“Fair.”

They planted them behind the chandlery.

Three grew.

One flowered in winter.

One attracted moths larger than Tomas approved of.

The third tasted, according to Jory, exactly like a herb his mother had put in soup.

He sat beside it for a long time.

Then he asked for a cutting.

---

The second letter had no living recipient.

They found a house that had become a school.

No one remembered the name.

Mara asked the teacher whether there were old records.

There were.

They spent the afternoon in a room full of dust and wooden boxes.

The recipient had died twenty-three years earlier.

His daughter had moved away.

Her son lived in a town three days north.

Mara wrote the address down.

They did not continue that day.

The route interval was closing.

Colm watched her face.

“Do you want to extend?”

“Can we safely?”

“Possibly.”

“Then no.”

They returned.

Mara entered the result.

Not delivered.

Not failed.

**Further contact found.**

Bera approved.

The third letter took them to a house where the person named on the envelope denied ever having been through a crossing.

Mara apologized.

As they turned to leave, the woman said, “Wait.”

She took the letter.

Her hands shook.

No one asked why she had denied it.

---

The clerk from Low Water returned in summer.

She appeared in the office window.

Not behind the glass.

In the view beyond it.

The curtain was open to let in light from a bright, unfamiliar afternoon. Mara was sorting correspondence when the street outside emptied.

A desk stood on the pavement.

The clerk sat behind it.

Mara did not touch the window.

She called Sen.

He came downstairs with Colm.

Bera followed.

The clerk opened her ledger.

“Your station is obstructing lawful return.”

Mara checked the latch.

Still shut.

“Whose return?”

“Several outstanding accounts.”

“Names.”

The clerk smiled.

“You insist on that.”

“Yes.”

“You have records that belong to the office.”

“We have letters.”

“Property.”

“Whose?”

“The office’s.”

Bera stepped beside Mara.

“Produce the ownership record.”

The clerk’s eyes narrowed.

For a moment, the afternoon outside flickered.

Mara saw the bridge.

The still river.

The fourth arch.

Then the street returned.

The clerk turned a page.

“You cannot keep everyone.”

Mara thought of Kit.

Of Anja.

Of Bera in the garden with her grown son.

“We don’t,” she said.

The clerk looked at her as if the answer had been in a language she did not know.

---

Sen spoke next.

“What is your name?”

The clerk’s pen stopped.

“My designation is not relevant.”

“It may be.”

“I am the receiving officer.”

“For how long?”

The woman closed the ledger.

“Your inquiries exceed local authority.”

“Perhaps,” Sen said. “What is your name?”

The window darkened.

Bera touched Mara’s elbow.

The clerk’s face had changed.

Not much.

Just enough.

Mara had seen that change before: in Tomas, in her father, in people hearing a question they had built years around avoiding.

“Do you know it?” she asked.

The clerk stood.

The desk remained.

Her chair slid backward without sound.

“Your station will receive notice.”

Then she was gone.

The view outside returned to an ordinary street.

A boy ran past carrying a loaf of bread.

Colm let out a long breath.

Sen stayed by the window.

“She may be held there too.”

Tomas, who had arrived halfway down the stairs, said, “That doesn’t make her harmless.”

“No,” Mara replied.

It did not.

But she took a new sheet of paper.

At the top, she wrote:

**Receiving officer — name unknown.**

---

They searched the oldest records.

Not for the office.

For its staff.

There were lists of appointments, resignations, transfers, and payments.

The receiving officer appeared first as a signature.

Then initials.

Then a designation.

At some point, the name had disappeared.

Bera worked backward.

Sen compared station seals.

Colm found a reference in an equipment requisition.

A chair.

A desk.

A lamp.

One personal locker.

The requisition had been signed:

**E. DAREN**

Mara stared at the name.

It meant nothing to her.

That was almost reassuring.

No hidden family connection.

No version of herself.

Just someone who had once needed a chair.

They found the appointment notice two days later.

**Elian Daren, junior receiving clerk, temporary placement.**

Temporary.

Mara put down the paper.

“How long ago?”

Sen checked the date conversion.

“Eighty-six years.”

Outside, the harbor bell struck noon.

Inside, no one moved.

---

Elian Daren had been twenty-two.

She had applied for the post because it offered lodging and passage credits.

Her previous employment was listed as kitchen assistant aboard a river steamer.

Her next of kin was a brother named Sef.

The contract was for six months.

There was a renewal clause.

Mara read it.

Bera read it.

Then they both read it again.

The placement renewed automatically while any case assigned to the clerk remained unresolved.

Below it was a duty statement.

All arrivals after the clerk’s first day would be assigned to her until another clerk was appointed.

There was no record of another appointment.

Tomas took the page.

He held it for a long time.

“She could never finish.”

“No,” Bera said.

“And if people stayed unresolved—”

“She stayed.”

Mara thought of the cabinet knocking behind Elian’s desk.

The room full of boxes.

The young man with the broom.

“She made other people wait because she couldn’t leave.”

“That may be part of it,” Sen said.

“Not all.”

“No.”

Bera folded the contract.

“We’ll need her consent to help.”
