Tomas looked at her.

“She may not give it.”

“I know.”

---

They wrote to Elian.

The letter was short.

It used her name.

It described what they had found.

It did not promise release.

It did not excuse what she had done.

It offered an independent review of her placement.

Mara signed.

So did Bera, Sen, and Colm.

Tomas read it and added his name at the bottom.

“I reserve the right to dislike her,” he said.

“Granted,” Bera replied.

They sent the letter through the bridge drawing.

That was Sen’s idea.

The paper had been an outgoing signal once. With the right marker, it might carry something back.

Mara placed the envelope against the glass.

Colm adjusted the instrument.

For a moment, the drawing deepened.

The river moved.

The envelope sank into the image as though laid on water.

Then it was gone.

They waited.

A week passed.

Two.

The station continued.

People arrived.

Letters left.

The winter-flowering plant grew a second branch.

On the eighteenth day, the bridge drawing changed.

A small envelope lay on the desk.

Mara touched the glass with the return marker.

The reply fell onto the table.

There were four words.

**I cannot leave unattended.**

---

Bera read the reply.

Then she wrote beneath it:

**Who requires that?**

They sent it back.

The answer came the same day.

**The office.**

Sen leaned over the page.

“Ask whether that means the building, the board, or the contract.”

Mara did.

The reply took longer.

When it came, the handwriting was less steady.

**I do not know anymore.**

Tomas stood by the window.

For once, he had no comment.

Colm opened the old technical log.

“There may be an automatic continuity process.”

“Better words,” Tomas said quietly.

“A machine that keeps the place working after the people responsible have gone.”

“And if nobody tells it to stop?”

“It keeps working.”

Mara looked toward the covered original lamp.

An instrument built to return people.

A house that kept attendants.

A contract that renewed until impossible work was done.

“Can we tell it to stop?”

“Maybe.”

“Without moving someone else into her place?”

Colm did not answer immediately.

Then he said, “That is the question we need to solve.”

---

They did not begin with the office.

They began with one cupboard.

Elian sent a diagram.

It was crude, drawn with the impatience of someone who had spent decades knowing exactly where everything was and had forgotten that others did not.

The cupboard held three unresolved goods consignments.

No people.

Mara insisted on confirming that.

Twice.

One crate of cracked plates.

One missing parcel whose contents were unknown.

One shipment of apples that had decayed seventy years earlier.

“These are keeping her there?” Tomas asked.

“Among other things.”

“Apples.”

Bera studied the assignment rules.

“The office requires a final state.”

“Rotten seems final.”

“Not if no one entered it.”

They asked Elian to mark the apples as destroyed.

She replied that only the sender could authorize disposal.

The sender was dead.

The sender’s company was gone.

The destination station had closed.

Colm put his head in his hands.

Mara wrote a proposed procedure.

Public notice.

Evidence of condition.

A waiting period.

Independent confirmation.

A recorded decision with the right to challenge it.

Bera revised the wording.

Renn reviewed it.

Elian applied it.

Three days later, the bridge drawing showed a cupboard with one empty shelf.

---

They celebrated with cake.

Not because the work was finished.

Because a shelf was empty.

Tomas said this was a depressing standard for celebration.

He had two slices.

The cracked plates followed.

The missing parcel required more work.

Its sender was dead, but the intended recipient’s granddaughter still lived near Reedmarket.

Nessa found her.

She had heard about the parcel all her life.

It had contained a wedding veil.

Her grandmother had married without it and complained about the missing lace every anniversary.

The granddaughter authorized the claim to be closed.

She asked for a copy of the correspondence.

Elian sent it.

The cupboard emptied.

In the next letter, her handwriting changed.

Not dramatically.

The loops grew less cramped.

At the bottom, beneath the formal notice of closure, she wrote:

**There is another cupboard.**

Bera smiled without happiness.

“Of course there is.”

---

The second cupboard held people.

Not physically.

Records.

Route fragments.

Things taken as collateral.

A ring.

A name.

The memory of a door.

Mara read the list and felt the room grow colder.

“Can those be returned?”

Sen examined the descriptions.

“Some.”

“What about the others?”

“We need to know what was actually removed and what the office merely claimed to hold.”

Tomas looked at him.

“There’s a difference?”

“Yes. A large one.”

The first item belonged to a man named Hadrin Pell.

Jory’s hand stopped halfway to his cup.

Mara looked up.

“Family?”

“My son.”

No one spoke.

Jory sat very still.

He had told them about Bell Lane.

About his mother’s soup.

About the hen’s impossible preferences.

He had never mentioned a son.

“Where is he?” Mara asked gently.

“I don’t know.”

“What was taken?”

She checked the list.

**Recognition of original household.**

Jory stared at the paper.

Then he began to laugh.

It was the worst sound Mara had ever heard from him.

“All those years,” he said. “I thought he’d chosen not to know me.”

---

They found Hadrin in a town of narrow brick houses.

He was fifty-seven.

He worked repairing pumps.

He had a wife, three grown children, and a photograph on the mantelpiece of a man he could not name.

Jory stood at the edge of the route.

He had changed his shirt twice.

The hen remained at the station.

Mara went first, with Hadrin’s permission obtained by letter.

She explained only what they knew.

A record.

A claim.

A possible interference with memory.

Not a certainty.

Hadrin listened.

Then he brought the photograph.

It was Jory, younger, standing beside a boy at a fair.

The boy held a wooden horse.

“I’ve had this since I can remember,” Hadrin said. “I thought perhaps he was an uncle.”

Mara looked toward the passage.

Jory had not moved.

“Would you like to meet him?”

Hadrin swallowed.

“Yes.”

The old man stepped through.

Neither recognized the other in the simple way Mara had hoped for.

There was no sudden light.

No collapse of decades.

Hadrin looked at Jory’s face, then at the photograph.

Jory looked at the man his son had become.

At last Hadrin said, “You’re taller in the picture.”

Jory laughed through tears.

“I’ve been shrinking.”

---

Returning the record did not restore everything.

It loosened something.

Hadrin remembered the fair.

Then the smell of Jory’s coat.

Then an argument about leaving home.

The memories came over weeks, not minutes.

Some never came.

Elian sent the original contract.

Hadrin had asked for a place where no one knew him after a bitter fight.

The office had interpreted the request broadly.

Jory read that sentence.

He sat beside Mara for a long time.

“I was not an easy father.”

Mara thought of Eli.

“I believe you.”

Jory looked at her, surprised.

Then he nodded.

“Good.”

“Do you want me to say it doesn’t matter?”

“No.”

“What do you want?”

He looked at the letter from his son.

“A little more time.”

That, Mara thought, was at least something the station might help him ask for.

---

The work changed Elian.

Not quickly.

She remained formal.

She remained capable of cruelty when frightened.

Once, she closed a route without warning because a disagreement over jurisdiction became too much for her.

No one was hurt.

Mara wrote that it could not happen again.

Elian replied with three pages explaining the pressures on her office.

Bera read them.

Then she sent back one sentence.

**We understand the pressure; the boundary remains.**

For nine days, there was no reply.

On the tenth, Elian sent a revised procedure.

It required two-person authorization for closing an occupied route.

She had signed it.

There was a blank space for the second signature.

Mara did not sign immediately.

She asked Renn to review it.

Then Sen.

Then the travelers who might be affected.

By the time the procedure was agreed, everyone involved was tired of it.

That was acceptable.

It worked.

---

The photograph in the office changed in late autumn.

Not the people.

The background.

Mara noticed while filing a letter.

Behind the other Mara and Ada, the office window had once shown a bright street.

Now it showed the harbor.

Their harbor.

The repaired chandlery.

The sign.

Mara took the photograph upstairs.

Sen examined it.

“Could be a route shift.”

“Could it mean they’re nearer?”

“Yes.”

“Could it mean they’re in danger?”

“Yes.”

“Could it mean nothing we can use?”

“Yes.”

She sighed.

He looked apologetic.

“Those are the honest answers.”

“I know.”

She put the photograph back.

That night, she dreamed of a child checking biscuit tins.

In the dream, Mara asked, “Are you Ada?”

The child looked offended.

“Everyone asks the wrong thing first.”

“What should I ask?”

“Whether they’re stale.”

Mara woke laughing.

Then she sat in the dark, unexpectedly close to tears.

In the morning, she bought fresh biscuits.

Not because of the dream, she told herself.

Because the station was out.

---

Winter was hard.

Not magical.

The ordinary kind.

Coal cost more.

Three people fell ill.

A roof leaked.

The road south closed under snow, delaying lens parts.

Olan wrote apologetically.

Bera wrote back without apology and reminded him of the delivery clause.

He found another route.

Sen caught the fever and tried to work through it until Leth took the handpiece away.

Colm broke an instrument by hurrying.

Mara snapped at him.

Then apologized.

Then apologized again because the first apology had included too much explanation.

Tomas strained his back lifting a crate he should have asked someone to help with.

Jory fed the hen indoors against everyone’s wishes.

The hen laid an egg in the customs officer’s hat.

For one afternoon, the whole station laughed.

Mara wrote no grand lessons in the notebook that week.

She wrote:

Coal.

Soup.

Clean blankets.

Repair latch.

Ask for help before lifting.

It was enough work.

---

Bera left in the first week of spring.

Not forever.

She had agreed to spend three months with Perrin.

Her granddaughter was getting married.

Bera had been asked to come.

She had also been asked to help with the accounts for the family workshop, which she suspected was a tactful way of giving her somewhere to stand when conversation became difficult.

She accepted both invitations.

Before leaving, she trained the customs officer’s niece, Nima, to manage the station books.

Nima was nineteen and feared nothing except making an error in front of Bera.

Bera told her that concealment was worse than error.

Nima immediately confessed to three.

They got along after that.

At the route, Bera hugged Mara.

“You are allowed to take a day off.”

“So are you.”

“I shall take one at the wedding.”

“One?”

“Perhaps two.”

Mara smiled.

Bera stepped through.

On the far side, Perrin waited with a suitcase in each hand.

He looked at his mother’s single small bag.

“That’s all?”

“I know how to travel lightly now.”

His face changed.

She touched his arm.

“It was a joke.”

“I know.”

It would take time for them to learn which jokes were safe.

They had some time.

The route closed.

---

The station felt different without Bera.

Not empty.

Less certain of its shelves.

Nima worked carefully.

Too carefully at first.

She checked every sum four times and stayed late until Mara asked what she was afraid of.

“Losing someone.”

Mara set down the kettle.

“Through the accounts?”

“What if I enter the wrong status?”

“Then we correct it.”

“What if it changes something before we notice?”

Mara looked at the ledger.

The old systems had made this fear reasonable.

She could not dismiss it.

They spent the next week separating the human records from the route controls.

No status change in the ledger could operate an instrument.

No instrument could alter the ledger without leaving a visible request for review.

Colm built a physical switch between them.

Sen tested it.

Nima deliberately entered impossible values.

Nothing opened.

Nothing closed.

She smiled for the first time in days.

Mara added a line to the field manual.

*An accounting mistake must not become a door.*

The book accepted the ink.

It did not improve the sentence.

She left it anyway.

---

Elian’s office lost its third wall in May.

She reported this in a letter of alarming brevity.

**The north wall is no longer present. No immediate injuries. Records relocated. Advice requested.**

Colm read it twice.

“Define no longer present.”

They asked.

Elian sent a drawing.

Beyond the place where the wall had stood lay a dark sea.

No shore.

No sky.

Just water, level with the floor.

The desk stood a few feet from its edge.

Sen recognized the shape of the remaining room.

“It’s losing its anchors.”

“Because we’re clearing cases?” Mara asked.

“Possibly.”

“Is that good?”

“Not if the building depends on them.”

Tomas sat down.

“So if we finish, it falls apart.”

“That may be what the original attendants feared.”

Mara looked at Elian’s six-month contract.

Eighty-six years of work holding a room together.

“Can she leave now?”

They asked.

The reply came quickly.

**There are still occupied routes.**

“Then we move them,” Mara said.

Colm looked at her.

“Where?”

Mara looked around the chandlery.

The benches.

The crowded noticeboard.

The small workshop.

Not here.

Not all at once.

She took a new sheet of paper and began listing stations that had answered their letters.

---

Reedmarket agreed first.

Nessa’s reply was eight words.

**Have room for six. More if nobody snores.**

Kit and Renna offered the ferry house for temporary arrivals.

Renn arranged two staffed receiving rooms through stations still inside the network.

The gardener offered a cottage.

Olan offered transport crates, then clarified in a second letter that he meant for equipment, not people.

Mara appreciated the clarification.

The town council opened the old school.

The customs officer insisted on fire exits.

No one laughed.

They had learned enough about rooms with one door.

Sen worked on route transfer.

Colm worked on the portable beacons.

Nima tracked consent.

Every person in Low Water received an explanation through Elian.

Some refused to trust her.

Understandably.

Mara and Renn took turns speaking with them through temporary windows.

One woman asked for a different clerk.

Elian stepped away from the desk.

It was the first time Mara had seen her do that.

---

The first transfer was a man named Soro.

He had been waiting for a ship that no longer existed.

He understood this.

He had understood it for years.

The office had not.

When Mara asked where he wanted to go, he said, “Somewhere I can walk without coming back to the same chair.”

Reedmarket sent a view of its wooden walkways.

He considered the water beneath them.

“Can I swim?”

“Yes,” Nessa said through the connection. “But not under the mill.”

“Good.”

He crossed.

Nima marked the departure.

Nessa marked the arrival.

The two counts matched.

In Low Water, a section of ceiling vanished.

Elian moved her desk away from the falling dust.

“No immediate injury,” she reported.

Mara gripped the edge of the table.

“Stop saying it like that.”

Elian looked at her.

For a moment, the old coldness returned.

Then she looked at the ceiling.

“I was frightened,” she said.

It was the first plainly personal sentence she had spoken.

Mara nodded.

“All right. We’ll slow down and reinforce before the next one.”

---

They could not reinforce the ceiling.

Not with timber.

Not with steel.

The room was held by assigned obligations, not beams.

Sen disliked saying this because it sounded mystical and made Colm reach for instruments that could not measure it.

Renn proposed transferring the office’s duties to a temporary committee.

Bera, consulted by letter, replied:

**Do not replace one captive with six volunteers who cannot leave.**

She was right.

They revised the plan.

Fixed terms.

Explicit duties.

No automatic renewal.

Separate responsibility for occupied routes.

Authority to halt intake.

Independent review.

A funded closure process.

Tomas read the final draft and said it sounded like a roof built from arguments.

“It is,” Sen said.

“Will it hold?”

“We’ll test one section.”

They did.

The ceiling remained.

Elian touched the wall that had returned beside her desk.

Her hand trembled.

“Temporary,” Mara reminded her.

Elian nodded.

This time, temporary had an end date.

---

The last occupied route was a nursery.

No one knew why.

There were no children inside.

Only six empty beds and a rocking chair that moved occasionally though no one sat in it.

Elian had marked the route as occupied because she could hear breathing.

Sen listened through the instrument.

So did Renn.

So did Mara.

There was breathing.

Slow.

Deep.

Not from any one bed.

From the room itself.

Colm checked the old catalogues.

He found a model of emergency shelter designed for infants.

It maintained warmth, clean air, and a soothing sound until a caretaker arrived.

“How long?” Mara asked.

“Sixty-four years.”

Elian closed her eyes.

The breathing had been a recording.

Or its mechanical equivalent.

No children remained.

They verified this with three different methods.

Then they shut the shelter down.

The rocking chair stopped.

In Low Water, the last cabinet door swung open.

Empty.

Elian stood behind her desk.

For a long time, she did not move.

Mara spoke through the passage.

“You can leave.”

The clerk looked at the ledger.

“I should close—”

“We have the closure list.”

“The records—”

“Copied.”

“The desk—”

“It can stay.”

Elian put down her pen.

Then she picked it up again.

Mara waited.

At last, Elian placed it in her pocket.

She walked around the desk.

---

At the edge of the passage, Elian stopped.

Beyond her, the office had become little more than a floor above dark water.

The desk stood at the center.

The ledger lay open.

The chair waited.

“What happens to it?” she asked.

“The station will be decommissioned,” Renn said.

“Will it hurt?”

Mara looked at Sen.

He did not answer quickly.

“We don’t think the office feels pain,” he said. “We cannot prove that it does not.”

Elian looked back.

“I hated it.”

No one spoke.

“I kept it.”

“Yes,” Mara said.

“I don’t know what I am without it.”

“You don’t have to know before you step through.”

Elian’s face tightened.

Then she crossed.

She was shorter than Mara expected.

Her dark coat smelled of old paper.

When both feet touched the receiving tile, Nima said, clearly, “Arrival confirmed.”

Mara looked at the departure count.

One.

Arrival count.

One.

The passage closed.

On the bridge drawing, the desk disappeared.

---

Elian did not become kind at once.

She complained about the room assigned to her.

She questioned Nima’s competence.

She tried to reorganize the station records without asking.

Mara stopped her.

Elian reacted badly.

Then, later, she apologized with visible difficulty.

There were people in town who did not want to see her.

Bera, returning for a visit, was one of them.

Mara did not arrange a meeting.

Elian asked whether Bera knew she was there.

“Yes.”

“Will she speak to me?”

“Not now.”

“I have explanations.”

“I know.”

Elian looked at the floor.

“Does she?”

“She knows you have them.”

That was all Mara said.

Renn organized a review of the harms caused by Low Water.

Elian participated.

Sometimes she answered directly.

Sometimes she defended herself.

Sometimes the session stopped because someone needed to leave.

No one called the process healing.

It was work.

It was incomplete.

It mattered.

---

On the first anniversary of her father’s return, Mara bought onions.

And flour.

And blue thread.

She did not realize the date until she reached the harbor.

The green boat bumped the steps.

Its bell remained silent.

At home, Eli was making a stew.

He had remembered.

Neither said so immediately.

Mara set the basket on the table.

“Blue thread,” he said.

“They had some.”

“Good.”

He stirred the pot.

She sat.

After a while, he asked, “Do you wish I hadn’t come back?”

She looked at him.

“No.”

He nodded too quickly.

She let the silence settle.

“Sometimes I wish you’d come back differently.”

“Yes.”

“Sometimes I wish you were the man I remembered.”

“I’m not.”

“I know.”

He put down the spoon.

“I’m trying not to leave the rest to you.”

She thought of the meals.

The repaired roofs.

The way he sat with new arrivals without demanding their stories.

“I’ve noticed.”

He closed his eyes.

When he opened them, the stew had begun to stick.

Mara stood to fetch water.

He laughed.

It was an ordinary mistake.

They were learning to have those again.

---

The next morning, a child arrived at the station.

She was not Ada.

Mara knew this at once and was ashamed of the flicker of disappointment.

The child was a boy of perhaps eight, with a bundle tied in a tablecloth and a serious expression.

He had come by road.

No boat.

No door.

No strange light.

“My aunt said you help people get home.”

“Sometimes,” Mara said.

“Can you help me?”

“Where is home?”

He named a village fifteen miles inland.

Mara knew it.

“What happened?”

“I came to find my father.”

“Did you?”

“No.”

“Where is your aunt?”

“At home.”

“Does she know you left?”

His silence answered.

Mara brought him inside.

Tomas sent a message to the village.

Eli made breakfast.

Colm put away the route instrument.

They did not need it.

By afternoon, the aunt arrived furious and frightened.

The boy cried.

So did she, though she tried to hide it.

Mara entered the case in the ledger.

**Returned by cart.**

Nima added the cost of oats.

---

Summer brought visitors who were merely curious.

Mara disliked them more than she expected.

They wanted to see a door open.

They asked whether the station could show them a richer version of themselves.

A younger spouse.

A world where an investment had succeeded.

A dead dog.

Some requests were cruel.

Some were simply human.

She learned not to answer them all with the same voice.

“No,” she told the man who wanted to inspect other versions of his business partner without permission.

“I’m sorry,” she told the woman who brought the dog’s collar.

“We don’t know how to do that safely.”

The woman nodded.

Then asked whether she could sit for a while.

Mara brought tea.

No instrument was used.

When the woman left, she took the collar with her.

Tomas watched from the doorway.

“Harder to charge for that.”

“We don’t.”

“I know.”

Bera, back at the accounts desk for the month, looked up.

“It still belongs in the budget.”

So they made room for tea.

---

The station’s first apprentice was Nima.

Not in accounting.

In route work.

She asked after six months of keeping the ledger.

Mara said they would need a curriculum.

Sen looked faintly wounded.

“We have one.”

“We have an introductory course that began while a coercive boat was outside.”

“That was an unusual class.”

“Then let’s make a usual one.”

They did.

The first lesson contained no active instrument.

It concerned listening.

The second concerned consent.

The third concerned what to do when an answer did not fit the question.

Only in the fourth did Nima touch a lens.

She found the pace maddening.

Colm sympathized.

Then he told her about the bench he had burned.

She became more patient.

At the end of the month, she conducted her first supervised route confirmation.

The traveler changed their mind.

Nima closed the instrument safely.

She looked disappointed.

Mara pointed to the lesson record.

Successful confirmation.

Informed refusal.

Safe closure.

Nima read it.

Then she smiled.

---

The second apprentice was Tomas.

He denied this vigorously.

“I’m learning enough to stop the rest of you doing something foolish.”

“That is a substantial portion of the syllabus,” Sen said.

Tomas was excellent with equipment and impatient with forms.

He could detect a misaligned housing by touch.

He could not remember which copy of a departure notice went to the receiving station.

Nima made him a checklist.

He complained.

He used it.

During his first supervised transfer, the route flickered.

Tomas did not panic.

He rang the bell, secured the line, and waited for confirmation.

Afterward, Mara praised him.

He grunted.

Then he asked whether the checklist could be laminated.

Olan’s factory did not know what lamination was.

They invented something close enough.

It became their most popular nonoptical product.

Bera insisted on a fair price.

---

The field manual grew.

Not rapidly.

A page here.

A diagram there.

Some entries came from Mara’s future handwriting.

Others appeared in hands she did not recognize.

Once, a note arrived in Tomas’s unmistakable scrawl.

*If the rope is frayed, replace it. Do not hold a meeting about the rope.*

He denied writing it.

Mara believed him.

Then they replaced the rope.

Another page contained a recipe for soup.

Sen suspected it was accidental.

Bera disagreed.

The margins accumulated corrections.

Warnings were rewritten when experience proved them too broad or too narrow.

A page headed **NEVER TRUST A FAMILIAR VOICE** became **VERIFY A FAMILIAR VOICE WHEN THE ROUTE IS UNCERTAIN**.

Mara liked that revision.

She had come to distrust rules that required people to become less human in order to stay safe.

Some danger came precisely from the wish to be certain enough never to be hurt.

She did not write that in the manual.

It belonged in a conversation, not a procedure.

The procedure said what to check.

That was enough.

---

One evening, Elian asked to see the sea.

Mara thought she meant the harbor.

“No,” Elian said. “The ordinary sea.”

They walked to the headland.

The ruins of the old lighthouse were still visible offshore at low tide, though the tower itself had returned to being stone and wreckage.

Elian stood on the grass.

Wind pulled at her coat.

For a long time, she watched the waves.

“There was no weather in the office.”

Mara waited.

“I used to remember rain.”

“Do you still?”

“Not correctly.”

A cloud passed over the sun.

Elian looked up.

“It changes without being asked.”

“Yes.”

“I had forgotten that.”

They stayed until the first drops fell.

Elian did not move.

Mara offered to go back.

“Not yet.”

Rain darkened the shoulders of the old coat.

At last Elian laughed.

It was a small, astonished sound.

Mara did not mistake it for absolution.

She was glad to hear it anyway.

---

The missing lane returned slowly.

Not by magic.

By memory, records, and people deciding to build where there had been a gap.

Jory’s sketches showed the old curve.

The customs archives held a property boundary no one had understood.

Tomas remembered a drain.

Eli found its stones.

The council argued about whether restoring a vanished street created new ownership claims.

Bera said it probably did and they should not pretend otherwise.

The arguments took months.

Eventually, they laid a narrow path behind the bakery.

They called it Bell Lane.

Jory walked it with Hadrin, who had come for a visit.

There were no old houses.

No grocer.

No family waiting behind a remembered door.

But there was room for a bench.

Jory sat.

His son sat beside him.

The hen pecked at the fresh gravel.

Tomas watched from the bakery doorway.

“Not the same.”

“No,” Mara said.

Jory looked up and waved them over.

They went.

---

On a clear morning in September, the green boat untied itself.

Mara was at the counter when the rope slipped from the mooring ring.

The bell rang twice.

She ran outside.

Tomas followed at a speed that suggested his back had improved.

The boat drifted a foot from the steps.

Then stopped.

Mara looked at the bell.

“What?”

It rang once.

Sen came out carrying the office log.

“There’s an incoming route request.”

“From where?”

He showed her.

The address was blank.

The station designation was not.

**TOMORROW**

Mara felt the air go still.

“Our station?”

“Another registration.”

Colm checked the instrument.

The request was properly signed.

Not by Mara.

By Ada Vale.

Mara sat on the harbor step.

Tomas stood beside her.

“Do you want to answer?”

She looked at the boat.

At the office beneath it.

At the photograph she had not looked at for weeks because looking had begun to feel too much like asking.

“First,” she said, “we find out what she’s requesting.”

Sen read the message.

“Temporary berth. Two passengers. One medical case. Consent to inspection confirmed.”

Mara stood.

“All right.”

---

The passage opened beside the green boat.

Not in the water.

In the air above it, like a second harbor laid over the first.

Another boat waited beyond.

It was green too, though its paint had been repaired in many places.

A young woman stood at the bow.

She had dark hair tied back with a strip of blue cloth.

One front tooth was very slightly crooked.

Mara knew her.

Not as a person.

As a possibility she had tried not to claim.

“Ada?”

The young woman’s face changed.

“Yes.”

“Who’s injured?”

“My mother.”

Mara looked past her.

A figure lay beneath blankets on the other boat’s bench.

Older.

Much older than the woman in the photograph.

Not the one-armed Mara who had brought bread.

This one had both hands.

One rested against the blanket.

The other held a station bell.

Mara turned to Colm.

“Medical assessment first.”

He nodded.

Sen called for the doctor.

Tomas secured the line.

Ada watched them move.

For a moment, her face crumpled with relief.

Then she stepped across.

---

The older Mara had a fever.

Nothing exotic.

An infection in a wound she had not treated soon enough.

The doctor was furious.

Ada looked relieved by the fury.

It was a familiar kind of danger.

A danger with clean water, bandages, medicine, and instructions.

They carried the older woman to the inn.

Mara stayed out of the room while the doctor worked.

Ada sat in the corridor with her elbows on her knees.

“You look strange,” she said.

Mara almost laughed.

“I imagine.”

“Younger than the photographs.”

“How old are you?”

“Twenty-four.”

“And she?”

“Seventy-one.”

Mara sat beside her.

For a while, neither knew where to look.

Then Ada said, “She told me not to come here unless I had to.”

“Why?”

“Because you might think you owed us something.”

Mara remembered the letter.

The girl was not a promise.

“Do you?”

“Think you owe us?”

“Yes.”

“No.”

Ada rubbed her hands together.

“I do hope you’ll let us stay until she can travel.”

“Of course.”

“Thank you.”

From inside the room, the doctor demanded more hot water.

Both women stood at once.

---

Ada was nothing like the child in Mara’s dreams.

She disliked biscuits.

She loved porridge.

She was patient with machines and impatient with people who used machinery as an excuse not to answer questions.

The photograph had captured her on an unusual day, she explained.

“She’d been feeding me sweet things because she didn’t know what else children ate.”

Mara looked toward the closed bedroom door.

“That sounds plausible.”

“She got better.”

“What happened to her?”

“The wound?”

“Everything.”

Ada smiled sadly.

“That takes a while.”

“We have some time.”

“I hope so.”

The older Mara slept for two days.

In the meantime, Ada helped Colm repair a cracked beacon and showed Nima a better way to test a route line.

She did not ask to see the ledger.

She did not treat the station as hers.

Mara noticed and was grateful.

On the third morning, Ada found the field manual.

She touched the cover.

“Oh.”

“You know it?”

“Yes.”

“Is it hers?”

Ada looked at the pages.

“Some of it.”

“Whose is the rest?”

She turned to the soup recipe.

Her mouth trembled.

“That was mine.”

---

When the older Mara woke, she asked for water.

Then for Ada.

Then whether anyone had inspected the boat’s forward housing.

Ada folded her arms.

“You can be ill for one morning without supervising.”

“I’ve been ill for three.”

“Then you’re getting practice.”

The older woman smiled.

Mara stood in the doorway.

For a moment, looking at herself was unbearable.

Not because of the age.

Because the face contained a lifetime she did not know.

The older Mara saw her.

“Hello.”

“Hello.”

“You found the drawer.”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

“Eventually.”

“I deserved that.”

Mara came to the bedside.

The older woman’s hands were scarred.

Both present.

Both trembling slightly.

“Did you write the manual?”

“Some.”

“Did you write the bad schedule?”

The older woman closed her eyes.

Ada looked away.

“Yes,” she said.

Mara sat.

The room seemed to grow smaller.

“Tell me.”

---

She had signed a different agreement.

Not Olan’s first offer.

A later one.

A network partnership designed to keep small stations solvent.

There had been a famine in three linked regions.

Routes were failing.

Travelers were stranded.

The proposed system paid stations for maintaining continuity.

At first, it helped.

Rooms stayed warm.

People had food.

Repairs were made.

Then the measures became targets.

The targets became conditions.

The conditions became reasons not to finish a return.

“I thought safeguards would be added,” the older Mara said.

“Were they?”

“Yes.”

“Did they work?”

“Some did.”

“Enough?”

“No.”

She looked at Ada.

“I withdrew our station. Then I found out withdrawal did not release the people whose routes had been assigned through us.”

“The existing agreement.”

“Until resolved.”

Mara’s mouth went dry.

“You tried to change it.”

“Yes.”

“The office under the boat?”

“My way of keeping records outside the network.”

“The manual?”

“My way of warning whoever came next.”

“Me.”

“Among others.”

Mara looked at the woman in the bed.

“You could have told me directly.”

“I didn’t know which of you would receive it.”

“And when you did?”

The older Mara did not answer.

Mara waited.

At last the woman said, “I was ashamed.”

---

Anger came as relief.

Mara had spent months being confused by futures, instruments, contracts, and versions of herself whose motives she could only guess.

Here was something plain.

“You left me warnings in a book because you couldn’t bear to say what you’d done.”

“Yes.”

“You let me wonder whether I was destined to make it.”

“I hoped you wouldn’t.”

“That is not the same as helping.”

“No.”

Ada moved toward the window.

The older Mara reached for her hand, then stopped.

Mara saw that, too.

She stood.

“I need to go.”

The older woman nodded.

“Will you come back?”

“I don’t know when.”

“All right.”

In the corridor, Mara leaned against the wall.

Her father was waiting with a tray.

He looked at her face.

“Tea?”

She shook her head.

He set the tray down.

For a moment she thought he would offer advice.

Instead he said, “Walk?”

They went to the headland.

Mara did not speak until they reached the grass.

Then she told him.

He listened.

When she finished, he looked at the sea.

“Do you want me to say you aren’t her?”

“No.”

“Good.”

She turned.

He met her eyes.

“You aren’t. But that isn’t what hurts.”

Mara sat on the ground.

After a moment, he sat beside her.

---

Ada found her the next afternoon.

Mara was in the workshop, sanding the edge of a new housing.

“You don’t have to forgive her,” Ada said.

“I know.”

“She thinks you do.”

“That sounds like her problem.”

Ada nodded.

“It is.”

Mara set down the sandpaper.

“I’m sorry.”

“For what?”

“The way I said that.”

“You’re allowed.”

“Not at you.”

Ada leaned against the workbench.

For a while, they watched dust settle.

Then Mara asked, “Did she treat you well?”

Ada considered.

“Yes. Not perfectly.”

“I wasn’t asking for perfectly.”

“I know. People sometimes are.”

Mara nodded.

“She tried to protect me by not telling me things,” Ada said. “Then I got old enough to notice. We had a terrible year.”

“Only one?”

Ada laughed.

“The worst one lasted a year.”

“What changed?”

“I left for a while. She learned to write letters that contained information instead of advice.”

Mara smiled despite herself.

“That sounds useful.”

“It was.”

Ada picked up the sandpaper.

“May I?”

Mara handed it to her.

They worked on opposite sides of the same housing.

---

The older Mara recovered slowly.

She did not resume command.

Partly because Ada would not permit it.

Partly because the station did not recognize her authority merely because it recognized her face.

Nima asked her to sign the visitors’ log.

The older woman did.

Tomas made her use the checklist.

She did.

Bera, arriving for another month at the station, read her account of the continuity scheme and asked seventeen questions.

The older Mara answered fourteen.

For the remaining three, she said she did not know.

Bera wrote them down.

Mara watched from the doorway.

It was not punishment.

It was not forgiveness.

It was the ordinary work of making a person’s knowledge available without making their judgment absolute.

That, she thought, might be harder than either.

---

The older Mara and Elian met in the garden.

No one arranged it.

Elian was pruning the winter-flowering shrub, badly but with determination.

The older Mara came out for air.

They recognized each other.

Mara saw the moment from the window.

Elian stood.

The pruning shears remained in her hand.

For several seconds, neither moved.

Then Elian put the shears on the bench.

“You signed it.”

“Yes.”

“I kept it.”

“Yes.”

Mara could not hear what came next.

She considered going outside.

Bera, beside her, shook her head.

“Wait.”

The two older women sat.

They did not embrace.

They spoke for nearly an hour.

When Elian came inside, her face was pale.

“What happened?” Mara asked.

“She apologized.”

“What did you say?”

“That I had used her signature to avoid thinking.”

Mara waited.

Elian looked toward the garden.

“She said she had used the committee’s approval for the same purpose.”

Neither statement made the past smaller.

But something had been named.

---

The other boat was repaired by October.

Ada inspected every part herself.

Colm did not resent it.

He asked questions.

She answered.

The older Mara stood at the harbor steps with a walking stick she disliked.

Mara stood beside her.

They had spoken several times since the sickroom.

Not enough to settle everything.

Enough to stop avoiding one another.

“Where will you go?” Mara asked.

“Back to the stations still using the old schedule.”

“Will they listen?”

“Some.”

“And the others?”

“I’ll leave copies.”

Mara nodded.

The older woman reached into her coat.

For a moment, Mara feared another mysterious letter.

Instead she produced a small packet of biscuits.

“For the office.”

“Ada says she hates them.”

“I know. These are for you.”

Mara laughed.

Then, unexpectedly, she hugged her.

The older woman held very still before returning the embrace.

“I won’t tell you what to do,” she whispered.

“Good.”

“I may still want to.”

“I know.”

They stepped apart.

Ada came down the quay carrying the final case.

She hugged Mara too.

Not as a daughter.

As someone she had come to know.

That was enough.

---

After they left, the photograph remained.

So did the manual.

Neither changed to erase what had happened.

Mara was glad.

She had begun to fear tidy stories in which the right choice made every difficult version of the past disappear.

The station still had the bad contract in its records.

The signatures.

The missing names.

The people who had returned.

The people who had not.

Nima filed the older Mara’s testimony under her full route designation, not under *founder*.

Bera approved.

Tomas asked whether there was a category for *people who should have read more carefully*.

Nima said the drawer would need to be larger.

They all laughed.

Then they went back to work.

---

The first international conference of independent return stations was held in the customs hall because no one could afford a better venue.

Tomas called it a meeting.

Nessa called it a nuisance.

Renn called it overdue.

Olan sent chairs.

This was helpful until they discovered that six had been designed for people with different knees.

Colm adapted them.

The agenda was eight pages.

By noon, they had completed half of the first.

At issue was whether every station should be required to maintain an emergency reserve.

Everyone agreed in principle.

No one agreed on how much.

Reedmarket had flood risk.

The inland stations had fuel shortages.

One station existed in a moving train and objected to rules assuming a fixed building.

Mara listened.

Then she asked what each could actually provide.

The conversation improved.

Not dramatically.

Enough.

By evening, they had a shared reserve agreement based on capacity rather than identical contributions.

Bera called it a beginning.

Tomas called for supper.

Both were correct.

---

On the second day, a traveler stood to speak.

Her name was Leth.

Most delegates knew her as the woman who had played during the previous evening’s meal.

She laid the repaired violin on the table.

“You’ve discussed equipment,” she said. “And staff. And liability. And who pays when a passage fails.”

“Yes,” Renn said.

“Who can complain when the passage succeeds and the station behaves badly?”

The hall grew quiet.

Mara looked down at the agenda.

There was a section on dispute resolution.

Between stations.

Not between stations and travelers.

Leth waited.

Nessa swore softly.

Bera drew a line through the next item.

“Then we discuss that now.”

It took the rest of the day.

They established an independent review group.

Travelers would hold half the seats.

No station could hear a complaint about itself.

Records would be available to the person named in them.

Corrections would be visible.

Appeals would not suspend food, shelter, or essential care.

Leth listened to the final wording.

Then she nodded.

“Now you can go back to the chairs.”

---

The conference ended without a name.

This irritated the printer.

He had been asked to prepare a public notice and needed something to put at the top.

Suggestions ranged from the Free Passage Association to the Allied Offices of Responsible Return.

Tomas rejected both.

Nessa suggested the People Who Count Twice.

Everyone laughed.

Then stopped.

Mara looked at the old charter.

Departure.

Arrival.

A person at both ends.

“Too long,” the printer said.

Bera took the pencil.

She wrote:

**THE TWO-COUNT AGREEMENT**

It was not elegant.

It did not sound powerful.

It described something people could check.

They kept it.

---

Years did not pass neatly.

They accumulated.

A repaired roof.

A new apprentice.

A letter that took too long.

A route found after hope had become habit.

A route lost after everyone thought it safe.

The station made mistakes.

One traveler arrived two streets from the intended address and spent an hour frightened before the local operator found him.

The report was public.

The procedure changed.

A staff member concealed an equipment fault.

No one was hurt.

The concealment was still serious.

There was an inquiry.

There was retraining.

There was a period during which the person did not operate alone.

Not every disagreement became a lesson suitable for the manual.

Some remained disagreements.

Mara grew older.

Not into either woman she had met.

Into someone who occasionally understood them better and occasionally disagreed more strongly.

Her father’s hair became fully white.

Tomas acquired glasses and denied needing them.

Jory’s hen died.

The whole station attended the burial because Jory asked.

No one made a joke.

---

Jory died the following spring.

At home.

In the room over Tomas’s shop.

Hadrin was with him.

So was Mara.

The window was open.

He had asked for that.

Near the end, he became confused and called Mara by his sister’s name.

She did not correct him.

He asked whether Bell Lane was still there.

“Yes,” she said.

“All of it?”

“No.”

He seemed to consider this.

“Enough to walk?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

Afterward, Tomas sat on the stairs for a long time.

Mara brought tea.

He did not drink it.

The next day, they closed the station for two hours.

The notice on the door said only:

**CLOSED FOR JORY PELL’S FUNERAL. EMERGENCIES: RING THE BELL.**

No one rang it.

Hadrin planted a cutting of the soup herb beside the bench in Bell Lane.

It grew.

---

The office window looked onto a familiar garden one morning.

Perrin’s garden.

Bera stood beneath the tree, arguing with someone about the placement of a table.

Mara watched for a moment before closing the curtain.

She would not turn the window into an excuse to visit without asking.

Later that day, a letter arrived.

Bera’s granddaughter had a child.

There was a photograph.

A small, furious-looking baby wrapped in a yellow blanket.

Bera wrote that everyone was well, the workshop accounts were a disgrace, and she intended to stay through the winter.

At the bottom:

*You can stop keeping my room empty. I will write before I visit.*

Mara sat with the letter for a while.

Then she went upstairs.

The room had two windows.

Both opened.

They prepared it for the next arrival.

---

The next arrival was a man who refused to say what he had lost.

Mara told him he did not have to.

He looked suspicious.

Everyone else had asked.

“Do you need help finding a place?” she said.

“Yes.”

“Can you describe it without telling me what happened?”

“Perhaps.”

They began there.

It took three meetings before he gave his name.

Five before he could look at the instrument.

Nine before he agreed to inspect a possible destination.

On the tenth, he decided to stay in town.

Mara closed the search at his request.

Nima, now an experienced operator, signed the record.

No one asked whether the effort had been worthwhile.

The man found work at the bakery.

He made excellent bread.

That was not the reason the effort had been worthwhile.

It was simply something that happened afterward.

---

The green boat brought a wooden box on the morning of Mara’s thirty-fifth birthday.

She regarded it with deep suspicion.

Tomas, who had remembered the date and was pretending not to, stood beside her.

“Open boxes,” he said.

“That advice has caused trouble.”

“It has also caused lunch.”

She opened it.

Inside were tools.

Small, precise tools for repairing optical housings.

Good ones.

A note lay on top.

*For the work you actually enjoy.*

It was signed by Ada.

Not *your daughter*.

Just Ada.

Mara smiled.

At the bottom was a second line.

*Mother says not to mention the second screw. I have mentioned it.*

Mara laughed so loudly that the customs officer looked out of his window.

That afternoon, she spent two hours at the workshop bench.

No meetings.

No letters.

No difficult questions.

Only a lens housing that could be made better with patient hands.

---

When the first ordinary ship asked to install a return instrument, Mara refused.

The captain wanted to bypass dangerous weather.

It was a reasonable wish.

The proposed installation was not reasonable.

No receiving operator.

No trained crew.

No stable route marks.

The captain offered money.

Mara still refused.

He accused her of protecting a monopoly.

That stung because she knew enough about monopolies to fear becoming one.

She asked the review group to examine the refusal.

They upheld it.

They also recommended a public training path for mobile operators.

Mara accepted the recommendation.

The captain returned six months later with two trained crew members, a receiving agreement, and a revised design.

This time, they approved a trial.

The ship completed three safe passages.

On the fourth, the captain chose to wait out a storm rather than risk an uncertain destination.

Mara considered that the best result of all.

She did not put it that way to him.

She congratulated him on the judgment.

He grunted, much like Tomas.

---

The harbor changed.

There were more languages in the market.

New dishes at the inn.

Arguments about housing.

Arguments about wages.

A foolish proposal to charge visitors extra for water.

A better proposal to expand the cistern.

Not everyone welcomed the station.

Some feared it.

Some resented it.

Some had good reasons to criticize it.

Mara learned to distinguish those things imperfectly.

At a council meeting, a fisherman stood and said the station had made rents too high for his daughter.

He was right.

The station had brought people and money without enough homes.

Mara could not solve it by pointing to the people they had helped.

She said so.

The council argued.

Then they began planning housing.

It took years.

It was less exciting than opening a passage.

It was also necessary.

Corren designed rooms with generous windows and doors that opened exactly where they appeared to.

He was proud of that.

---

One day, the field manual stopped adding pages.

No announcement.

No final warning.

The last entry concerned the safe storage of calibration medium in freezing weather.

Mara waited a month.

Then six.

Nothing changed.

Sen examined the binding.

Colm tested for active signals.

There were none.

“It may be finished,” Sen said.

Mara looked at the blank pages.

“No.”

“Finished arriving, then.”

That seemed more likely.

They continued writing in it.

When it became too full, they made copies.

When the copies diverged, they established version numbers.

Tomas objected to the first numbering scheme.

For once, everyone agreed with him.

The soup recipe remained in every edition.

No one could establish whether this was technically necessary.

No one proposed removing it.

---

The original lamp stayed under its hood for nine years.

Mara sometimes forgot it was there.

Then a new apprentice would ask, and the story would have to be told.

Not all at once.

Not as a warning against curiosity.

As a record of what happened when useful discoveries outran careful questions.

The lamp was eventually moved to a locked display room.

The maker’s mark remained visible.

Vale and Daughters.

Someone asked whether Mara wanted it covered.

“No.”

“It could confuse people.”

“Then we explain.”

The label listed the instrument’s functions, modifications, failures, and known harms.

It named Iven.

Eli.

Tomas.

The people they had saved with medicine.

The people they had lost through routes they did not understand.

It did not flatten the account into villains and heroes.

Visitors sometimes found this unsatisfying.

Mara understood.

She left the label as it was.

---

Eli asked to see the lamp alone.

Mara let him.

He stayed in the room for an hour.

When he came out, he looked tired.

“Did it do anything?” she asked.

“No.”

“What did you do?”

“Sat.”

She nodded.

He looked toward the harbor.

“I used to think if I found the right world, none of it would count.”

“The years?”

“The years. Leaving. Your mother. Everything.”

Mara waited.

“It still counts.”

“Yes.”

He nodded.

“I know.”

She took his arm.

They went to market.

He bought apples.

She bought thread.

Neither needed to explain why those ordinary things felt worth doing slowly.

---

The bridge drawing remained on the wall.

After Low Water closed, it showed only the bridge.

No desk.

No door beneath the arch.

Sometimes rain fell in the picture.

Sometimes people crossed, too small to identify.

Elian visited it occasionally.

She no longer wore the dark coat.

She had taken work cataloguing old route agreements for the review group.

It suited her exactness.

It also required other people to check her conclusions.

She accepted that better some days than others.

Mara asked once whether she wanted the drawing.

Elian shook her head.

“Not yet.”

“What are you waiting for?”

“I don’t know.”

Years earlier, Mara would have found that answer frustrating.

Now she left it alone.

A season later, Elian asked for a copy.

Not the original.

A copy.

She hung it in her room beside a window that opened onto the actual sea.

---

The first time Mara heard the name *Tomorrow* spoken by someone who had never seen the boat, she was in a city of yellow stone.

She had traveled there to inspect a new station.

At a market stall, a child pointed to the bell at her belt.

“Are you from Tomorrow?”

Mara stopped.

“Yes.”

“My uncle came through there.”

“What’s his name?”

The child told her.

Mara remembered a man who had wanted boots before answering questions.

“How is he?”

“He complains.”

Mara smiled.

“That sounds right.”

“He says you gave him the wrong size first.”

“We did.”

“He says you found another pair.”

“We did.”

The child nodded, satisfied.

Then she returned to selecting oranges.

Mara stood for a moment in the noise of the market.

No grand account.

No miracle.

A wrong pair of boots.

Then a better one.

She thought she could live with being remembered that way.

---

At forty, Mara took her first long leave.

The station did not collapse.

This surprised her more than she admitted.

Nima ran the schedules.

Sen handled the difficult correspondence.

Colm oversaw equipment.

Leth chaired the weekly meeting and ended it on time.

Tomas sent a note saying everything was fine except for the biscuit quality, which he blamed on Mara’s absence.

Mara traveled by ordinary ship.

She wanted to feel distance taking time.

She wanted the discomfort of knowing she could not step back into the office in a moment.

At first, she checked every harbor for green boats.

Then less often.

In a hill town, she spent a week learning to repair a tiled roof.

She was not good at it.

She enjoyed that.

She sent the older Mara a letter.

*I am finally learning the roof.*

The reply reached her two ports later.

*Good. Do not begin with a steep one.*

Mara looked up at the roof she had already repaired.

Then laughed.

---

When she returned, the station had changed the shelves.

No one had asked her.

The new arrangement was better.

She stood in the doorway for several seconds, feeling something embarrassingly like grief.

Nima saw her.

“We kept the old labels.”

“That isn’t—”

“I know.”

Mara looked at the room.

Her room.

Not her room.

A place she had helped build and did not own simply because she remembered its beginning.

She set down her bag.

“Show me where things go.”

Nima smiled.

The tour took ten minutes.

Mara needed a week to stop reaching for the wrong drawer.

No one made a speech about it.

Tomas made several jokes.

That helped.

---

The letter about the older Mara’s death came in winter.

Ada wrote it.

The handwriting was steady until the final paragraph.

Her mother had died at home.

There had been no last journey.

No attempt to bargain.

She had been irritated by the cold tea, pleased with a letter from a former traveler, and asleep when it happened.

Ada enclosed the station bell.

Not the whole boat.

Just the bell.

Mara held it in both hands.

It was worn smooth where fingers had gripped the loop.

Inside the rim, scratched in tiny letters, was a name.

Sella.

Mara had known the name from the one-armed version of herself.

She had never asked whether this Mara had loved the same person.

Now she would not.

The absence of that answer hurt more than she expected.

She put the bell on the desk.

Then she wrote to Ada.

Not advice.

Not a lesson.

Information.

A memory.

An offer to visit if wanted.

---

Ada came in spring.

They walked along Bell Lane.

Jory’s herb had spread between the paving stones.

The bench needed repainting.

Ada carried no mysterious parcel.

Only a bag of clothes.

“I don’t know what to do with the office,” she said.

“Do you want it?”

“Some days.”

“And other days?”

“I want a house with a garden and no one asking whether a door is safe.”

Mara nodded.

“You can choose something else.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

Ada looked at her.

“Not every morning.”

Mara smiled sadly.

“No. Neither do I.”

They sat on the bench.

After a while, Ada said, “Could the office become a training station?”

“Perhaps.”

“With someone else running it.”

“Yes.”

“And I could visit.”

“Yes.”

Ada breathed out.

“I think I needed to hear it from someone who would actually help.”

Mara took out her notebook.

“Then let’s make a list.”

---

The training station opened a year later.

Ada attended the first class.

Not as an instructor.

As a student of gardening during the afternoons, while route training took place elsewhere.

She said she had spent enough of her childhood learning emergency procedures.

Mara approved.

The office window was fixed to show an actual garden during teaching hours.

Colm objected that *fixed* was technically inaccurate.

Nima told him to enjoy the flowers.

The first lesson began with a traveler’s account.

The second with a failed case.

The third with how to say, “We don’t know.”

Only after that did anyone touch a lens.

A student asked whether this was inefficient.

Sen, older now and finally able to make irritation sound gentle, said, “Compared with what?”

No one had a good answer.

They continued.

---

Mara’s father died on a morning with no strange light.

He had been ill for months.

There was time to say some things.

Not everything.

There never seemed to be everything.

At the end, he asked whether the window was open.

It was.

He could hear the harbor.

Tomas sat on one side of the bed.

Mara on the other.

Eli looked at her.

“I came back.”

“Yes.”

“Late.”

“Yes.”

He smiled faintly.

She held his hand.

“You stayed.”

His eyes closed.

She did not know whether he heard the last words.

She said them anyway.

---

For a month after the funeral, Mara avoided the boat.

She knew the difference between a person who had died and another version who might still be alive.

Knowing did not make the desire smaller.

One evening, she stood at the harbor steps with the station bell in her hand.

Nima came and stood beside her.

She did not ask why.

After a while, Mara said, “I could ask it.”

“Yes.”

“It might show me.”

“Yes.”

“It wouldn’t be him.”

“No.”

Mara looked at the dark water.

“It would still be someone.”

Nima nodded.

That was the difficult part.

Not a trick.

Not an empty imitation.

A real person whose existence could become an excuse to refuse the loss of another.

Mara sat on the step.

Nima sat beside her.

They stayed until the lamps came on.

Then Mara put the bell back on the boat.

---

The next day, she took leave again.

This time she stayed home.

She mended clothes.

She slept badly.

She forgot to eat and was fed.

She listened to Leth play.

She argued with Tomas about whether grief made people foolish or merely revealed existing foolishness.

They reached no conclusion.

One afternoon, she found the old note about the fish.

*Back before four.*

She sat at the kitchen table and cried.

There was no instruction in the manual for that.

She was grateful.

---

When Mara returned to work, there was a letter from a station beyond Reedmarket.

A child had arrived alone.

Her name was Sef.

Not Ada.

Not a clue.

Not a promise.

Just a child who needed help.

Mara read the report.

Then she looked at Nima.

“Who’s assigned?”

“Leth and Corin.”

Corin was a new operator, patient and careful.

Mara nodded.

“Do they need anything?”

“Extra route time. A translator. Possibly someone who knows the inland dialects.”

“Arrange it.”

Nima had already done so.

Mara smiled.

For once, being unnecessary felt like relief rather than loss.

She went to the workshop.

There was a housing with a stripped screw.

Some things, apparently, endured.

---

The Two-Count Agreement grew to thirty-seven stations.

Then thirty-six.

One withdrew after a dispute over inspections.

Mara wanted to persuade it back.

Renn, now gray-haired and still formidable, reminded her that membership had to remain voluntary.

“What about the travelers there?”

“They retain access to the review group.”

“What about unsafe equipment?”

“We publish the findings.”

“What if they ignore them?”

“Then we warn people and offer alternatives.”

Mara disliked the limits.

She could see why others had built stronger powers.

Why stronger powers had become traps.

She did not mistake discomfort for proof that the limits were wrong.

They published the findings.

The station returned the following year under new management.

No one called it a victory.

They checked the equipment.

---

The harbor’s children grew up with two kinds of stories.

In one kind, the sea brought back the dead and a girl saved the town.

In the other, an old machine malfunctioned, several people made mistakes, and a group learned how to count arrivals properly.

Mara preferred neither alone.

She visited the school when asked.

The children wanted the walking-on-water part.

She told it.

They wanted the strange boat.

She told that too.

Then she showed them the three-column list.

What we know.

What we think.

What we have not tested.

One child asked whether she had been afraid.

“Yes.”

“Did you stop being afraid?”

“No.”

The child looked disappointed.

Mara considered.

“I got better at knowing what to do while I was.”

That seemed more useful.

Another child asked whether Tomas really argued with crabs.

“Yes,” Mara said. “And he still loses.”

The class laughed.

Tomas, waiting outside, heard about it before supper.

---

The green boat’s paint needed renewing.

This caused an argument.

Mara wanted the same green.

Colm said the original pigment was no longer available.

Olan’s successor offered twelve alternatives.

Tomas said paint was paint.

Nima pointed out that he had spent an entire afternoon selecting a color for his shop door.

He denied it.

The argument lasted three days.

Finally, they chose a slightly bluer green.

The boat accepted it without comment.

The bell rang once after the final coat dried.

Everyone stopped.

Then a gull landed on the bow and immediately ruined part of the finish.

Tomas chased it away.

Mara laughed so hard she had to sit down.

For a few minutes, the boat was only a boat.

---

On the morning Mara turned fifty, she found a loaf of bread on the forward seat.

Warm.

Beside it lay an envelope.

Her name was written on it in handwriting she recognized as her own.

She stood on the steps.

Then she called Nima.

And Sen.

And Tomas, who complained about being summoned before breakfast but came quickly.

They examined the envelope.

No active route.

No coercive signal.

No hidden agreement.

Mara opened it.

Inside were seven words.

*You are allowed to enjoy the morning.*

She read them aloud.

Tomas looked at the bread.

“At last,” he said, “useful correspondence.”

They ate on the quay.

The harbor woke around them.

Shutters opened.

Ropes creaked.

Someone argued with a cart wheel.

No light came on beneath the water.

No bell began counting down.

Mara buttered a piece of bread.

For once, she did not hurry.

---

At eleven, the bell rang.

Not urgently.

A request.

Mara looked toward the boat.

Nima was already standing.

“I’ll take it.”

Mara began to rise anyway.

Nima glanced back.

“Enjoy the morning.”

Mara sat.

Tomas handed her the last piece of bread.

From the boat came voices.

A name.

A question.

A careful explanation.

The work continued without needing her at its center.

She listened to the harbor instead.

---

By noon, Nima had returned with a problem.

Not an emergency.

A problem.

“A station called East Window has found a route marker that predates its building.”

Mara wiped butter from her fingers.

“What sort of marker?”

Nima handed her a sketch.

A circle divided by three lines.

Mara recognized the watermark from the drawing in Leth’s violin.

Her good morning did not vanish.

It simply acquired another part.

“Who’s asked for help?”

“The local operator. And someone who says they remember us.”

“Name?”

“Corren.”

Mara went still.

Corren had died two years earlier.

Not dramatically.

Not lost.

At home, after a long illness, with the roof repaired and the dimensions of every room written down in his own hand.

Nima waited.

Mara looked at the sketch.

“Another route?”

“Probably.”

“Did he ask for anyone specifically?”

“You.”

Tomas stood.

Mara looked at him.

His face was tired, old, and suddenly intent.

“What did he say?” she asked.

Nima consulted the message.

“He says he has finished the room.”

---

They did not open a passage.

They requested verification.

The man at East Window answered questions no one on that route should have known.

The name of Iven’s first cat.

The flaw in the cart brake.

The exact color of the door before the house burned.

All correct.

That did not prove he was their Corren.

It proved he had a closely related history.

Mara had learned to respect the difference.

“What room?” she asked through the handpiece.

The voice was older than the one she remembered.

“Not that room.”

“Then which?”

“The room I should have built.”

Tomas closed his eyes.

Mara waited.

“It has two doors,” Corren said. “Both open from inside.”

“Where does it go?”

“That’s what I need you to help me find out.”

The request was ordinary in its shape.

An unknown thing.

A person asking for help.

A need to decide how much risk was reasonable.

Mara looked at Nima.

“Who’s available for assessment?”

Nima smiled slightly.

“I thought you might ask.”

---

The room at East Window had no hidden staircase.

No brass plate.

No voice behind a wall.

It was a square chamber made of timber, glass, and stone.

Sunlight entered through three high windows.

A bench stood against one wall.

There were indeed two doors.

Both had simple handles.

Both opened inward and outward.

Corren stood in the middle, older by decades than the man Mara had known.

He did not ask to be embraced.

He did not pretend that recognition settled everything.

“Hello, Tom.”

Tomas took off his cap.

“Hello.”

They looked at one another.

Then Tomas inspected the nearest hinge.

“Good work.”

Corren’s mouth trembled.

“Thank you.”

Mara walked the perimeter with Nima.

Sen checked the instrument panel.

Colm examined the foundations.

At the center of the floor was the divided-circle mark.

“What does it do?” Mara asked.

Corren looked at the two doors.

“I think,” he said, “it lets a route refuse to open.”

---

That was not what anyone had expected.

Sen crouched beside the mark.

“Explain.”

Corren took a notebook from the bench.

He had kept measurements, tests, failures, and revisions.

Mara felt affection before she understood the design.

“The old assemblies pull places together,” he said. “They look for what the operator wants.”

“Yes.”

“This one checks whether the receiving place can bear the connection.”

Colm looked up.

“Capacity sensing.”

“More than capacity.”

Corren turned a page.

“It checks whether the route has been invited from both ends.”

Nima leaned closer.

“We already require receiving confirmation.”

“From a person,” Corren said. “This checks the structure.”

“Can a place refuse?”

“I don’t know if that’s the right word.”

Mara looked around the room.

The old house had kept people.

The boat had waited.

The office had followed rules until its walls depended on them.

Whether places wanted things was a question she did not know how to ask well.

“Can it detect a trapped attendant?” she said.

Corren met her eyes.

“That was the first thing I tried.”

---

The first test failed.

Not catastrophically.

The instrument refused to open a passage between the room and the station.

No explanation.

Just a red mark on the panel.

Colm checked the calibration.

Sen checked the invitation.

Nima checked the receiving operator.

Everything appeared correct.

Tomas sat on the bench.

“Perhaps it doesn’t like us.”

Corren frowned at the notebook.

“It opened yesterday.”

“To where?”

“The courtyard.”

“Was anyone on the other side?”

“Yes.”

“Who?”

“The baker.”

Mara looked up.

“Why the baker?”

“He was here.”

“What did you ask?”

“Whether he wanted me to bring the bread inside.”

Mara went to the receiving panel.

“Nima. What did we ask the station?”

“For authorization to establish a test route.”

“And what did it answer?”

“Authorized.”

Corren stared at the divided circle.

Then he laughed softly.

“What?” Sen asked.

“The system isn’t looking for permission.”

Mara felt the difference before she could put it into words.

“It’s looking for a shared purpose.”

---

They tried again.

Not with ceremonial language.

With a specific request.

Mara asked whether the station would receive a crate of test instruments for inspection.

The receiving operator confirmed.

The route opened.

A narrow, steady passage.

No wandering images.

No associative sweep.

The room did not show Mara anyone she missed.

It showed the workbench.

Colm carried the crate across.

The passage closed when the task was complete.

No one spoke for several seconds.

Then Sen said, “We need to test that many more times.”

Mara laughed.

“Yes.”

Corren nodded.

“Yes.”

Tomas stood.

“I’ll fetch lunch.”

The room had not solved longing.

It had not restored the dead.

It had not made travel free of risk.

It had done one thing clearly.

That was enough to begin testing.

---

They spent the afternoon trying to make it fail.

Not by damaging it.

By asking badly.

Vague requests.

Conflicting requests.

An invitation withdrawn halfway through preparation.

A destination described incorrectly.

A receiving operator too tired to complete the confirmation.

The room refused some.

Asked for clarification on others.

Once, it opened to the wrong side of the workbench.

Colm nearly tripped over a stool.

They stopped.

Recorded it.

Changed the floor markers.

Tried again.

At sunset, Mara’s notebook was full.

Corren looked exhausted and happy.

Tomas sat beside him on the bench.

“You built a room that asks questions.”

Corren considered.

“I built a room that waits for answers.”

Mara looked at the two doors.

For the first time in years, she felt the beginning of a discovery without also feeling a warning close around it.

Then she looked back at the notebook.

There were still several things in the third column.

She underlined them.

---

That evening, at the inn in East Window, Mara received a message from home.

A traveler had arrived with no route record.

No one was injured.

The traveler remembered a garden.

A house.

A woman called Sella.

Mara set the paper down.

Nima, across the table, saw her face.

“What is it?”

Mara handed over the message.

Nima read it.

Then she waited.

The name was not a command.

Not a debt.

Not destiny.

It was a name.

Mara had learned that, and learned it again, and would likely have to keep learning it.

“What does the traveler want?” she asked.

Nima checked the second page.

“Help finding her sister.”

Mara nodded.

“Then we start there.”

Outside, the street lamps came on one by one.

Inside, Corren brought his notebook to the table.

He had found another problem in the third test.

Mara made room beside her plate.

“Show me.”

---

The problem was small enough to be dangerous.

When a receiving operator agreed to a task, the room accepted the statement as representing everyone affected by the route.

That was usually reasonable.

Not always.

Colm had discovered it by proposing to move the inn’s kitchen stove without asking the cook.

The cook had objected before anything happened.

Loudly.

Corren had apologized.

Now he pointed to the line in the test record.

“We confirmed the sender and receiver.”

“But not the owner,” Nima said.

“Or the people using it,” Mara added.

Sen, at the end of the table, rubbed his eyes.

“This is how a six-page protocol becomes eighty.”

“Then perhaps the instrument should not decide all of it,” Mara said.

They looked at her.

“It can check the route. We still have to check the request.”

Corren sat back.

He seemed disappointed.

Then relieved.

“I wanted to make it impossible to misuse.”

Mara thought of her younger self, furious at every answer that was not complete.

“I know.”

“Is that foolish?”

“No.”

She turned the notebook toward him.

“But we shouldn’t pretend we’ve done it.”

---

They named the room after no one.

Corren refused.

Mara refused more emphatically.

Tomas suggested *The Room*, which worked until people began asking which room.

Nima proposed *Paired Passage Chamber*.

Sen liked it.

Everyone else thought it sounded like dental equipment.

The cook at the inn, who had become involved against her will, called it the Two-Door Room.

That stayed.

The first public report began:

**This is an experimental route system. It does not replace informed judgment. It has been tested under the conditions listed below and no others.**

Olan’s successor said the wording would make it difficult to sell.

Bera, consulted by letter, wrote:

**Excellent. Let us make it difficult to sell dishonestly.**

Mara pinned that above the workshop bench.

---

When they returned to the harbor, the traveler who knew Sella was waiting.

Her name was Imra.

She was a gardener.

She had crossed a gate during a storm and found herself in the wrong town.

Her sister had followed.

Then the gate had closed.

Mara listened.

“What was your sister’s name?”

“Sella.”

“What does she do?”

“Repairs clocks. Badly, if you ask the clocks. Well, if you ask the owners.”

Mara smiled despite herself.

Imra noticed.

“Do you know her?”

“No.”

The answer was true.

It was also incomplete.

Mara considered.

“I’ve heard of someone with that name.”

“Could be her.”

“Could be.”

Imra studied her.

Then she said, “Please don’t make her into someone else before you find her.”

Mara felt the words land.

“I won’t.”

She hoped she could keep that promise.

She wrote it in the margin, not as a fact.

As a responsibility.

---

They found the gate in a field beyond the northern road.

It had not been there the previous day.

The farmer was annoyed.

It blocked the movement of his sheep and made a faint humming sound at dawn.

Mara apologized.

The farmer said apologies would not move a gate.

Tomas asked whether they could use the field for testing.

The farmer named a price.

Bera would have approved of the negotiation that followed.

The gate stood between two stone posts.

No fence.

No wall.

On one side, the field.

On the other, apparently the same field.

Nima placed a marker.

The instrument showed a weak route.

Colm examined the hinges.

“They’re ordinary.”

“That has never reassured me,” Tomas said.

Imra stood a few paces away.

She did not touch the gate.

Mara asked what she remembered.

“Rain. Sella behind me. I thought she was complaining about the mud.”

“What was she actually saying?”

Imra closed her eyes.

“Wait.”

---

They waited.

Not because the word was a spell.

Because the route had a repeating interval.

Nima found it in the readings.

Every nineteen minutes, the weak signal strengthened.

At the third interval, they saw a garden beyond the gate.

Not the field.

A garden with a broken wall and a house whose windows were all dark except one.

Imra gripped Mara’s sleeve.

“That’s ours.”

Mara kept her voice steady.

“Do you want to call?”

“Yes.”

She called Sella’s name.

No answer.

The route faded.

They waited again.

At the next interval, a light moved in the house.

Someone came to the window.

Mara could not see the face.

Imra called.

The figure stopped.

Then ran from the room.

A minute later, a woman appeared at the far end of the garden.

She was carrying a clock.

Not sensibly.

As though she had picked it up in a moment of alarm and forgotten to put it down.

Imra laughed.

Mara felt an unexpected ache.

The woman shouted something.

The route was too weak to carry it.

Nima adjusted the receiver.

This time the words came through.

“Don’t open it yet!”

---

Sella had spent four days trying to understand the gate.

She had done so with a clock, a notebook, and a growing dislike of the universe.

The route did not connect places at the same rate.

For every nineteen minutes in the field, an irregular amount of time passed in the garden.

Sometimes seconds.

Sometimes hours.

If they opened the gate during the wrong phase, the passage might move before anyone crossed.

Mara listened carefully.

Then she asked Sella to show the notebook.

The woman held it toward the route.

Her handwriting was dreadful.

The observations were excellent.

Colm leaned close.

“She’s right.”

Sella heard him.

“I had hoped so. I’ve been living on pears and spite.”

Imra covered her mouth, laughing and crying.

Mara looked at the clock.

“Can you mark the next safe interval?”

“I can mark a likely one.”

“Good. We’ll verify from this side.”

Sella nodded.

Then she looked directly at Mara for the first time.

“Who are you?”

“Mara Vale.”

The woman frowned slightly.

“Have we met?”

“No.”

“All right. I’m Sella. Please tell me you brought food.”

Tomas lifted the lunch bag.

For once, no one mocked him for it.

---

They did not hurry the crossing.

They synchronized the clocks.

They tested with a rope.

Then a weighted cart.

Then the cart again.

The route held.

Sella crossed with her notebook and the clock.

The sisters embraced so forcefully that the clock struck once between them.

Mara stepped back.

She had imagined this woman without meaning to.

Through the older Maras’ stories.

Through the engraved bell.

Through a name that had gathered tenderness before it belonged to a face.

The actual Sella had mud on her boots, a scrape on her cheek, and immediate opinions about the route equipment.

She was not an answer.

Mara was relieved.

Then frightened by the relief.

Tomas handed out bread.

Sella ate three pieces before speaking again.

“Now,” she said, “who built that receiver? The adjustment scale is backwards.”

Colm stared.

“It is not.”

She took the instrument.

Mara sat on the grass.

Nima sat beside her.

Neither said anything.

Across the field, an argument began that sounded promisingly ordinary.

---

Sella stayed in town while the gate was stabilized.

Imra wanted to return to the garden.

Sella wanted to know why the receiver’s adjustment scale was backwards.

Colm insisted it was conventional.

Sella said convention was not a direction.

Sen mediated badly.

Nima solved the problem by relabeling the control according to observed effect.

Everyone was mildly dissatisfied.

The instrument became easier to use.

Mara found this encouraging.

She and Sella spoke in short intervals.

About clocks.

About gardens.

About the difficulty of obtaining good screws.

Not about other Maras.

Not at first.

Then Mara decided omission was becoming a choice.

She told her.

Not as a confession of destiny.

As information Sella deserved to have.

Sella listened.

When Mara finished, she looked at the bell on the desk.

“And one of me married one of you.”

“Yes.”

“Were they happy?”

“Sometimes. Quite often, for a while.”

Sella nodded.

“That sounds like a marriage.”

Mara laughed.

Then looked down.

Sella waited.

At last she said, “You know I’m not obliged to do that.”

“Yes.”

“Neither are you.”

“I know.”

“Good.”

She picked up the broken clock.

“Now hold this spring before it escapes.”

---

The gate was ready three weeks later.

Imra went home.

Sella went with her to carry tools, check the house, and repair the pantry latch.

Then she returned.

By request.

By a recorded route.

With two counts that matched.

She rented a workshop near Bell Lane.

No one made an announcement.

Tomas noticed anyway.

He said nothing for nearly a day, which Mara considered heroic.

Then he asked whether clock repairs would be discounted for old friends.
