Sella said they would cost extra if the friends had been opening the cases with fish knives.

Tomas looked betrayed.

Mara laughed.

The future did not become certain.

It became someone nearby who might come to supper.

That was enough to make her nervous.

It was also enough to make her set another place.

---

On the first evening Sella came to supper, Mara burned the onions.

Not badly.

Enough to notice.

Sella ate them without comment until Mara apologized for the third time.

“They’re onions,” she said. “Not a treaty.”

Mara put down her fork.

“I’m usually better at ordinary things than this.”

“Are you?”

“Sometimes.”

Sella smiled.

“Good. I’d hate to be the only one with evidence against me.”

They talked until the kettle went cold.

About work.

About Imra.

About Eli.

Mara told the story of the note about fish.

Sella listened without trying to improve it.

When she left, she paused at the door.

“Would you like to come to the workshop tomorrow?”

“For what?”

“I’m repairing a clock that strikes thirteen.”

“Is that unusual here?”

“I don’t yet know.”

Mara smiled.

“Yes.”

Afterward, she stood in the quiet kitchen.

No bell rang.

No page filled itself.

She was glad.

---

The clock that struck thirteen had nothing to do with routes.

It had a damaged gear.

Sella repaired it.

Mara held the spring.

They ate lunch on the workshop step.

A cart passed.

A child dropped an apple.

Tomas shouted at someone for tying a boat badly.

The day continued.

Mara had spent years learning that not every strange thing was a sign.

Now she was learning that not every good thing needed to become one.

She did not put that in the manual either.

Sella would have laughed at it.

Not unkindly.

---

At the station, the Two-Door Room trials expanded.

One chamber was built in Reedmarket.

One aboard a ship.

One in a mountain town where winter regularly cut off supplies.

The results were promising.

Not perfect.

The shared-purpose requirement reduced certain forms of coercive routing but created new questions.

Who could speak for a household?

How specific did a request need to be?

Could a receiving community refuse emergency arrivals?

What if refusal meant death?

The meetings became difficult.

Mara participated.

So did people who had been travelers.

So did station workers who would have to provide beds.

No one found a rule that made every case easy.

They established emergency minimums, appeal paths, capacity sharing, and limits on unilateral control.

Then they tested the procedures against invented cases.

Then real ones.

The real ones were always harder.

Corren, in East Window, kept building doors that opened from inside.

He said that part at least could be made simple.

---

One of the real cases arrived in a storm.

A coastal station requested immediate passage for eighty people.

Its sea wall had failed.

The receiving network had space for fifty.

Perhaps sixty.

Not eighty.

The old system would have held the remainder in continuity.

Safe on paper.

Unresolved in practice.

Mara looked at the capacity table.

Nima was already calling other stations.

Tomas opened the customs hall.

Sella cleared her workshop.

The innkeeper found floor space.

The town council argued for six minutes and then sent blankets.

They received eighty-three.

The extra three were children whose departure count had been taken before they were found.

Nima corrected the record visibly.

No one was left between counts.

The next week was miserable.

Crowded.

Expensive.

Exhausting.

People quarreled.

Someone stole a coat.

Someone returned it.

Three families chose to settle.

The rest moved on as routes became available.

At the review, Mara did not call the response a triumph.

She asked what had nearly failed.

There was a long list.

They began with the blankets.

---

The old network changed.

Slowly.

Not because one station defeated it.

Because too many member stations began asking for the same records.

Because travelers spoke.

Because the fee structure became visible.

Because people inside the organization, including Dalia Fen, decided the existing arrangement could not be defended.

The continuity payments ended in stages.

Some stations closed.

Some merged.

Some found honest ways to remain.

Others resisted until their travelers left.

Dalia visited Mara years after bringing the charter.

She looked older and less polished.

“I thought I was offering help,” she said.

“You were.”

“And a trap.”

“Yes.”

Dalia nodded.

“I disliked you for making me see both.”

Mara smiled faintly.

“I disliked you for making the choice difficult.”

“It was difficult.”

“Yes.”

They drank tea.

No one asked for forgiveness.

They discussed the revised charter.

It was still too long.

Bera had comments.

---

The station became quieter when Mara began taking fewer shifts.

She was not old enough to need to stop.

She was old enough to know that needing to stop was a poor succession plan.

Nima became lead operator.

Sen moved into training and records work.

Colm ran the workshop.

Leth continued to represent travelers and occasionally threatened to resign if meetings exceeded their scheduled time.

Tomas retired three times.

Each retirement lasted less than a month.

Mara kept two regular shifts.

She also kept the right to be told when something genuinely required her history.

Not every difficult case.

Not every uncertain route.

Only the ones where she knew something no one else did.

There were fewer than she expected.

She found other things to learn.

Sella taught her clock repair.

Mara taught Sella the knot.

Neither was patient at first.

They improved.

---

The photograph of Ada and the other Mara moved to the training station.

With Ada’s permission.

Beside it hung photographs of other operators.

Travelers.

Builders.

People who had challenged the rules.

People who had refused to cross.

The wall did not belong only to those who stayed.

Kit sent a picture of the ferry house.

Bera sent one of her family.

Hadrin sent a drawing of Jory’s old hen, made by a grandchild who had never met it.

Mara laughed when she saw it.

Then cried.

The training students learned the names.

Not all of them.

Too many for that.

Enough to understand that the systems had been used by people whose lives continued outside the lesson.

---

On a rainy afternoon, a student asked Mara whether she would do it all again.

The question came during a discussion of the first lighthouse incident.

Mara looked at the diagram.

The lamp.

The mirror.

The wrong assumptions.

“What do you mean by all?”

“Get in the boat. Open the letter. Ask your father.”

Mara thought of the girl on the harbor steps.

The warmth of the bread.

The note in her own handwriting.

“Yes,” she said.

“And everything afterward?”

“I would change quite a lot.”

“Then it wasn’t worth it?”

Mara considered the trap in the question.

Not malicious.

Just a young mind wanting a clean total.

“Some things were worth doing,” she said. “Some things should never have happened. I don’t think one cancels the other.”

The student wrote that down.

Mara almost asked them not to make it sound wiser than it was.

Then she let the sentence stand.

---

The bell rang during the final lesson of that term.

Two strokes.

An incoming request.

Nima answered.

Mara stayed with the students.

For several minutes, they continued discussing a route failure.

Then Nima appeared in the doorway.

“Mara.”

Something in her voice made the room quiet.

“What is it?”

“A boat.”

Mara stood.

“What kind?”

“Blue. White stripe. Patch in the sail.”

Mara’s hand closed around the edge of the desk.

“A sleeping dog?”

Nima nodded.

The students looked between them.

Mara took a breath.

Then another.

“Who’s aboard?”

“No one we can see.”

She went to the harbor.

---

The boat was her father’s skiff.

Or one close enough that every visible detail agreed.

Blue paint.

White stripe.

The patched sail.

A dent in the stern where Mara had once dropped a stone she was trying to use as an anchor.

It rested against the steps where *Tomorrow* had first appeared.

Tomas stood on the quay.

He had come without being called.

Sella stood beside him.

Mara stopped at the top of the steps.

No bell rang.

No light moved beneath the water.

In the boat lay a basket.

Inside the basket were onions, flour, and a spool of blue thread.

Mara closed her eyes.

For a moment, she was twenty again.

Then fifty.

Then simply herself, standing on stone with rain in her hair.

Nima came beside her.

“What do you want to do?”

Mara opened her eyes.

“Check the route.”

“Already started.”

“Thank you.”

Sella took her hand.

They waited.

---

The route record was incomplete.

Not hidden.

Old.

The skiff had passed through several weak crossings over many years.

Objects could drift, Sen explained. Not often. Not predictably.

A boat lost on one route might surface on another long after the people connected to it had died.

The basket was recent.

That was the difficult part.

Colm found a receipt beneath the flour.

Local market.

That morning.

Mara’s name was written on it.

Not in her hand.

In the grocer’s.

She looked toward the market street.

The grocer confirmed the sale.

“Yes, of course. You came in early.”

“I didn’t.”

He frowned.

“Then someone like you.”

“How old?”

“Older.”

“Alone?”

“No.”

Mara’s breath caught.

“With whom?”

The grocer thought.

“A woman. Carrying a clock.”

Sella looked at Mara.

For several seconds, neither spoke.

Then Sella began to laugh.

Mara laughed too, startled and unsteady.

Somewhere, apparently, they had become the sort of people who left upsetting groceries in boats.

“I’m going to have words with us,” Sella said.

---

There was no note.

Mara checked carefully.

Under the basket.

Inside the flour wrapping.

Around the thread.

Nothing.

Only groceries.

Perhaps that was the point.

Perhaps there was no point.

Perhaps two older women had found a lost skiff and decided to return it with provisions because they knew the kitchen would need them.

Mara refused to make certainty out of any of those possibilities.

They secured the boat.

They recorded the arrival.

They left the basket on the kitchen table.

That evening, Sella cooked.

Mara sat nearby, winding blue thread onto a smaller spool.

“Do you think they’re happy?” she asked.

Sella stirred the onions.

“I hope they’re fed.”

Mara smiled.

“That too.”

Outside, rain tapped the glass.

Inside, the kitchen smelled of supper.

The skiff would need repairs.

The station had a meeting in the morning.

There was a letter from Bera to answer.

Mara wound the thread carefully, keeping it from tangling.

---

The repaired skiff became a training boat.

For ordinary sailing.

Mara insisted.

Too many apprentices understood route lines and could not tie a mooring knot.

Tomas approved so strongly that he briefly came out of retirement for the fourth time.

He taught them wind.

Tide.

Weight.

The stubborn fact that a boat could tip because someone stood in the wrong place.

No metaphor was required.

On the first day, Nima got wet.

On the second, Colm lost a hat.

On the third, a student who had been terrified of the sea laughed while holding the tiller.

Mara watched from the quay.

The patched sail filled.

The sleeping dog stretched in the wind.

For a moment, she thought she could hear her father’s laugh.

Memory, she decided.

A good one.

She let it remain that.

---

Tomas’s fifth retirement lasted.

He said he was tired.

No one argued.

He moved to a smaller room overlooking Bell Lane and left the fish shop to a niece who had been running most of it for years.

He still visited the station.

He still criticized the knots.

But he no longer came every morning.

Mara missed him before he was gone.

She told him once.

He looked uncomfortable.

“I’m three streets away.”

“I know.”

“You can visit.”

“I do.”

“Then stop rehearsing.”

She laughed.

He was right in one way and wrong in another.

She brought him soup.

He complained that Jory’s herb had grown too strong.

Then he asked for more.

They sat by the window.

No urgent bell sounded.

For an hour, nothing needed solving.

---

The letter that arrived next was not addressed to Mara.

It was addressed to the station.

The handwriting was unfamiliar.

The paper smelled of smoke.

Inside was a simple request.

*We have built a door that opens from the inside. We do not know what is outside. We would like someone to answer before we open it.*

Below were six signatures.

No map.

No route designation.

Only the divided circle.

Nima brought it to the afternoon meeting.

Mara read it once.

Then passed it on.

“What do we know?” Nima asked.

The room settled into the familiar work.

Not certainty.

Not destiny.

A beginning.

Sen reached for a fresh sheet.

Colm opened the instrument case.

Leth asked who had written the first line.

Sella examined the paper.

Mara took up her pencil.

On the page before them, there was room for questions.

And from somewhere very far away, through a connection not yet strong enough to carry words, they heard someone waiting on the other side of a door.The sound was not knocking.

At first, everyone thought it was.

Three irregular beats. A pause. Two more.

Then Leth asked them to be quiet.

She leaned toward the receiver, eyes closed.

“There are two sounds.”

Colm adjusted the gain.

The first was a dull tapping.

The second was lower, almost beneath hearing.

A scrape followed by a long, hollow note.

Sella took the receiver from him and turned it slightly.

“A wheel,” she said.

“What sort?”

“One with a damaged bearing.”

The hollow note came again.

The tapping continued over it.

Mara looked at the six signatures.

They were written in different hands.

**Roa Senet.**

**Pavel Or.**

**Lian Or.**

**Dessa Venn.**

**Mek.**

**Tavi.**

Beside the last name, someone had drawn a fish.

Mara touched the edge of the paper.

“Can we send sound back?”

“Probably,” Colm said. “Not a passage.”

“Sound will do.”

Nima checked the output marker.

Then Mara spoke.

“This is the harbor station *Tomorrow*. We have your letter. Can you hear us?”

The tapping stopped.

The wheel kept turning.

---

A voice answered after a delay long enough that Mara had begun to prepare a second attempt.

“Yes.”

The speaker was a woman.

Her voice had the careful flatness of someone who was trying not to wake a sleeping person.

“My name is Roa.”

“Mara Vale. Is anyone injured?”

“No.”

A pause.

“Pavel has a burn on his hand. It’s healing.”

“How many people are with you?”

“Six.”

“Are you in immediate danger?”

The wheel scraped.

Roa said, “Not immediate.”

Mara looked at Nima.

Nima made a note.

“What are you concerned about?”

“The water.”

“Rising?”

“No. Stopping.”

Mara waited.

Roa took a breath.

“There’s a wheel that brings it up. The wheel is slowing.”

“Drinking water?”

“And the light.”

Colm leaned closer.

“Does the water power a generator?”

“I don’t know that word.”

“Does turning the wheel make the lamps work?”

“It makes the ceiling work.”

Mara looked around the room.

No one offered an interpretation.

Good.

“Tell us what you can see,” she said.

---

There was a village.

Small.

Nine houses, a meeting room, a workshop, a storehouse, and a shallow canal that ran through the middle.

Above it was a white ceiling.

Not clouds.

Not a sky painted on plaster.

A white surface that brightened and dimmed on a regular cycle.

The village had always been there, as far as Roa knew.

She was thirty-eight.

Her parents had been born there.

So had their parents.

There had once been more people.

Twenty-one, in her childhood.

Illness had taken some.

Others had gone through the old door.

“Where did it lead?” Mara asked.

“We don’t know.”

“Did any return?”

“No.”

“Who opened it?”

“The keeper.”

“Where is the keeper now?”

“Gone.”

“Through the door?”

“Yes.”

The wheel’s hollow note rose and fell.

Roa waited until it passed.

“He took the handle.”

Mara closed her eyes briefly.

“How long ago?”

“Eight years.”

“And you built another door.”

“Pavel did. In the old frame. We found the circle underneath.”

“How did you send the letter?”

“We put it through the gap.”

“What gap?”

“The one that wasn’t there until we finished.”

---

The call weakened before they had all their questions answered.

Roa said the light was fading.

Not because evening had come.

Because the wheel had to be stopped to cool the bearing.

They agreed on a signal for the next attempt.

Three taps.

Then Roa would speak her name.

Mara told her not to open the new door yet.

Roa laughed softly.

“We haven’t agreed whether to.”

“Good,” Mara said. “You don’t have to agree before we talk again.”

The connection ended.

For a moment, the station seemed unusually loud.

Footsteps overhead.

A gull on the roof.

The ordinary sea.

Colm drew a circle around the divided mark.

“An enclosed refuge.”

Sen nodded.

“Possibly.”

“Self-contained hydraulic system.”

“Possibly.”

“Old network?”

Sen examined the paper.

“Older than the network, perhaps.”

Sella listened to the last recorded turn of the wheel.

“The bearing needs replacement.”

“Can you tell what kind?”

“No. But they’ve kept it going far beyond what anyone should ask of it.”

Mara looked at the signatures.

Dessa Venn.

She did not assume.

She sent a copy to Bera anyway.

---

The old catalogues contained seven kinds of enclosed refuge.

None was called a village.

There were emergency rooms for ships.

Long-term shelters for stations under siege.

Quarantine chambers.

Transit accommodations.

Agricultural reserves.

One model designed for children.

And something listed as a **continuity settlement**.

Sen placed that catalogue on the table.

The illustration showed a canal.

Nine houses.

A meeting room.

A workshop.

A storehouse.

Above them, a white ceiling.

The description said the settlement was intended to preserve a small community during prolonged route interruption.

Expected service life: one hundred years.

Recommended occupancy: sixty.

Designed return interval: six months.

Mara looked at the drawing.

“How old is the catalogue?”

“Two hundred and fourteen years.”

No one spoke.

On the next page, in small print, was a maintenance schedule.

The waterwheel bearing required replacement every eighteen months.

Sella rested both hands on the table.

“Then let’s find out what they’ve been using instead.”

---

Roa returned to the connection with Pavel.

He was older than she was, perhaps sixty, with a voice worn rough by smoke.

They could not see him yet.

He described the bearing.

Hardwood.

A sleeve made from pieces of ceramic.

Oil pressed from seeds.

A spring that kept the shaft from shifting too far when the wheel turned.

Sella listened without interrupting.

When he finished, she asked three precise questions.

Pavel answered two.

For the third, he said, “I can measure.”

“Please don’t put your hand near it while it’s turning.”

A pause.

Then he laughed.

“You’ve met mechanics before.”

“Yes.”

“Are you one?”

“On good days.”

“Then you’ll understand why the measurements won’t be exact.”

“I’ll understand. Send them anyway.”

The first image came through while they talked.

Not a clear picture.

A wavering patch of light.

Mara saw a wall.

A dark-haired woman.

An older man with one bandaged hand.

Behind them stood a child holding a wooden fish.

The child looked toward the receiver.

“Is there really water outside your town?”

Mara smiled.

“Yes.”

“How much?”

She considered the harbor, the headland, the horizon.

“More than I know how to count.”

The child turned to Roa.

“I told you.”

---

Tavi was nine.

Mek was fourteen.

Lian was Pavel’s wife.

Dessa was the oldest, though no one agreed on her age.

She remembered a time when there had been a keeper who stayed until someone else was trained.

The last keeper had not done that.

He had discovered a route.

He had said he would return with parts.

Then he had taken the handle.

“Why take it?” Nima asked.

Dessa leaned toward the image.

“Because he thought we would follow.”

“Did you want to?”

“Some of us.”

“Did he explain the risk?”

“He explained that he knew best.”

Dessa’s mouth tightened.

Mara thought of Bera and understood why she had sent the name.

Not because every Venn belonged to one family.

Because certain expressions made kinship seem plausible even before records did.

“What did he leave?” she asked.

Dessa glanced over her shoulder.

“The keys to the storehouse. Three pages of instructions. A promise.”

Tavi held up the fish.

“And this.”

The toy was beautifully made.

Mara felt an anger so familiar that it no longer surprised her.

A gift could be kind.

It could also become the last object someone held while waiting.

---

They sent tools first.

Small ones.

A gauge.

Two measuring strips.

A packet of clean dressings for Pavel’s hand.

The transfer slot was no wider than Mara’s palm.

Colm stabilized it using a paired marker.

Roa received each item and named it back.

No one reached through.

No one tested the opening with a finger.

Tavi asked whether the sea could fit through the slot.

“Not usefully,” Nima said.

“Could a fish?”

“A small one.”

“Alive?”

“We aren’t going to test that.”

“Why?”

“It wouldn’t have agreed.”

Tavi considered this.

Then nodded.

Mara turned away to hide a smile.

The bearing measurements arrived the next morning on a strip of cloth.

Pavel had no paper to spare.

Sella spread it on the workshop bench.

Corren’s surviving apprentice examined the dimensions.

“We can make this.”

“How quickly?”

“Properly?”

“Yes.”

“Two days.”

Mara looked at the worn old housing beside the bench.

No mystery.

No moral dilemma.

A part.

A measurement.

Two days.

She had learned to value such answers.

---

The moral dilemma arrived before the bearing was finished.

It came in Dessa’s voice.

“We don’t all want to leave.”

Mara had expected that.

It did not make the question easier.

“Who does?”

“Mek. Tavi, if there are fish. Roa thinks we should. Pavel wants to see the outside.”

“And you?”

“This is my home.”

“Lian?”

Dessa looked away.

“She says the dead are here.”

Mara wrote nothing for a moment.

Then she asked, “Could we speak with each of you separately?”

“Why?”

“So no one has to decide in front of everyone else.”

Dessa studied her.

“We’ve known one another all our lives.”

“Yes.”

“That ought to make it easier.”

“Does it?”

Dessa sighed.

“No.”

They arranged six conversations.

Tavi’s required a grown person nearby because he wanted one.

Mek asked for no one.

Pavel wanted Lian in the room.

Lian asked Pavel to leave for hers.

He did.

Mara was glad to see it.

---

Mek wanted to leave immediately.

He had never seen a tree taller than a roof.

Never felt rain.

Never heard a person he did not already know until Mara’s voice came through the receiver.

“Can I choose?” he asked.

“There are things we need to understand first.”

“That means no.”

“It means not today.”

He looked away.

Mara recognized the expression.

The anger of someone for whom every delay sounded like another adult deciding that waiting cost nothing.

She leaned toward the image.

“You deserve a clear answer. I don’t have one yet about the route’s safety. I do have one about whether we intend to keep you there.”

He looked back.

“We don’t.”

“Will you promise?”

“We will help you find a safe way out. I won’t promise a date before we know it.”

Mek pressed his lips together.

“That’s what he said.”

“The keeper?”

“Yes.”

Mara did not ask him to trust her instead.

She showed him the work log.

The bearing order.

The route tests.

The names of people responsible.

“Then don’t rely only on what I say. Watch what we do.”

He studied the page.

At last he pointed to a blank line.

“What’s that?”

“Next test.”

“When?”

“Tomorrow morning.”

“Tell me what happens.”

“We will.”

---

Lian did not want to leave the dead.

Her daughter was buried beside the canal.

A grandson.

Her parents.

A friend who had taught her to weave.

The village held nearly everyone she had loved.

“If I leave,” she said, “who will remember where they are?”

Mara sat quietly.

There were answers that would have sounded kind and been useless.

You carry them with you.

Memory is not a place.

The dead would want you to be happy.

She could not know any of those things on Lian’s behalf.

“Would it help to know you could visit?”

“Yes.”

“We don’t know yet whether that will be possible.”

Lian nodded.

“Then I can’t decide yet.”

“That’s all right.”

“What if you need us to decide?”

“We need to know what matters to you before we decide what to build.”

Lian looked surprised.

Then, slowly, she began describing the burial ground.

The stones.

The willow planted from a cutting.

The little channel of water that ran past it.

Mara wrote it all down.

---

Pavel wanted to see a storm.

He was embarrassed by the wish.

“It seems foolish.”

“No,” Sella said.

She had joined the conversation to discuss the bearing.

“I’ve spent my life trying to stop water getting where it shouldn’t,” he said. “I’d like to see it do what it wants.”

Sella smiled.

“We can arrange rain more reliably than a storm.”

“Rain would do.”

He looked down at his bandaged hand.

“Lian thinks I want to leave her.”

“Do you?”

“No.”

“Then tell her what you told us.”

“I have.”

“Again, perhaps.”

He sighed.

Outside the workshop, rain began to strike the station roof.

Sella held the receiver near the open window.

Pavel listened.

At first, he said nothing.

Then he covered his mouth.

Mara looked away.

Some things remained too intimate to watch.

---

Dessa wanted proof that the refuge would not be abandoned if fewer people lived there.

The waterwheel required daily care.

The canal needed clearing.

The ceiling had dark patches.

If Mek left, there would be one fewer pair of strong hands.

If Pavel left, no one else understood the shaft alignment.

If Roa left, who would manage the food?

Her wish to stay was not simple attachment.

It was tied to everyone else’s choices.

Mara recognized the old shape of captivity.

Not a locked door.

A system that made one person’s freedom another person’s disaster.

“We can send maintenance help,” she said.

“For how long?”

“Initially, while we assess the structure.”

“And after?”

“We need a plan that doesn’t depend on everyone staying.”

Dessa leaned back.

“Then make that plan before you offer the children the sky.”

Mara accepted the rebuke.

It was fair.

---

The bearing arrived on the second day.

Sella inspected it.

Pavel checked the measurements through the receiver.

Then they began the installation.

It required stopping the wheel.

The ceiling dimmed.

The canal slowed.

Roa brought lamps.

Mek held the shaft steady with a lever.

Lian kept Tavi away from the work area.

Dessa read the steps aloud from the instructions they had agreed together.

Sella listened.

She did not take over when Pavel used a different order for loosening the retaining bolts.

She asked why.

He explained that one had a damaged thread.

They adjusted the procedure.

The old bearing came free in three pieces.

It should have failed years before.

The new one fitted.

Not perfectly.

A thin shim was needed.

Pavel made it from a strip of metal he had saved for something he could no longer remember.

The wheel turned.

Once.

Twice.

The hollow note vanished.

Above the village, the ceiling brightened.

Tavi shouted.

Everyone else sat down.

---

With the immediate danger eased, they could examine the refuge properly.

The drawings showed something the villagers did not know.

The canal did not merely carry water.

It distributed calibration medium through a closed loop.

The water was separate, though the two systems shared a drive.

The wheel powered circulation, light, ventilation, and structural stability.

That explained why ordinary maintenance failures could threaten more than comfort.

It also explained the dark patches.

The refuge had been losing calibration medium.

Slowly.

For decades.

“How much remains?” Mara asked.

Colm studied the readings.

“Enough for months. Perhaps a year.”

“Can we replenish it?”

“Yes, if we find the reservoir.”

“Where is it?”

He turned the drawing.

“Under the burial ground.”

Mara closed her notebook.

Across the connection, Lian sat beside the canal, mending a sleeve.

The willow moved in air that had never touched an ordinary sky.

Mara asked Colm to check again.

He did.

The answer did not change.

---

They told Lian before telling the village as a group.

Not to make her responsible for the decision.

So she would not hear it first as a technical obstacle in front of everyone.

She listened.

Then asked whether the graves would have to be moved.

“We don’t know yet,” Mara said. “The original access may be somewhere else.”

“And if it isn’t?”

“We will discuss the options.”

Lian looked toward the willow.

“Will you say it’s only earth?”

“No.”

“Will you say the dead can’t care?”

“No.”

She nodded.

Then she cried.

Mara stayed on the connection.

There was nothing more useful to do.

Later, Lian called Pavel into the room.

They spoke quietly for several minutes.

Then she turned back.

“Find the original access first.”

“We will.”

“And if you have to dig, I will be there.”

“Yes.”

---

The original access was beneath a kitchen.

Not beneath the graves.

The catalogue had shown the reservoir’s central chamber, not its service tunnel.

Colm found the second drawing in a maintenance supplement that had been filed under ventilation.

He swore so loudly that Nima came downstairs.

The villagers laughed with relief when they heard.

Lian did not laugh.

She thanked him.

Then asked whose kitchen.

Roa’s.

Roa looked at the floor.

“I wondered why that tile was always warm.”

They lifted it.

Beneath lay a ladder.

At the bottom, a narrow passage.

The air was stale.

No one descended.

Colm sent a small inspection device.

It found the reservoir.

And, beside it, a shelf.

On the shelf were four unopened containers of calibration medium.

Dated nine years earlier.

The year before the keeper left.

Roa stared at the image.

“He had the supplies.”

No one answered.

The wheel turned smoothly in the background.

---

There was a letter beside the containers.

The inspection device could not open it.

They ventilated the passage, tested the air, secured a line, and sent Pavel down with Roa at the ladder.

He returned with the envelope.

It was addressed:

**TO MY REPLACEMENT**

Roa read it aloud.

The keeper had known the refuge was failing.

He had brought supplies.

He had been ordered not to spend them because the settlement was marked for decommissioning.

Its residents were classified as descendants of temporary occupants, not registered travelers.

Responsibility for their relocation was disputed.

He had requested authorization.

It had not come.

He had decided to leave to argue the case in person.

He had taken the handle to prevent an unsafe crossing.

He expected to return within ten days.

At the bottom, in hurried handwriting:

*If I am delayed, use the reserve. Do not wait for permission.*

Roa stopped reading.

Dessa put both hands over her face.

The letter had been in a locked service chamber.

No one knew it was there.

Mara looked at the words.

A good instruction.

A terrible delivery.

Eight years of consequence.

---

They searched for the keeper.

His name was Varo Pell.

No known relation to Jory.

They checked anyway.

He had arrived at a network office eight years earlier.

Filed a petition.

Been referred to another station.

Then another.

His last recorded departure had no matching arrival.

Sen read the route designation.

His face tightened.

“Low Water.”

Mara sat down.

The room was suddenly too full of old doors.

They searched Elian’s records.

The copied cabinets.

The lists of unresolved goods.

The restored names.

Varo Pell appeared in a file marked:

**REPRESENTATIVE CAPACITY DISPUTED**

He had refused to identify himself as a traveler because he was there on behalf of the settlement.

The office had refused to process him as an attendant because he lacked current authorization.

He had remained between categories.

Not physically in a cupboard.

In a waiting room whose route had never been counted as occupied.

Mara called Elian.

She came that evening.

When she saw the name, she sat without being asked.

“I remember him.”

---

Varo had been angry.

Elian remembered that first.

He had shouted.

Broken a cup.

Accused the office of keeping people where it could forget them.

She had placed him in a quiet room until he could be assessed.

“Where?” Mara asked.

Elian opened the old floor plan.

Her finger moved to a section beyond the space where the third wall had disappeared.

“There.”

Sen looked at the map.

“That section was not in the evacuation record.”

“No.”

“Why?”

Elian swallowed.

“I had marked it as administrative.”

Mara felt the old anger rise.

Not fresh.

Worn by use and no less sharp.

“Was he there when the office closed?”

“I don’t know.”

“How many others?”

Elian did not answer.

Mara stood.

She needed to move before she spoke carelessly.

Nima took the question.

“How many rooms were marked administrative but used to hold people?”

Elian looked at the map.

Then began counting.

---

There were eleven.

Some had been empty for years.

Some held records.

Three might have held people.

The uncertainty was not innocent.

Elian knew that.

She said so before anyone asked.

“I used the category because I did not want them in the main ledger.”

“Why?” Nima asked.

“Because unresolved arrivals were reviewed. Administrative delays were not.”

Mara came back to the table.

“Did you think they would leave?”

“I thought someone else would decide.”

“No one else was there.”

“I know that now.”

“You knew it then.”

Elian’s face crumpled.

For a moment, Mara wanted to push harder.

To make the admission complete enough to equal the harm.

Then she saw Nima watching her.

Not warning.

Waiting.

Mara sat.

“We need every room,” she said. “Every name you remember. Every route you omitted. If you need time to write, take it here. If you need someone to question you, we will.”

Elian nodded.

She took the pencil.

Her hand shook.

She wrote.

---

The bridge drawing had not shown a door for years.

Now Mara placed it on the table.

Sen set a marker beside it.

Elian stood across from them.

“Can you find the waiting room?” Mara asked.

“I can remember it.”

“That is not the same thing.”

“No.”

“Can you describe it without asking the instrument to make it?”

Elian closed her eyes.

A narrow room.

Three chairs.

A window with frosted glass.

A clock that did not move.

A door that could be opened only from the corridor.

Mara’s hands tightened.

Corren’s Two-Door Room had been built for exactly this kind of failure.

Nima brought the portable paired marker.

They would not open a route based only on Elian’s memory.

They needed a response from the other side.

Sen sent a signal.

No answer.

Again.

Nothing.

On the third attempt, the bridge drawing darkened.

A tiny square appeared beneath the fourth arch.

Then, very faintly, someone knocked.

---

The person who answered was not Varo.

Her name was Iset.

She had been waiting eleven years.

She did not believe them when they said they could hear her.

She accused them of using another recording.

Mara asked what would help verify a live conversation.

Iset gave her a number.

A complicated one.

Mara asked Nima to write it down.

Then Iset asked them to subtract another.

They did.

She asked for the result backward.

Tomas, who had come to sit in the doorway despite his retirement, muttered that this seemed unfair before supper.

Iset heard him.

For several seconds, she said nothing.

Then she laughed.

Not happily.

But with surprise.

“No recording would say that.”

Tomas looked pleased.

Mara leaned toward the receiver.

“Is anyone else with you?”

“A man. Varo. He’s asleep.”

“Is he well?”

“No.”

Mara called for the doctor.

---

They established a view.

The waiting room was worse than Elian remembered.

The chairs had been dismantled for parts.

A blanket hung over the frosted window to conserve heat.

The clock still showed the same time.

Varo lay on the floor.

Iset had kept him alive with water and food delivered through a slot by a mechanism that had never asked whether anyone remained.

There was a third cup.

Mara asked about it.

Iset looked away.

“He died four winters ago.”

“There’s no winter in the room,” Elian whispered.

Iset turned toward the sound.

For the first time, her face changed completely.

“You.”

Elian stepped back.

Mara moved between her and the receiver, though the gesture was useless.

“Elian is here to help locate the route,” she said. “You do not have to speak with her.”

Iset stared.

Then she said, “Take her away.”

Elian left.

No argument.

Nima closed the door behind her.

Mara returned to the connection.

“We will continue without her.”

---

The rescue took six hours.

Not because the opening was large.

Because the room had no registered departure point.

It had been designed not to let its occupants leave until a decision came from outside.

The decision had never come.

Sen and Nima reconstructed a route mark from the food-delivery slot.

Colm built an adapter.

The doctor guided Iset through checking Varo’s breathing.

Sella kept the failing clock mechanism from interfering with the room’s timing field.

Tomas made tea no one remembered to drink.

Mara remained at the receiver.

She told Iset what was happening.

When progress stopped, she said so.

When a test failed, she said so.

When they had to wait for a component, she explained why.

Iset listened without gratitude.

Mara did not need gratitude.

She needed Iset to know when the floor beneath her would change.

At dusk, the passage opened.

Iset came first.

She carried the third cup.

Varo followed on a stretcher.

Departure count: two living travelers.

One set of remains requested for later recovery.

Arrival count: two.

Mara read the numbers aloud.

Then she put down the pencil because her hand would not stop shaking.

---

Varo survived.

For three days, that was all the doctor would say.

Roa listened through the receiver.

She did not ask to see him while he was unconscious.

Dessa did.

When the image appeared, she stared at his face.

“He got old.”

“So did we,” Pavel said.

Dessa turned away.

The anger in the refuge had nowhere simple to go.

The keeper had left.

He had meant to return.

He had hidden the supplies unintentionally.

He had taken the handle intentionally.

He had been trapped.

He had still made decisions for them without asking.

Mara did not try to settle the account.

She told them what they knew.

She distinguished evidence from explanation.

Then she asked what they needed next.

Roa said, “The reservoir.”

So they returned to the work.

---

Iset moved into Bera’s old room.

She checked the windows herself.

Then the door.

Then the latch.

She asked whether there were any other locks she could not see.

Mara said no.

Iset asked Colm to confirm.

He did.

She asked Nima whether the answer would change if she refused to give more testimony.

“No.”

“If I refuse to see Elian?”

“No.”

“If I leave?”

“You may leave.”

Iset sat on the bed.

The third cup stood on the table.

“What if I take this?”

“It’s yours.”

She looked at it.

“It wasn’t his either. It belonged to the room.”

Mara waited.

Iset closed her hand around it.

“I’m taking it.”

“All right.”

Only then did she begin to cry.

---

Elian requested that her own case be reopened.

The review group agreed.

She offered to surrender her work, her room, and her place in town.

Mara read the statement.

It was full of renunciation.

She took it back to Elian.

“What would surrendering your room do for Iset?”

Elian looked exhausted.

“Nothing.”

“What would disappearing do?”

“Nothing.”

“Then don’t make it the answer.”

Elian stared at the paper.

“What is the answer?”

“You need to participate in finding everyone else. You need to give a full account. The review group will decide what restrictions are necessary. Iset and the others will decide whether they want anything from you.”

“And you?”

Mara took a breath.

“I don’t know what I want from you today.”

Elian nodded.

For once, neither sought to make that smaller.

---

The other two administrative rooms were empty.

One contained records that should have been transferred decades earlier.

The other contained a coat, a pair of shoes, and a note scratched into the wall.

No name.

Only:

*I went through the window.*

The frosted pane was broken.

Beyond it, no route remained.

They recorded the room.

The objects.

The dimensions.

The mark on the wall.

They did not call the search complete.

Mara added a line to the missing list.

**Unknown traveler. Administrative room three. Possible unregistered departure.**

Then she sat alone for a while.

Sella found her at the desk.

“Home?”

“Soon.”

Sella looked at the open ledger.

She did not close it.

She brought a chair.

They sat together until Mara could put the pencil down.

---

The refuge’s reservoir was repaired.

The ceiling brightened evenly.

The canal flowed.

For the first time in years, the village had enough water to fill the public basin.

Tavi splashed in it until Lian told him he was wasting none of the water but all of everyone’s patience.

Mek laughed.

Dessa sat beneath the willow.

Roa opened the storehouse and made an inventory that was not governed by fear of the next shortage.

Pavel tested the new bearing.

Sella sent him a spare.

Then a second spare.

He wrote back that she was becoming excessive.

She replied that this was a recognized professional trait.

The village was no longer in immediate danger.

That made the next decisions possible.

It did not make them easy.

---

Mek left first.

Not permanently.

He enrolled in a school near the harbor and stayed with a family who had agreed to support him.

He visited the refuge twice a week.

At first, every return was full of stories.

Then the stories became harder to tell.

Not because he loved the village less.

Because his life outside contained references no one there shared.

He noticed.

So did Roa.

They began a regular exchange.

Visitors from the harbor came to learn the refuge’s seed-growing methods.

The refuge had preserved varieties lost elsewhere.

Pavel taught mechanical improvisation.

Lian taught weaving.

Dessa told stories that contradicted the catalogue’s account of the settlement.

The village had not merely been preserved.

It had made things.

Changed things.

Become a place.

Mara wrote that into the station record.

Not as praise.

As correction.

---

Tavi saw the sea on a windy morning.

He stood between Roa and Nima at the top of the harbor steps.

For a long time, he said nothing.

Mara had expected delight.

Or fear.

She had not expected anger.

“It’s too much,” he said.

Roa knelt beside him.

“What is?”

“All of it.”

The horizon.

The gulls.

The boats moving in different directions.

The water without a wall.

He covered his ears.

They took him inside.

No one told him to be brave.

No one reminded him how much he had wanted fish.

Later, in a quiet room, he asked whether he could try again from farther away.

So they went to a window on the upper floor.

The sea was smaller in a frame.

He watched for ten minutes.

Then twenty.

The next day, he went to the quay.

On the fourth day, he touched the water.

On the fifth, he asked about boats.

Mara did not say, “At last.”

She showed him the blue skiff.

---

Lian stayed.

Pavel stayed with her, though he visited the harbor whenever rain was forecast.

Dessa stayed too.

Roa divided her time.

The refuge received a new service door with two handles, both inside.

No keeper could remove them.

The maintenance plan was shared with three stations.

Supplies were stored where residents could reach them.

The settlement’s status was changed from temporary accommodation to an independent community.

That required more forms than anyone enjoyed.

It also required listening to Dessa explain, repeatedly, that a place did not stop being a home because someone had once expected it to be temporary.

At the signing, she wore a blue ribbon in her hair.

Mara complimented it.

Dessa looked pleased.

“Bera sent it.”

The family connection, when they finally traced it, was distant.

Neither woman cared.

They had become friends by then.

---

Varo returned to the refuge after his recovery.

He did not bring a handle.

He brought the old keys.

He placed them on the meeting-room table.

“I should have told you where the reserve was.”

“Yes,” Roa said.

“I should not have decided alone.”

“No.”

“I thought I was protecting you.”

Dessa looked at him.

“We know.”

He waited for more.

There was no more.

Not that day.

Later, Pavel asked him to help inspect the ventilation.

Lian offered him supper.

Mek refused to speak with him.

Tavi showed him the wooden fish, then put it away.

These were not contradictory responses.

They belonged to different people.

Varo stayed one night.

Then returned to the harbor.

He would visit again if invited.

Mara thought that was a reasonable beginning.

---

Iset did not remain in town.

She had a sister somewhere in the western routes.

The search took four months.

During that time, she worked in Sella’s shop.

Not because she had to earn her room.

Because small mechanical failures were easier to bear than large human ones.

She was good at cleaning clock movements.

Patient.

Exact.

She did not like bells.

Sella removed the chime from the clock nearest her bench.

When the route to her sister was found, Iset asked to inspect it alone.

Mara arranged a private viewing.

Afterward, Iset sat on the quay for an hour.

Then she returned.

“Yes,” she said.

They prepared the passage.

Before leaving, she handed Mara the third cup.

“Find his name.”

“We’re trying.”

“I know.”

She went through.

Mara placed the cup in the records room, with a label that did not pretend they knew more than they did.

The search remained open.

---

The name came from a laundry list.

Not a route ledger.

Not Elian’s memory.

An old service invoice for blankets delivered to administrative room two.

Three occupants.

Varo Pell.

Iset Narin.

**Jalen Ost.**

Mara read the name aloud.

Then again.

Jalen Ost had been a musician.

He had missed a connection while traveling to his daughter’s wedding.

They found the daughter.

She was older now than Jalen had been when he died.

She listened to the account without interrupting.

Then she asked for the cup.

Mara sent it with the remains, a complete record, and an offer to answer questions whenever she wished.

Months later, a letter returned.

No forgiveness.

No thanks.

A request for changes to the complaint procedure.

Mara read it at the next meeting.

Every requested change was considered.

Most were adopted.

The others received explanations.

Jalen’s daughter argued two of them successfully.

Her name was added to the review group.

---

The village under the white ceiling acquired a name of its own.

It had always been called Home.

That was enough internally.

It became difficult when writing addresses.

Suggestions included Wheelstead, Canal End, and New Light.

Dessa rejected New Light because the light was old and the word made it sound as though nothing had existed before the repairs.

Tavi suggested Fish.

No one understood his reasoning.

Mek proposed Inside.

Pavel said that would make every letter confusing.

Lian finally suggested Willowbank.

There was a willow.

There was a bank.

No one found a reason to object.

The first official letter from Willowbank invited the harbor station to a harvest meal.

Mara went.

So did Sella.

They brought bread.

Pavel listened to Sella describe a storm he had missed.

Tavi showed Mara a drawing of the sea.

It was small.

Framed.

Entirely his own.

---

At the meal, Dessa asked Mara whether there were more refuges.

Mara set down her cup.

“Yes.”

“How many?”

“We don’t know.”

“Like ours?”

“Possibly.”

“Still occupied?”

“Possibly.”

Dessa nodded.

Then she handed Mara a packet of seeds.

“For the next one.”

Mara looked at them.

“You don’t have to supply every rescue.”

“No. But we have seeds.”

It was such a simple answer that Mara smiled.

She accepted the packet.

Back at the station, she placed it beside the catalogue of seven refuge types.

The work had grown again.

She did not take all of it onto her own desk.

She called a meeting.

---

Nima led the refuge search program.

Mara advised.

That distinction had to be defended more than once, mostly against Mara’s own habits.

Nima learned to ask, “Do you have information, or do you have a preference?”

Mara disliked the question.

It was useful.

Sometimes she had information.

Sometimes only the memory of having once been the person who decided.

She learned to say which.

The program began with catalogues, supply records, and maintenance routes.

It did not begin with opening every door they found.

Three empty shelters were located.

One had been properly decommissioned.

One contained a thriving colony of moths.

One had become a library.

The librarian objected to being classified as an unresolved arrival.

They corrected the record.

Then asked whether she knew of other refuges.

She did.

---

The library stood inside a railway carriage that had not moved in seventy years.

Its windows looked onto a desert.

The door opened into a city.

The undercarriage rested, according to the technical readings, nowhere at all.

The librarian’s name was Ves.

She had been born in the carriage.

Her mother had inherited it from a teacher.

The teacher had acquired it from a station that no longer existed.

Ves did not consider this unusual.

She was more concerned that the visitors might bring damp near the books.

Mara respected that.

Nima explained the search.

Ves listened.

Then led them to a shelf labeled **PLACES INTENDED TO BE TEMPORARY**.

There were sixty-three volumes.

Colm made a low sound.

Ves looked at him sharply.

“No bending the spines.”

He straightened.

They began with the index.

---

One refuge was listed as an orchard.

Another as a hospital.

Another as a ship’s lower deck.

A fourth had no description, only a warning:

**DO NOT RESTORE EXTERNAL ACCESS WITHOUT LOCAL REVIEW.**

Mara read the line twice.

“Why?”

Ves took down the corresponding volume.

The refuge had been built to shelter a community from an invading army.

Its residents had sealed the route voluntarily.

Their descendants had renewed the decision every ten years.

No maintenance request had been received for a century.

“That doesn’t mean they need rescue,” Nima said.

“No,” Mara agreed.

They wrote an inquiry.

Not a summons.

Not an announcement of inspection.

An inquiry.

The first sentence asked whether contact was wanted.

They left the letter with Ves, who knew the old courier channels.

Then they returned home.

For once, waiting was the correct action.

---

The hospital answered first.

It was not one of the refuges they had contacted.

It had heard through another station.

Its letter contained no plea.

Only a request for a component no longer manufactured.

Colm examined the specification.

“We can make it.”

“How quickly?” Nima asked.

“Properly?”

Mara smiled from the other side of the table.

“Yes,” Nima said.

“Four days.”

They sent the component.

The hospital sent a report.

No one traveled.

No dramatic passage opened.

A machine kept working.

Thirty-two patients continued receiving care.

Mara entered the correspondence in the archive.

The case occupied less than a page.

She made sure the outcome was recorded anyway.

---

The orchard took longer.

Its trees had outlived the people who planted them.

One caretaker remained.

She was ninety-seven and did not want to leave.

She wanted apprentices.

The search program could help with that.

Two young growers from Willowbank volunteered.

They brought seeds from Dessa.

The caretaker met them with a pruning knife and very little patience.

Within a month, all three were complaining about one another in affectionate detail.

Mara read the letters with pleasure.

Not every isolated place needed emptying.

Some needed company.

Some needed spare parts.

Some needed to be left alone after a clear answer.

The program learned this unevenly.

Its records improved.

---

The sealed community replied after eight months.

The answer was no.

No external access.

No visitors.

No inspection.

They thanked the station for asking.

They requested copies of current medical and agricultural texts, if those could be transferred without opening a person-sized route.

Nima arranged the transfer.

Some members of the network objected.

They argued that a refusal might conceal coercion.

It might.

It might also be a refusal.

Mara listened to the debate.

They agreed on a limited follow-up offering private channels for individuals who wanted information, without forcing a communal opening.

The community accepted.

No one used the channel for six months.

Then one person asked a question about a crop disease.

They answered.

Trust, Mara thought, sometimes looked disappointingly like waiting for a question about mildew.

She did not say this at the meeting.

She sent the agricultural text.

---

The blue skiff went missing one autumn night.

Mara was woken by a knock at the kitchen door.

Tavi stood outside with Mek.

Tavi was older now, but not old enough to disguise panic.

“We took it.”

Mara held the lamp higher.

“Where is it?”

“On the mud below the eastern pier.”

“Is anyone hurt?”

“No.”

“Anyone still aboard?”

“No.”

She woke Sella.

Then called the harbor watch.

The skiff was recovered at dawn.

A cracked plank.

A damaged oar.

No one lost.

Tavi waited in the chandlery, white-faced.

Mek sat beside him.

Mara came in carrying the broken oar.

She set it down.

“Tell me.”

They did.

They had wanted to see the harbor at night.

They had not checked the tide.

They had argued over the tiller.

The boat had grounded.

Mara listened.

Then she said, “You will help repair it.”

They nodded.

“You will also take more sailing lessons.”

They nodded again.

“And next time you want to see the harbor at night, ask.”

Tavi looked up.

“Would you have said yes?”

“With someone experienced.”

He swallowed.

“Oh.”

Mara thought of herself climbing into a green boat while Tomas told her not to.

She did not mention it.

Not yet.

---

During the repairs, she told them.

The whole beginning.

Not the school version.

The impatience.

The fear.

The way she had climbed down partly because Tomas had told her not to.

Tavi looked relieved.

Mara shook her head.

“That does not make your decision wise.”

“I know.”

“Does it make yours wise?” Mek asked.

Mara considered.

“No. It makes it mine.”

They worked in silence for a while.

Then Mek said, “That’s not a very useful answer.”

“No,” Mara admitted. “Here’s a useful one: curiosity does not excuse failing to check the tide.”

Tavi laughed.

Mara handed him the sanding block.

The sleeping-dog patch needed mending too.

She found blue thread.

There was usually some now.

---

Tomas died in his sleep in the room overlooking Bell Lane.

The morning was clear.

Mara had expected, foolishly, that the world would make some unusual effort.

It did not.

The fish shop opened late.

The station closed for the afternoon.

Someone left a boat hook beside his chair at the funeral.

Nima placed the laminated checklist next to it.

Mara could not look at both together without crying.

Sella held her hand.

Afterward, they walked to the harbor.

The ropes were badly tied on a visiting boat.

Mara stopped.

Then she fixed them.

Not as a ritual.

Because they were badly tied.

That would have pleased him more.

---

The station inherited Tomas’s boat hook.

It was hung near the door.

Not as a weapon.

Not as a symbol.

As a useful tool.

A small label beneath it read:

**ASK BEFORE USING ON OPTICAL EQUIPMENT.**

Nima had written it.

Mara laughed when she saw it.

Then cried again.

Grief had become familiar without becoming easier.

She knew the routes existed.

She knew there might be a Tomas elsewhere.

She did not go looking.

This was not because she had become wiser than longing.

It was because longing no longer got to make every decision alone.

---

At sixty, Mara gave the station key to Nima.

The office had already recognized her for years.

The key still mattered.

They stood on the harbor steps.

No ceremony had been planned.

People gathered anyway.

Mara placed the small key in Nima’s hand.

“It may open things I haven’t found.”

Nima looked at it.

“That is not reassuring.”

“No.”

“Any other advice?”

Mara thought.

“Read the underside.”

Nima smiled.

“I expected more.”

“So did I, once.”

They embraced.

The bell rang.

Once.

Not approval, Mara reminded herself.

Not unless they knew that.

A sound.

A familiar one.

She let it be enough.

---

Retirement was not a room Mara entered all at once.

She took work home.

Then brought it back.

She offered advice no one had requested.

Then learned to ask first.

She missed the urgency.

She slept better without it.

Sella was patient for three weeks.

Then she gave Mara a broken clock and said, “You need something that cannot be solved by calling a meeting.”

Mara examined the mechanism.

“It could be.”

“Don’t.”

She did not.

The repair took a month.

It was the happiest she had been while being repeatedly wrong.

---

The station continued to send her difficult letters.

Fewer now.

Mostly questions about events she remembered.

She answered carefully.

When memory was uncertain, she said so.

Once, a historian asked her to describe the exact moment she decided to found the station.

Mara wrote back:

*I don’t think there was one.*

The historian asked for something more vivid.

Mara considered the bread.

The letter.

The light under the sea.

Then she wrote:

*There was a list that needed another column.*

The historian seemed dissatisfied.

Sella laughed when Mara told her.

“You could have given them the walking-on-water part.”

“They already have it.”

“Then perhaps they’ll have to learn to use the list.”

---

Bera visited in summer.

She was old enough now that travel tired her.

Perrin’s granddaughter came with her.

The little furious-looking baby from the photograph was a grown woman with a calm voice and a talent for arranging pillows exactly where Bera said she did not need them.

Mara and Bera sat by the open windows.

For a while, they discussed accounts.

Then family.

Then the dead.

At last Bera said, “Do you remember the first day?”

“Yes.”

“The bread on the gatepost.”

“Yes.”

“I thought it was a trick.”

“I know.”

“No. You didn’t.”

Mara looked at her.

Bera smiled.

“You hoped it wasn’t.”

“Yes.”

“I’m glad you brought it anyway.”

Mara reached for her hand.

They sat without speaking until Sella brought tea.

---

The reply from the sealed community changed after twelve years.

Their review assembly had voted to allow a small delegation to visit.

Not because they had abandoned their reasons for caution.

Because they wanted to compare them with the world as it now was.

Nima led the delegation.

Mara declined an invitation.

She wanted to go.

She also wanted the work to belong to people whose histories were not always tied to hers.

Nima sent detailed letters.

The community had built astonishing water systems.

Its political arguments were ordinary.

Its children were curious.

Some elders feared outside trade.

Some young people feared being trapped by everyone else’s fear.

No one was entirely wrong.

Mara read the reports by the kitchen window.

Then she returned to the clock on the table.

It was good to know without having to direct.

---

The unknown traveler from administrative room three was found through a shoe.

A repair mark on the sole matched a cobbler’s registry in a town beyond the western routes.

The traveler had arrived there injured, years before.

He had given a name.

Then changed it.

Then married.

Then died.

His family knew he had come through a window.

They had thought it a figure of speech.

The station sent the record.

The family sent a photograph.

The man stood in a garden with three children.

He looked tired.

He also looked happy.

Mara studied the picture.

Not everyone had been rescued.

Some had escaped.

Some had made lives without the station ever knowing.

She was glad.

Nima closed the missing entry only after the family confirmed the identification.

The line had been open for nearly twenty years.

---

One winter evening, the green boat’s office produced a new drawer.

Nima called Mara.

Mara considered refusing.

Then curiosity won, as it often had.

The drawer was beneath the second desk.

Its label read:

**UNASKED QUESTIONS**

Sella came too.

“I don’t trust that,” she said.

“Neither do I,” Mara replied.

Nima checked for active routes.

None.

They opened it.

Inside was a single blank notebook.

No warning.

No future handwriting.

Just paper.

On the first page, in small printed letters:

**A place to keep them. Not an obligation to answer.**

Mara sat back.

For once, the office seemed to have learned something.

Nima picked up a pencil.

“What’s first?”

Sella said, “Where does the stove pipe go?”

They all laughed.

Nima wrote it down.

---

The stove pipe led to a chimney on an abandoned station platform.

The answer took three weeks.

It was found by an apprentice who had no reverence for old mysteries and considerable experience cleaning flues.

She followed the soot.

Mara delighted in this.

Sen, who had wondered about the pipe for decades, was embarrassed.

Then delighted too.

The platform stood in a valley where trains no longer ran.

Its chimney was the only part still maintained by the boat.

The apprentice cleaned it.

The stove drew better.

A mystery ended in reduced smoke.

Mara thought that was an excellent outcome.

They marked the question answered.

Then added three more.

---

The second question concerned the warm bread.

Who had baked the first loaf?

The one in the green boat on the morning Eli returned.

The older one-armed Mara had said a bakery in a city no longer attached to that morning.

No address.

No name.

They searched only when there was time.

Eventually, a route log identified the city.

The bakery still existed.

Its records did not.

The current baker listened to the inquiry and asked whether the loaf had contained fennel.

Mara tried to remember.

Warmth.

Crust.

Yeast.

Fear.

She could not recall fennel.

The baker shrugged.

“Then it may have been us.”

He sent a loaf.

It arrived cold.

Mara warmed it in the oven.

Sella cut it.

There was fennel.

Perhaps.

They ate it with soup.

The question remained open.

That was acceptable.

---

The third question came from Tavi.

He was an experienced sailor now.

He worked both ordinary routes and optical ones.

He had learned to love the sea without needing to make it smaller.

His question was simple.

“What happens if a boat reaches a place before the place has been built?”

Mara looked at him.

“Why?”

He placed a chart on the table.

At its edge was a harbor.

Their harbor.

But the quay was different.

The customs house was absent.

The lighthouse stood intact on the headland.

On the water, in careful green ink, was a little boat.

Beside it, a date.

Eleven years before the lighthouse burned.

Mara sat down.

Sella leaned over the chart.

Nima looked from the date to Mara.

“Is this a route proposal?”

Tavi shook his head.

“It’s a received request.”

“From whom?”

He handed her the second page.

The signature was precise.

**Iven. Keeper of the North Light.**

---

Mara did not answer immediately.

The old impulse was there.

Of course it was.

Warn Iven.

Stop the fire.

Stop Eli.

Save the missing lane.

Save the family.

Save the years.

She looked at the chart until the ink blurred.

Then she asked Tavi, “What does she want?”

He read the message.

“A technical consultation. She’s found an instrument beneath the lantern room. She says it isn’t behaving according to the maker’s description.”

Mara closed her eyes.

Sella sat beside her.

Nima waited.

When Mara opened her eyes again, she said, “We need to know whether this is our past.”

Sen, summoned by the unusual route designation, studied the chart.

“We may never be able to prove that.”

“Could answering change what happened here?”

“I don’t know.”

“Could refusing leave another town to suffer the same thing?”

“Yes.”

Mara looked at the station’s procedures.

They had been written for uncertain routes.

Not this uncertainty.

Still, some parts held.

Ask what is needed.

Do not claim what you cannot know.

Count at both ends.

She took a fresh page.

“Then we answer the technical question first.”

---

Iven’s voice was younger than Mara remembered.

Strong.

Impatient.

Not bent by age or secrecy.

She sounded like someone who had asked for a manual and received a committee.

“I need the calibration specifications.”

Nima provided them.

Iven checked the figures.

“There’s been a modification.”

“What kind?”

“A secondary mirror. The previous keeper called it a focusing aid.”

“Can you remove it safely?”

“Yes.”

“Do that before another test.”

Iven paused.

“You sound certain.”

“About the mirror’s effect, yes.”

“And the rest?”

“We need more information.”

“Good. I dislike people who are certain about everything.”

Mara almost laughed.

Tomas would have had something to say about that.

She missed him sharply.

Iven removed the mirror.

The signal steadied.

“Better,” she said.

Then, after a pause:

“There are two young men here who have been using it without permission.”

Mara gripped the edge of the desk.

Nima glanced at her.

“What have they brought through?” she asked.

“Medicine.”

Sen lowered his eyes.

Mara took a slow breath.

They were too late for the beginning.

Perhaps not for what came next.

---

They told Iven about the return assembly.

The maker’s mark.

The reservoir.

The manual attendant button.

The need to record every departure and arrival.

They did not tell her everything at once.

Not because they wished to hide it.

Because she had a live instrument, two frightened young men, and a town with people recovering from fever.

The immediate task was safety.

Iven listened.

Then she asked who they were.

Nima gave the station designation.

Sen gave his credentials.

Mara remained silent.

Iven noticed.

“There’s someone else.”

“Yes,” Nima said.

“An operator?”

“An experienced one.”

“Why isn’t she speaking?”

Mara closed her eyes.

Then leaned toward the receiver.

“Because this route is personally difficult for me.”

Iven was quiet.

“That is a sensible reason.”

“Thank you.”

“Do you know the instrument?”

“Yes.”

“Then tell me what I should not do.”

Mara looked at the original lamp’s record.

“Do not break the housing.”

---

Iven asked why.

Mara explained.

Precisely.

No prophecy.

No story of her own childhood.

The housing contained the alignment field.

Breaking it while passages remained occupied could collapse nearby structures into the active route.

The lens would survive.

The danger would not.

Iven took notes.

Mara could hear the pencil.

Then another voice spoke in the background.

Young.

Familiar.

Her father’s.

“What if someone is already lost?”

Mara’s hand went cold.

Nima reached for the mute control.

Mara shook her head.

Iven repeated the question into the receiver.

“What kind of lost?” Mara asked.

A pause.

Then Eli’s voice, closer.

“Dead.”

Mara stared at the desk.

Her mother had died.

The route’s history was not identical in every detail she had assumed.

Or the date conversion was wrong.

Or this was a moment she had never understood.

Nima waited beside her.

Mara took a breath.

“The instrument can find someone like the person you lost,” she said. “That does not mean it can return the person who died.”

Silence.

Then young Eli said, “How do you know?”

Mara looked at Sella.

At Sen.

At the bell.

“Because people have tried,” she said.

---

The question did not end his longing.

Mara knew it would not.

He asked about differences.

About lives.

About whether a person who had not died was less real.

“No,” Mara said.

“Then why shouldn’t I look?”

Iven made a sharp sound.

Mara lifted a hand, though no one at the other end could see it.

“You can care about someone you find,” she said. “But you must not make them responsible for being the person you lost.”

“And if they want to come?”

“Then the route must be safe. The choice must be theirs. And you still need to consider who is waiting for you where you are.”

A long silence.

Then he said, very quietly, “I have a daughter.”

Mara closed her eyes.

“I thought you might.”

Behind her, Sella took her free hand.

The line remained open.

No one hurried it.

---

They did not solve Eli’s grief.

They gave Iven a safe shutdown procedure.

They sent a copy of the old charter.

They arranged a follow-up with operators who were not personally tied to the route.

Mara requested that her role be limited.

It was the hardest useful decision she had made in years.

Nima accepted it without praising her.

That helped.

At the end of the call, Iven asked whether Mara wanted to leave a message.

Mara considered all the things she could say.

Stay.

Go home.

Ask your daughter what she needs.

Do not make her wait nine years.

Do not make her learn your name as an absence.

She could not separate warning from wanting.

At last she said, “Tell them to read the underside.”

Iven laughed.

“That sounds like something I should have done already.”

“Yes,” Mara said.

The connection closed.

She sat very still.

Sella stayed beside her.

---

Nothing vanished.

The station remained.

The harbor remained.

Mara remembered the same childhood.

The same years.

The same father returning across the water.

No new past replaced the old one.

She was relieved.

Then disappointed.

Then ashamed of the disappointment.

Sella listened.

“You’re allowed to want it to have been different.”

“I know.”

“Knowing seems not to stop you needing to hear it.”

Mara laughed wetly.

“No.”

Outside, Nima was arranging the next technical consultation with Iven.

The work continued.

Somewhere, perhaps, a child would have a father who came home before four.

Mara could wish that without demanding proof.

She went to the kitchen.

The onions needed cutting.

---

A letter arrived from Iven six weeks later.

The instrument was secured.

The young men had agreed to training.

The medicine had saved nineteen people by her count.

Mara smiled at the number.

No one would ever agree.

The letter included a sketch of the modified housing.

Iven had found the hidden instructions.

She had also found the box.

*An insultingly obvious place to put a manual,* she wrote.

Mara laughed aloud.

At the bottom:

*The younger of the two has begun keeping a departure ledger. His daughter has drawn a fish on the cover. He complains that it reduces the dignity of the work.*

Mara set the letter down.

Her hands shook.

Sella read over her shoulder.

Then sat beside her.

For a long time, neither spoke.

---

The correspondence became less frequent.

That was good.

Iven’s station no longer needed constant help.

Its reports were technical.

Ordinary.

Repairs.

Weather.

A route refused because the receiving mark was unstable.

A traveler returned safely.

Mara did not ask about the child.

She wanted to.

She did not.

The boundary was not punishment.

It was a way of letting another life remain its own.

Once, Iven sent a loaf of bread with a note saying the local baker had improved his ovens.

Mara cut it.

No fennel.

She and Sella ate it anyway.

---

When Mara was seventy, the station asked her to record her memories.

Not for a grand history.

For errors.

Things that had been misunderstood.

Details lost between versions.

She agreed on the condition that the recordings could include uncertainty.

The first session lasted two hours.

She spent twenty minutes explaining that the boat had been green, not emerald, and that later paint had made the distinction confusing.

The interviewer was patient.

Mara appreciated that.

At the end, the interviewer asked what she remembered most clearly.

Mara looked toward the window.

“Warm bread.”

“Not your father?”

“Of course my father.”

“But the first thing?”

“The bread.”

She smiled.

“It meant someone had been nearby.”

---

The recordings took a year.

Mara became tired.

They shortened the sessions.

She forgot names, then remembered them at supper and sent corrections.

She told the story of the customs officers three times.

No one removed the repetitions until she asked.

Some stories were painful.

Some were funny.

Some remained unresolved.

At the final session, the interviewer asked whether the station had fulfilled its purpose.

Mara thought for a long time.

“Which one?”

“The original purpose.”

“I don’t think there was an original one large enough to contain what happened.”

“Then what should we write?”

Mara looked at the ledger on the shelf.

“Write that people kept arriving.”

The interviewer waited.

“And we tried to meet them properly.”

---

Sella’s hands grew stiff.

Mara’s eyesight worsened.

They adapted the workshop.

Larger lamps.

Better lenses.

Tools with thicker handles.

A younger repairer took over the jobs they could no longer do safely.

They argued about this less than either had expected.

Perhaps they were tired.

Perhaps they had learned something.

Perhaps both.

On warm days, they sat outside the workshop.

Children brought broken toys.

Some could be repaired.

Some could not.

Mara learned to explain the difference without making impossibility sound like a personal failure.

Sella was better at it.

She would place the broken pieces on the table and ask what the child wanted to keep.

Sometimes they made something new.

Sometimes they only glued a crack.

Not everything needed transformation.

---

The first bell began to crack.

A hairline fracture near the rim.

Nima brought it to Sella.

Mara recognized it before the cloth was unfolded.

The bell from *Tomorrow*.

The one that had rung before her father returned.

Sella examined it.

“It can be stabilized.”

“Will it sound the same?” Nima asked.

“No.”

Mara took the bell carefully.

The brass was warm from other hands.

“What happens if we replace it?” she asked.

“The station can be re-registered.”

“Will the old one remain active?”

“We can retire it.”

Mara looked at Sella.

Sella nodded.

The new bell was made by a local founder.

Its tone was lower.

Clearer.

Unfamiliar.

For weeks, Mara turned her head a moment too late when it rang.

Then she learned.

The old bell sat on a shelf in the kitchen.

Silent.

Not useless.

---

A child asked to ring it.

Mara explained that it was cracked.

“Will it break?”

“It might.”

“Then why keep it?”

Mara looked at the brass.

She could have said because it mattered.

Because it had brought people home.

Because old things deserved care.

Instead she asked, “Do you keep anything you can’t use anymore?”

The child thought.

“A shell.”

“Why?”

“I like it.”

Mara smiled.

“There you are.”

The child accepted that.

Adults were sometimes harder.

---

The green boat left the harbor more often under Nima.

The mobile office had been built to travel.

Mara had kept it close because the town needed it and because, if she was honest, so did she.

Nima took it to flood zones.

Remote stations.

Places where a letter was not enough.

The chandlery remained the home office.

The boat returned.

Not always on schedule.

Always with a message when possible.

Mara stood on the quay when it came in.

Not every time.

Enough.

Nima would step ashore and tell her what mattered.

Mara learned to ask, “Do you want advice?”

Sometimes Nima said yes.

Sometimes no.

Both answers became easier.

---

The last letter from Iven’s route came in a hand Mara recognized.

Not Iven’s.

Her own.

Younger.

The envelope was addressed to the harbor station, not to her personally.

Inside was a technical report and a short note.

*Keeper Iven says you helped us when our equipment was unsafe. I am taking over the local records. Please send any corrections to the attached procedures.*

The signature read:

**Mara Vale.**

Mara sat with the letter in her lap.

Then she laughed.

Not because it was funny.

Because the world still knew how to surprise her.

Sella came to the doorway.

“What?”

Mara handed her the page.

Sella read it.

“Good handwriting.”

“Better than mine now.”

“Don’t fish for reassurance.”

Mara smiled.

They reviewed the procedures together.

There were three corrections.

They sent them.

No prophecy.

No advice about marriage.

No warning about a life that would not be the same.

Just three useful corrections.

---

The reply thanked them.

It also asked a question.

*Do you know why the maker’s plate says Vale and Daughters?*

Mara looked at Sella.

Sella folded her arms.

“Well?”

Mara took a fresh sheet.

She did not want to lie.

She did not want to hand a young woman a future like a contract.

At last she wrote:

*The instruments have passed through several routes, and names do not always identify a single history. We can send what records we have. None of them obliges you to become the person who wrote them.*

She read it aloud.

Sella nodded.

“Add the records.”

Mara did.

The photograph was not among them.

Not yet.

That belonged to Ada too.

She wrote to ask.

---

Ada, older now and still growing things, replied:

*Send it if she wants personal records after seeing the technical ones. Ask first.*

Mara smiled.

Of course.

They asked.

The younger Mara said she would rather wait.

So they waited.

The photograph stayed on the wall at the training station.

The child in it continued to look dissatisfied with the biscuits.

Time had not improved the expression.

Mara was glad.

---

One evening, the harbor bell rang twelve times.

An ordinary hour.

Mara and Sella sat by the kitchen window.

For a moment, Mara’s body remembered the old fear before her mind could name the sound.

Sella noticed.

She placed a hand over Mara’s.

“Twelve o’clock.”

“Yes.”

“Ordinary tide.”

“Yes.”

They listened until the last stroke faded.

Below the harbor, there was darkness.

No moving light.

Above it, the boats shifted on their ropes.

Mara breathed slowly.

Then reached for the teapot.

The tea had gone cold.

They drank it anyway.

---

The next morning, a letter arrived from the training station.

A student had proposed an experiment that might allow abandoned refuge systems to signal independently when maintenance failed.

No keeper required.

No letter hidden beneath a floor.

No child waiting eight years beside a slowing wheel.

The design was incomplete.

The student wanted criticism.

Mara read the first page.

Then the second.

Sella leaned closer.

“There’s a problem with the trigger.”

“Yes.”

“But the idea?”

Mara smiled.

“The idea is worth testing.”

They cleared the table.

Sella fetched a ruler.

Mara found a fresh pencil.

Outside, someone on the quay was calling for a rope.

Inside, the first question took shape in the margin:

*How will the people living there know the signal has been sent?*

Then another:

*Can they choose who receives it?*

And another, beneath that:

*What happens if no one answers?*The student’s name was Arel.

She answered the first two questions within a week.

The third took longer.
