They contacted the garden.

Jun examined the image.

Then a naturalist from another station.

The creature was alive.

It required water of a particular mineral balance.

The well had provided that by accident.

They could maintain it safely for now.

The boy watched them prepare a larger vessel.

“Will it go home?”

“We’ll try to find out where that is.”

“How will you ask it?”

Nima looked at the slow pulse of light.

“We don’t know yet.”

The boy nodded.

He had heard that answer before.

He stayed to help label the water samples.

---

The creature answered music.

Not words.

Leth discovered this when she played in the public room.

Its light changed with the notes.

At first, everyone assumed coincidence.

They tested carefully.

Repeated patterns.

Different intervals.

Silence.

The responses remained consistent.

Arel built a recorder.

Leth worked with the naturalist.

The boy returned each afternoon.

They learned three signals.

Comfort.

Distress.

A request they could not yet interpret.

The request came whenever a low note was held steadily.

The creature pressed toward the glass.

Its light divided into three points.

Then joined again.

Nima watched the recordings.

“Others?”

“Possibly,” Leth said.

“Home?”

“Possibly.”

“Let’s not choose the meaning too quickly.”

They wrote both possibilities down.

Then kept listening.

---

Mara visited once to see it.

The vessel stood in a quiet room with shaded windows.

The creature’s light moved slowly through the water.

Leth played the three-note pattern associated with comfort.

The light steadied.

Mara sat in her old chair, brought into the room because it was comfortable.

For a long time, she watched.

“Beautiful,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Does it want to be looked at?”

Leth glanced at the naturalist.

“We don’t know.”

Mara nodded.

“Then perhaps that belongs in the questions.”

It did.

They shortened observation periods.

Added cover.

Recorded whether the creature approached or withdrew.

The work became slower.

More careful.

The boy did not complain.

He had begun to care less about seeing it glow and more about whether it was well.

That seemed a useful change.

---

The signal they thought meant home turned out to mean depth.

They discovered this by placing the vessel above a second, connected tank.

The creature moved downward.

Then repeated the pattern.

They added another level.

Again.

Not a wish to escape.

A requirement.

Its natural environment lay deeper than they had guessed.

The harbor was deep enough in places, but its water was wrong.

A controlled column was built in the workshop cellar.

The creature descended.

Its light brightened.

Then, for the first time, it produced a new pattern.

Five pulses.

A pause.

Two.

Leth wrote the rhythm.

The naturalist checked the environmental readings.

Arel recorded everything.

Nima contacted the well station.

“Can you tell us how deep the bucket traveled on the day it arrived?”

Oren’s reply came the next morning.

Not far.

But the rope had returned wet beyond the length that had been lowered.

A route.

Small.

Unregistered.

Perhaps still open.

They began tracing it.

---

The trace led beneath a sea they had never mapped.

No land nearby.

No receiving station.

No human voice.

The creature’s light changed when shown a filtered image of the water.

Not enough to prove recognition.

Enough to justify further work.

They sent an instrument first.

Pressure.

Temperature.

Minerals.

Movement.

All close to what the creature needed.

Then sound.

A long, low vibration.

The creature answered immediately.

Not with five pulses.

With many.

The room filled with light reflected from the water column.

Leth stopped playing.

No one spoke.

The receiver carried another vibration.

Then another.

The creature’s response became steady.

Arel looked at the naturalist.

The naturalist looked at Nima.

“Now,” she said, “we need to learn whether this is welcome.”

Nima nodded.

They did not open the full passage.

Not yet.

They had found a possible home.

They still had to ask, as well as they could, whether the creature wished to go there.

---

The boy who had brought the jar understood this better than some of the adults.

“Let it choose,” he said.

The naturalist agreed in principle.

The difficulty was designing a choice that did not force danger into the decision.

They built a small, stable connection between the water column and a protected chamber on the distant seabed.

The creature could approach.

Withdraw.

Return.

No current would pull it through.

No gate would close behind it without observation.

The first day, it did not approach.

The second, it touched the edge.

The third, it crossed halfway and returned.

The boy watched the readings from the next room.

On the fourth day, it went through.

Stayed.

Came back once.

Then left again.

They maintained the connection for a full cycle.

At the end, the creature remained with the others.

Nima closed the route slowly.

The vessel in the cellar was empty.

The boy cried.

Leth sat beside him.

“Did we do it right?” he asked.

“We did it carefully,” she said.

He wiped his face.

“Is that the same?”

“Not always.”

He looked toward the empty water.

“But this time?”

She took a breath.

“We have good reasons to think so.”

---

The empty vessel was cleaned.

The instruments were stored.

The records were shared.

No one wrote a triumphant account about returning a mysterious creature to its rightful place.

They wrote what they had observed.

What they had inferred.

What remained uncertain.

The boy contributed a drawing.

It showed the creature as three lights joined by a silver line.

Underneath, he wrote:

*It went away by itself.*

Nima kept that sentence in the report.

It was accurate.

It mattered.

Mara read the report at home.

Then she looked at the child’s drawing.

Sella watched her smile.

“What?”

“Nothing needs improving.”

Sella leaned over.

After a moment, she nodded.

“No.”

---

That night, Mara dreamed of the harbor.

Not the old harbor.

Not a possible one.

The harbor as it was now.

The handrail.

The repaired steps.

The sign on the chandlery.

People moving in and out.

She stood beside the green boat.

No one asked her to board.

No one offered a lost person.

No bell warned of a changing tide.

A young operator came down the steps carrying a notebook.

“Are you waiting for someone?” he asked.

Mara thought.

Then shook her head.

“Just looking.”

He nodded.

“That’s allowed.”

She woke with the feeling of sunlight on her face.

It was raining.

Sella was asleep beside her.

Mara listened to the rain for a while.

Then she closed her eyes again.

---

In the morning, the station sent a question.

Not urgent.

A new apprentice had found an annotation in the oldest ledger.

A sentence in Mara’s hand:

*The last column should remain the widest.*

No one could find the table it referred to.

Did she remember?

Mara smiled.

“Yes.”

She asked Sella for paper.

Her hand tired easily.

She wrote slowly.

*The column was called uncertainties.*

Then, after a pause:

*You may use a different layout. Just do not make uncertainty disappear because the page looks neater without it.*

She signed.

Sella read it.

“Send?”

Mara nodded.

The reply went to the station.

The apprentice copied it into the training notes.

Then redesigned the table.

The last column remained wide.

Not because Mara had ordered it.

Because the apprentice found there was a great deal to put there.

---

By afternoon, the new table had its first entry.

A traveler remembered a city with two moons.

The instrument found three cities matching the description.

In one, the moons were actual celestial bodies.

In another, one was a reflection from a permanent ice ring.

In the third, the second moon was a lamp mounted above the harbor.

The traveler could not say which was home.

The apprentice did not choose the most impressive answer.

She asked what the traveler remembered buying at the market.

The answer was a particular kind of bread.

Round.

Salted.

A bitter seed in the crust.

The third city made it.

So did the first.

The second did not.

One possibility removed.

Not a solution.

Progress.

They continued.

Outside, the tide rose one step.

Inside, the questions grew more specific.

---

The traveler remembered a dog.

Then a bridge.

Then the smell of hot iron near the market.

The first city had a foundry beside the bridge.

The third had closed its foundry forty years earlier.

The traveler was twenty-six.

Unless route time had shifted, that made the first more likely.

The apprentice wrote the condition.

Nima approved.

They established a view.

The traveler looked at the city for a long time.

Then said, “No.”

The apprentice felt the work collapse for a moment.

Then steadied.

“All right. What feels wrong?”

“The river turns the other way.”

A map error.

Or a different city.

Or memory.

They did not decide immediately.

They widened the search.

The bitter-seeded bread became a clue, not proof.

The apprentice learned something she would remember longer than an easy success.

---

At home, Mara asked whether the traveler had found the city.

“Not yet,” Nima said.

“Are they safe?”

“Yes.”

“Then there is time.”

Nima smiled tiredly.

“Some days I believe that more easily than others.”

“So did I.”

“So do you?”

Mara looked at Sella.

At the kitchen.

At the letters.

“Not about everything.”

Nima nodded.

They sat together without trying to improve the answer.

Later, when Nima returned to the station, the traveler had remembered the name of a street.

It was misspelled in three different archives.

A fourth had it correctly.

The search moved again.

---

The street led to a fourth city.

Two moons.

A foundry.

A river turning the remembered way.

Round bread with bitter seeds.

The traveler recognized the harbor lamp before seeing the house.

They asked for contact.

The receiving station answered.

A family was found.

The route was verified.

The traveler chose to go.

At departure, the apprentice read the count aloud.

At arrival, the other station confirmed.

The two numbers matched.

The case closed with a note:

**Initial search criteria insufficient. Local details decisive.**

The apprentice added another line in her own notebook:

*Ask about bread.*

Nima saw it.

She thought of Mara.

Then of the first warm loaf in the green boat.

She did not tell the apprentice that the advice was old.

It was new to her.

That was enough.

---

Mara received the good news with breakfast.

Sella read the message aloud.

Then set the paper beside the breadboard.

Mara smiled.

“What kind of bread?”

Sella read the description.

“Round. Salted. Bitter seeds.”

“Do we have any?”

“No.”

“We should try some.”

“I’ll ask.”

The receiving station sent a loaf.

It arrived three days later.

Mara tasted it.

Considered.

Sella waited.

“Well?”

“Too bitter.”

Sella laughed.

They ate it with cheese.

Not every connection had to become a favorite.

Some were worth knowing about anyway.

---

The loaf’s wrapper contained a small correction.

The seeds were not bitter.

The bread had been scorched.

The baker apologized and promised another.

Mara laughed until she coughed.

Sella took the wrapper.

“Will you tell Nima?”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“Because even bread can be misleading evidence.”

Nima added the correction to the training case.

The apprentice read it.

Then wrote beneath *Ask about bread*:

*Ask whether it was burnt.*

The manual grew by one more useful absurdity.

---

At the end of that week, the station’s incoming board filled with three new requests.

A bridge whose far end had moved.

A household receiving letters meant for people who had not yet been born.

A station whose bell rang only when no one was present to hear it.

Nima assigned the bridge to the field team.

The letters to records.

The bell to Arel, who complained that the problem statement was impossible to verify.

Hal suggested a recorder.

Arel said she had already thought of that.

Hal smiled.

The work began.

Mara heard about the requests later.

She chose the bell.

“Why that one?” Sella asked.

Mara smiled.

“Professional curiosity.”

Sella brought the first report.

The bell had been recorded.

It did not ring when the room was empty.

It rang when the recorder believed the room was empty.

Mara sat up a little straighter.

“That,” she said, “is a different question.”

---

The recorder detected presence by movement.

Someone in the room was very still.

Arel checked the heat readings.

One warm place near the back wall.

The station operator insisted no one was there.

They did not argue.

They asked whether the room had been fully inspected.

Shelves.

Cupboards.

Ceiling space.

Floor panels.

At the back, behind a stack of old maps, they found a cat.

Sleeping.

The bell was connected to a motion-sensitive draft shield.

When the cat slept, the shield closed.

When it closed, a loose wire touched the bell circuit.

No missing traveler.

No hidden intelligence.

A cat.

A wire.

A poor design.

Arel repaired the wire.

The operator adopted the cat.

Mara was delighted.

Sella read the report twice.

“This is your favorite.”

“Today.”

They sent a donation for cat food.

The station named the cat Attendant.

Sen objected.

Then withdrew the objection after seeing the photograph.

---

The bridge was harder.

The letters harder still.

Nima worked late.

Hal learned to stop when tired enough to mistake a name.

Arel learned that some equipment failures were funny only after everyone was safe.

The new apprentices learned all of it imperfectly.

Mara watched from farther away.

She could no longer read every report.

She chose.

The cat.

The traveler who found the right city.

The refuge where the ceiling was bright again.

Not because the harder cases mattered less.

Because one person’s attention was finite.

She had spent much of her life discovering that.

Now she practiced living by it.

---

On a warm evening, Sella wheeled Mara to the harbor.

The green boat had returned.

Its paint was fresh again.

Another shade.

The bell shone.

A young woman stood at the bow, checking the line.

She looked up.

“Mara Vale?”

“Yes.”

“I’m Reni. I’ve just joined the mobile team.”

“Welcome.”

Reni smiled.

Then hesitated.

“May I ask something?”

“Of course.”

“Did you really climb aboard before knowing what it was?”

Mara laughed softly.

“Yes.”

Reni looked relieved.

“Everyone makes it sound as though you had a plan.”

“No.”

“What did you have?”

Mara considered.

“A basket. An empty morning. Too much curiosity.”

Sella added, “And poor supervision.”

Mara looked at her.

Sella smiled.

Reni laughed.

Then the bell rang.

A request.

Reni straightened.

Mara lifted a hand.

“Go on.”

The young operator went below.

Sella turned the chair toward home.

Behind them, the office lights came on.

---

At the counter, Reni opened the channel.

The caller was a woman standing in a station full of sand.

Not a desert station.

A room filling with sand from beneath the floor.

Reni’s first thought was to open an evacuation route.

Her training made her ask one question first.

“How many people?”

“Four.”

“Can all four reach the receiving area?”

“Yes.”

“Is anyone unable to move without help?”

“One.”

Reni called Nima.

The route team prepared.

The caller described the instrument.

The sand rose around the table legs.

No time for a full investigation.

Enough time for the essential checks.

Receiving space clear.

Medical support ready.

Route stable.

Consent confirmed.

Four departures.

Four arrivals.

Then the passage closed.

Only afterward did they ask what had caused the sand.

Reni sat down, shaking.

Nima brought water.

No speech.

No immediate lesson.

First, recovery.

Then the report.

---

The sand had come from a broken storage route.

A shipment of construction material had been assigned to the wrong destination.

The receiving room had no volume limit.

A simple omission.

A dangerous one.

Arel updated the freight protocol.

The sender paid for the damage.

The affected station insisted on independent inspection before reopening.

The four travelers returned when ready.

One chose not to.

That choice was respected.

Reni read the final report.

She had done well.

She could also identify two things she would do differently next time.

Both were recorded.

Nima signed the assessment.

Then asked whether Reni wanted another shift immediately.

“No,” Reni said.

“Good. Tomorrow?”

“Tomorrow.”

The word felt less like a boat’s name and more like a reasonable amount of time.

---

Mara heard the account from Reni a week later.

The young operator came to the house with bread.

Not warm.

She apologized for that.

Mara waved the apology away.

Reni told the story quickly at first.

Then slowed when Mara asked about the person who needed help moving.

“An old man.”

“Did he want to come?”

“Yes. He kept apologizing for being slow.”

Mara nodded.

“What did you say?”

“That we had time.”

“Did you?”

“Enough.”

“Then it was a useful thing to say.”

Reni looked down at her hands.

“I was frightened.”

“Yes.”

“I thought perhaps that meant I wasn’t ready.”

Mara smiled gently.

“Did you keep asking the questions?”

“Yes.”

“Then tell me the next one.”

Reni thought.

Then began again, this time at the point where the sand first reached the chair. 
---

By the time she finished, Sella had made tea.

Reni stayed for another hour.

They discussed freight limits.

Old people apologizing.

The difference between saying there was time and pretending there was no danger.

Mara grew tired.

Reni noticed before Sella had to say anything.

She stood.

“Thank you.”

“You brought bread,” Mara said. “That made you welcome before the story.”

Reni smiled.

At the door, she paused.

“I’d like to come again.”

Mara looked at the calendar.

“Yes.”

They chose a day.

No promise beyond that.

Enough for one visit.

After Reni left, Sella sat beside Mara.

“You enjoyed that.”

“Yes.”

“Would you like more tea?”

“No.”

“Rest?”

“Yes.”

Sella adjusted the cushion.

Outside, the harbor continued.

Inside, Mara closed her eyes, not to leave the story, but because listening to it had become work too, and she had finally learned to stop before exhaustion made the next kindness harder. 
---

The next kindness was small.

A letter from a child at the training station asked whether Mara’s first basket still existed.

It did.

In the cupboard above the pantry.

Sella took it down.

The wicker was dark with age.

One handle had been repaired.

Mara ran her fingers over the rim.

“I forgot we kept it.”

“You kept everything useful.”

“And some other things.”

“Quite a few.”

They photographed the basket.

The child had asked for it as part of an exhibition on the station’s beginning.

Mara agreed to lend it.

Then added a note.

*It was an ordinary shopping basket. Please do not make it look more mysterious than it was.*

The exhibition displayed it beside a list.

Onions.

Flour.

Blue thread.

Visitors often stayed longer before that list than before the original lamp.

The curator could not explain why.

Mara thought she could.

She did not feel the need to write it down.

---

The basket returned with a thank-you note and a small repair to the handle.

A student had done it.

With permission.

The new wicker was lighter.

Mara examined the work.

Good.

Not invisible.

She liked that.

Sella placed the basket by the door.

“Use it?”

“For what?”

“Shopping.”

Mara laughed.

The next morning, Sella took it to market.

She bought onions.

Flour.

Blue thread.

Also pears, because she liked them and was tired of letting symbolism decide the shopping.

When she returned, Mara looked inside.

“Pears?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

They ate one together over the sink so the juice would not drip on the floor.

Outside, the station bell rang.

Sella listened.

Not their call.

She cut another slice.

---

That afternoon, the letters addressed to unborn people yielded a clue.

The household receiving them had once been a courier office.

Its sorting shelf retained an old route association.

The names were not predictions.

They belonged to living people on a route with a different calendar.

Hal corrected the address translation.

The letters went on their way.

The household kept one empty envelope as a curiosity.

With the sender’s permission.

The case report was short.

Mara read it before supper.

“Good,” she said.

Sella looked up.

“Which part?”

“No prophecy required.”

Sella smiled.

“That does simplify things.”

“Not always.”

“No.”

They put the report aside.

The pears were ripe.

That mattered more immediately.

---

The bridge’s far end was found in a field.

No river beneath it.

No road beyond.

The structure had migrated after a failed alignment test.

Its near end remained attached to a busy market street.

People had begun using it to reach the field because the soil was good.

The field’s owner objected.

The market merchants liked the new space.

The engineers wanted to restore the original location.

The local children preferred the field.

Nima sent a mediator, not only a technician.

The technical repair took one day.

The agreement took six weeks.

In the end, the bridge returned.

A separate, properly registered path to the field remained by consent.

The field’s owner received rent.

The children gained a play area.

The engineers were irritated by the delay.

The mediator asked whether they preferred having to repeat the work after a dispute.

They did not.

The case became required reading.

Not because it was dramatic.

Because moving a bridge was easier than deciding where it belonged.

---

Mara liked that report too.

She asked for it to be read aloud twice.

Sella wondered why.

“The field,” Mara said.

“What about it?”

“Someone would have called it an unintended side effect.”

“It was.”

“Yes. And people had begun living with it.”

Sella nodded.

“Repairing the machine wasn’t the whole repair.”

“No.”

They sat quietly.

Then Sella said, “You’re turning it into a saying.”

Mara laughed.

“Stop me.”

“I just did.”

The report went into the ordinary folder, not the manual.

Some thoughts could remain conversations.

---

The station’s anniversary came without agreement on which day to celebrate.

The first boat.

The first successful shutdown.

The first paid account.

The first charter.

The first time the sign went up.

Nima solved the problem by choosing a week.

There would be food, demonstrations, public records, and no speeches longer than five minutes.

Leth enforced the last condition.

Mara attended one afternoon.

The town had changed around the station.

So had the station around the town.

Children ran between tables.

Operators explained instruments.

Travelers told stories that did not always flatter the people who had helped them.

That was permitted.

Required, even, if the celebration was to mean anything.

At one table, a woman described being asked the same question six times.

At another, a man praised a clerk who had believed his uncertainty.

Mara listened.

Then she ate cake.

It was excellent.

Not every assessment needed qualifications.

---

A small group asked for a photograph.

Mara agreed.

They arranged themselves awkwardly on the quay.

Nima.

Hal.

Arel.

Reni.

Leth.

Colm.

Sen in a chair, looking annoyed at the sun.

Sella beside Mara.

Others joining as space allowed.

The photographer asked them to be still.

Someone laughed.

The shutter clicked at the wrong moment.

In the picture, no one looked entirely dignified.

Mara loved it.

A copy went to the training station.

Another to Willowbank.

Another to the library carriage.

At home, Sella placed theirs beside Ada’s old photograph.

Two moments.

No single future.

No complete account.

Just people who had happened to stand together long enough for the light to leave a record.

---

After the celebration, there were dishes.

Many dishes.

Mara had gone home.

Nima stood at the sink with Reni.

Hal dried.

Arel carried trays.

The work was warm, repetitive, and slightly ridiculous after a week discussing the future of route governance.

Reni held up a chipped cup.

“Keep?”

Nima examined it.

The crack did not reach the base.

“Not for hot drinks.”

“Then?”

“Pens.”

Hal took it.

The next cup was sound.

The next broken.

They sorted.

Outside, the last visitors left.

Inside, the station became itself again.

Not an anniversary.

A place needing clean cups for morning.

---

Morning brought a man carrying a sealed jar of ash.

He wanted to return it to a place that no longer appeared on any map.

Hal welcomed him.

Asked whether he wanted to sit.

The man said yes.

Nima brought the first clean cup.

Reni opened the ledger.

Arel checked the inquiry channel.

The man placed the jar carefully on the table.

“My sister wanted the hill behind our house,” he said.

“Tell us about the hill.”

He began with the trees.

Then the path.

Then the view from the top.

The place was absent from the maps.

It was not absent from him.

They would need both kinds of knowledge.

Reni wrote slowly.

The cup steamed.

The jar remained closed.

There was no reason to hurry the first question.

---

At home, Mara watched the morning light move across the kitchen floor.

The cracked bowl was still on the shelf.

Repaired long ago.

The basket stood by the door.

The blue coat hung above it.

Sella was in the workshop.

A clock ticked.

Mara thought of the first note.

Seven words.

The older hand.

The instruction that had opened so much.

She wondered, not for the first time, what would have happened if she had not read it.

Then she let the question rest.

There were other questions now.

What would Sella like for supper?

Would the weather hold?

Could the window latch be made easier to turn?

She reached for the little notebook beside her chair.

Not the manual.

An ordinary notebook.

She wrote:

**Ask about the latch.**

Then, after a moment:

**Pears if there are any.**

The page had room.

She left it open. 
---

Sella came in at noon with a repaired latch.

Mara looked at the notebook.

Then at the small brass fitting in Sella’s hand.

“I hadn’t asked yet.”

“I know.”

“How?”

“You’ve been fighting with the window for a week.”

Mara smiled.

“Observant.”

“Occasionally.”

They fitted the latch.

It turned easily.

Mara opened the window.

Air moved through the kitchen, carrying salt and bread and the faint sound of a violin from the station courtyard.

Leth was teaching.

A student missed a note.

Tried again.

Missed it differently.

Then found it.

Sella stood beside the window.

“There.”

Mara nodded.

“Yes.”

Neither meant only the latch.

Neither felt obliged to explain.

---

The hill behind the house was found through the trees.

A species with white bark and red-veined leaves.

Rare.

Grown in only a few recorded regions.

The man remembered the smell after rain.

The naturalist from the garden identified it.

The route search narrowed.

A view opened.

The hill was there.

The house was not.

The man looked at the empty foundation for a long time.

Then asked to see the path.

It remained.

Overgrown.

Passable.

He chose to go.

A local guide agreed to accompany him.

The jar of ash traveled with him.

No one at the station watched the scattering.

That belonged to him.

They kept the return route ready.

When he came back, the jar was empty.

He looked exhausted.

Then he asked for tea.

Reni brought it in the same cup.

He noticed.

Smiled.

And sat down.

---

That evening, Hal wrote the final entry.

**Request completed. Traveler returned safely.**

He hesitated.

The sentence seemed too small.

Nima read over his shoulder.

“What are you thinking?”

“It was more than that.”

“Yes.”

“Should I write more?”

“What do we need to know for the record?”

He looked at the route data.

The consent.

The return count.

“Nothing.”

“Then let the rest belong to him.”

Hal nodded.

He closed the file.

Outside, the man was walking toward the inn, carrying the empty jar in one hand.

He would decide what to do with it.

The station did not need to decide for him.

---

Mara slept through the afternoon the next day.

When she woke, the light had changed.

For a moment, she did not know the room.

Then the shelf.

The bell.

The window.

Sella’s tools on the table.

Home assembled itself from details.

She breathed out.

Sella came in.

“Tea?”

Mara nodded.

Then said, “I was lost for a moment.”

Sella sat beside her.

“I’m here.”

“I know.”

“You do now.”

“Yes.”

They held hands.

No instrument.

No route.

No proof beyond the room and the voice and the warmth of familiar fingers.

Sometimes returning was that small.

Sometimes it needed someone to stay until the place became recognizable again.

---

Nima visited later.

Mara was alert but tired.

They spoke about the station.

Then stopped.

Nima did not fill the silence with reports.

Mara watched the window.

At last she asked, “How many today?”

“Arrivals?”

“Yes.”

“Seven.”

“Departures?”

“Five.”

Mara frowned slightly.

Nima continued.

“Two staying. Both registered.”

Mara smiled.

“Good.”

Nima took her hand.

The old habit was there even now.

Count.

Check.

Know who remained.

Mara closed her eyes for a moment.

Then opened them.

“Did anyone bring bread?”

Nima laughed.

“Yes.”

“What kind?”

“Ordinary.”

“Best kind to begin with.”

---

The bread was brought the next morning.

A small loaf.

Still warm.

Mara touched it before anything else on the tray.

Sella saw.

Neither spoke about the memory.

They broke it together.

Crust.

Yeast.

Butter softening in the heat.

Outside, the harbor woke.

Someone argued with a crate.

Not crabs this time.

Apples.

The sound made Mara smile.

At the station, Hal opened the front door.

Reni checked the receiving room.

Arel inspected the signal board.

Nima stood for a moment at the counter, looking at the day’s blank page.

Then the first visitor came in.

A woman carrying a folded sail.

“I think this belongs to you,” she said.

Nima took the cloth.

Blue thread.

A patch shaped roughly like a sleeping dog.

She looked toward the harbor.

The blue skiff was still at its mooring.

Its sail was furled.

Intact.

Nima set the folded sail on the table.

“Tell me where you found it.”

---

The woman had found it on a beach three towns north.

No wreck.

No footprints.

Only the sail, dry above the tideline, weighted with a smooth stone.

Under the stone was a card.

She had nearly thrown it away.

Now she produced it.

The handwriting was not Mara’s.

Not Eli’s.

Not any hand Nima knew.

The message read:

*There is another boat. This one has people aboard.*

Below it, a set of coordinates.

And a time.

Not a deadline.

A proposed meeting.

Nima checked the route signature.

Ordinary geography.

No active optical field.

A coastal position beyond the headland.

She called the harbor watch.

Then Tavi.

Then the medical team.

No one went alone.

No one assumed a mystery required an extraordinary answer before checking the sea.

Within the hour, two rescue boats were ready.

Reni carried blankets.

Hal carried the passenger ledger.

Nima carried a radio and a plain notebook.

The old station bell remained at the dock.

The boats pushed out into open water.

---

They found the vessel where the card said it would be.

A small sailing boat.

Dismasted.

Low in the water.

Eight people aboard.

Cold.

Thirsty.

Alive.

No one knew who had left the sail on the beach.

No one knew how the coordinates had been sent.

They had no spare sail to give away.

The resemblance was exact.

The explanation could wait.

Reni helped lift a child into the rescue boat.

Nima spoke to the captain.

Hal counted.

Eight departures.

Eight arrivals.

Then he counted again.

The captain looked at the notebook.

“Does that help?”

“Yes,” Hal said.

The captain nodded.

“Then do it.”

They towed the damaged boat toward the harbor.

Behind them, the sea darkened under a line of cloud.

The storm would arrive after they reached shelter.

For once, the timing was kind.

---

At the quay, people were waiting.

Blankets.

Hot food.

A doctor.

Someone to take names.

Someone to find beds.

The rescued sailors spoke three languages.

The station found interpreters for two and improvised carefully for the third.

No one was made into a story before they had eaten.

The folded sail remained on the table.

Arel examined it later.

Its fibers were old.

The stitching newer.

The blue thread matched no local batch.

There was no route residue strong enough to trace.

Nima filed it as unexplained assistance.

Not miracle.

Not proof.

Assistance.

The people were safe.

That fact did not require them to solve the sender first.

---

Mara heard about the sail in the evening.

She listened with her eyes closed.

Sella thought she had fallen asleep.

Then she asked, “All eight?”

“All eight.”

“Good.”

Nima waited.

“Do you know anything about it?”

Mara opened her eyes.

“No.”

The answer seemed to please her.

Nima smiled.

“Neither do we.”

Mara looked toward the window.

The storm had reached the harbor.

Rain struck the glass.

Inside, the room was warm.

“Keep the sail,” she said.

“We will.”

“Not instead of fixing their boat.”

Nima laughed softly.

“No.”

Mara closed her eyes again.

This time, she slept.

---

The boat was repaired over the next fortnight.

Its captain stayed in town long enough to learn the shape of the harbor and the habits of the people who worked there.

He asked about payment.

The rescue fund covered the immediate cost.

The repairs were negotiated.

Some paid in money.

Some in work.

No debt was tied to permission to leave.

The captain read that clause twice.

Then signed.

When the boat departed, all eight people were aboard by choice.

The mysterious sail remained at the station.

Their own new sail was plain.

No sleeping dog.

No blue patch.

The captain preferred it that way.

Nima watched them go.

Then returned to the counter.

A man was waiting with a map.

A woman with a letter.

A child with no question yet, only a damaged toy.

Reni took the map.

Hal took the letter.

Arel looked at the toy and said, “I can’t promise, but I can have a look.”

The child nodded.

That was enough to begin.

---

The toy was a boat.

Green.

A little brass bead hung at the bow to serve as a bell.

Its mast had snapped.

Arel examined the break.

“Wooden dowel,” she said. “Easy enough.”

The child looked relieved.

“Can it still go places?”

“On water?”

“Anywhere.”

Arel smiled.

“This one goes where you put it.”

The child considered that.

“Good.”

They repaired the mast.

Arel gave him sandpaper and showed him how to smooth the joint.

He worked solemnly.

When the repair was finished, he set the boat on the counter and pushed it toward the edge.

Hal caught it.

“Not over waterfalls indoors.”

The child laughed.

Then picked it up and carried it out carefully.

Nima watched him leave.

The station had repaired a toy.

No report beyond materials used.

No route count.

Some work belonged in the day without needing a case number.

---

At home, Sella opened the window.

Mara was awake.

The air smelled of wet stone after the storm.

“Boat gone?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Safe?”

“As far as we know.”

Mara nodded.

The phrase no longer sounded like evasion.

It sounded like the boundary of honest knowledge.

Sella sat beside her.

For a while, they listened.

The harbor bell.

A cart.

A distant laugh.

Then Mara said, “I’m tired.”

“I know.”

“Not unhappy.”

Sella took her hand.

“I know.”

Mara looked at the light on the wall.

It moved slowly.

No hurry.

No warning.

Just the afternoon passing through the window.

---

The station’s next letter was addressed to both of them.

From Ada.

She was coming to visit.

No emergency.

No damaged boat.

No medical case.

Just a visit.

She asked whether the guest room was available and whether she should bring anything.

Sella wrote back.

Yes.

Pears, if convenient.

Mara added a line.

*And yourself, without a task.*

The letter left through the ordinary courier route.

Its departure was recorded.

Its arrival confirmed.

A small passage for paper.

A large promise carried in it.

Ada replied with a date.

They put it on the calendar.

For the next week, Mara asked every morning how many days remained.

Sella answered without teasing.

Some waiting was welcome.

It was still waiting.

---

Ada arrived with pears and no task.

She had gray in her hair now.

Mara looked at her for a long time.

“You’ve grown.”

Ada laughed.

“So have you.”

“In several inconvenient directions.”

They embraced carefully.

The afternoon passed in the kitchen.

Stories.

Silences.

Tea.

Ada spoke of the garden, of people Mara had known, of people she had not.

She did not fill every gap.

Mara did not ask for a complete account.

At dusk, Ada took down the old cracked bell.

“May I?”

Mara nodded.

Ada turned it in her hands.

“My mother kept one just like this.”

“Yes.”

“Different scratches.”

“Yes.”

She set it back.

Two lives could resemble one another without becoming interchangeable.

They had all learned that in different ways.

Sella brought supper.

No one wanted to leave the table.

---

The next morning, Ada went to the station.

She knew many of the people.

Others knew her only from the photograph.

She found that unsettling.

Nima apologized for the awkwardness.

Ada shook her head.

“It isn’t yours.”

She spent an hour in the garden with the growers.

Then helped repair a broken trellis.

A task after all.

Not assigned.

Chosen.

When she returned to the house, Mara noticed dirt on her cuffs.

“You were supposed to visit without work.”

“I did.”

“And then?”

“I saw a trellis.”

Mara smiled.

“Dangerous.”

“Very.”

They ate pears by the open window.

The fruit was ripe enough to bruise under their fingers.

Ada had brought too many.

Sella began planning preserves.

Mara suggested giving some to the station.

They did both.

---

On Ada’s last evening, Mara asked for the photograph.

The old one.

The young woman.

The child.

The missing tooth.

Ada brought it from the training station with permission.

Mara held it close.

“I used to think this was an instruction.”

“I know.”

“It became a question.”

“And now?”

Mara looked at Ada.

“Now it’s a photograph of someone I’m glad to know.”

Ada bowed her head.

For a moment, she could not speak.

Then she said, “Me too.”

Sella took the picture before Mara’s hands grew tired.

They placed it on the table.

Not as a promise.

Not as a warning.

As a record of a day when a child had inspected the biscuits and found them wanting.

Ada laughed when she remembered the exact complaint.

“They were damp.”

Mara looked offended on behalf of a woman she had both been and not been.

“That would justify an inspection failure.”

“It did.”

---

After Ada left, the house was quiet.

Not empty.

Sella was there.

The clock.

The window.

The shelf they had finally straightened.

Mara slept more.

Some days she wanted news.

Some days she wanted only the names of flowers in the garden.

Nima learned to ask.

The station continued.

A bridge repaired.

A traveler found.

A request declined.

A mistake corrected.

A cat adopted.

A child taught to tie a knot.

No single day carried the whole purpose.

Together, the days made something that could outlast the people who began it.

Mara knew this.

She also knew she would miss it.

Both were allowed.

---

One morning, she asked Sella to bring the ordinary notebook.

The one with the latch and the pears.

Sella placed it on her lap.

Mara turned to a blank page.

Her hand was unsteady.

She wrote slowly.

*If someone brings a letter, read it carefully.*

Then stopped.

Sella waited.

Mara smiled faintly.

“That’s obvious.”

“Sometimes obvious things are useful.”

She continued.

*If someone brings a person, offer them a chair.*

Another pause.

Then:

*If you do not know which you are being given, ask.*

She looked at the page.

Not a manual.

Not a final statement.

Three lines.

She handed the notebook to Sella.

“Keep it in the kitchen.”

“Not the archive?”

“The kitchen.”

Sella nodded.

She put it beside the breadboard.

There would be hands there.

Eyes.

People needing ordinary reminders.

---

At the station, Reni received a package that looked like a letter and contained a person’s voice.

The voice asked not to be opened in public.

She checked the seal.

Verified the route.

Moved to a private room.

Asked whether the voice could hear her.

“Yes.”

“Are you a live caller?”

“Yes.”

“Are you in immediate danger?”

“I don’t think so.”

“What do you need?”

“A witness.”

Reni sat down.

Nima joined her.

They established consent to record.

Then the voice began describing a station where departures had been counted but arrivals had not.

A new case.

An old danger.

Different names.

They listened.

The first page filled.

Then the second.

The work did not end because the lesson had been learned once.

It continued because someone had noticed it happening again.

---

Nima told Mara only the outline.

Mara listened.

Then asked, “Are they safe?”

“For now.”

“Who’s with them?”

“A local advocate. Two operators we trust. The review group is involved.”

Mara nodded.

“Good.”

She did not ask to lead it.

She did not ask for every name.

She trusted Nima with the work.

That trust had taken years to become more than a sentiment.

Now it had a shape.

A chair she no longer occupied.

A key in another hand.

A report she did not need to read before someone acted well.

Sella saw the relief on her face.

After Nima left, Mara said, “I think they’ll manage.”

Sella smiled.

“They already are.”

---

The harbor had rain for three days.

On the fourth, the sun returned.

Mara asked to sit outside.

They brought the chair into the small garden.

The winter-flowering shrub was out of season.

Jory’s herb had found its way there from a cutting.

A silver leaf caught the light.

Sella sat beside her.

No reports.

No letters.

A cup of tea cooling on the arm of the chair.

Mara watched a bee work along the flowers.

After a while, she said, “I used to think the sea was where things went.”

Sella waited.

“Then it kept bringing them back.”

“Not everything.”

“No.”

The bee moved on.

Mara closed her eyes.

Sella held her hand.

The afternoon continued, neither cruel nor kind, simply long enough for the shadow of the fence to cross the path.

---

That evening, Mara asked about the tide.

“Coming in,” Sella said.

“How far?”

“Near the second step.”

Mara nodded.

She had known those steps all her life.

The water’s height was a kind of clock no mechanism could quite replace.

“Will you open the window?”

“It’s open.”

“Good.”

From the harbor came a faint bell.

The new one.

Then voices.

Someone laughing.

Someone calling for a rope.

Mara smiled.

“Enough length,” she murmured.

“What?”

“For the knot.”

Sella pressed her hand.

“Yes.”

Outside, the rope was thrown.

Caught.

Secured.

The boat settled against the quay.

Inside, Mara listened until she could no longer distinguish the individual sounds, only the familiar shape of people arriving somewhere they were expected.

---

In the morning, Sella wrote to Nima.

Not a long letter.

She did not need one.

Nima came.

Then Ada was told.

Then the station.

The public room closed for an hour.

The routes remained staffed.

Someone had to be there for people who did not know what had happened in the kitchen by the harbor.

Mara’s chair was occupied that afternoon by an exhausted woman who had been traveling for two days.

No one asked her to move.

Sella saw when she came to the station.

She stood in the doorway for a moment.

Then nodded.

The instruction had been followed.

It hurt.

It was right.

Both remained true.

---

They did not ring twelve bells.

Mara had not asked for that.

They held a gathering by the harbor.

People spoke.

Some briefly.

Some too long.

Leth, old and patient with grief, did not enforce the five-minute rule.

Nima read from the first list.

What was known.

What was thought.

What had not been tested.

Ada read nothing.

She told a story about biscuits.

Sella brought the ordinary notebook from the kitchen.

She read the three lines.

Then closed it.

The sea moved against the steps.

No light came on beneath it.

No boat returned the person they had lost.

The green boat rested at its berth, ready for work.

---

Afterward, there was food.

There had to be.

People had traveled.

People were tired.

Someone had brought onions.

Someone flour.

A spool of blue thread lay beside a basket, offered by a person who had not known what else to bring.

Sella took it home.

She used it to mend the lining of the blue coat.

Then she hung the coat by the door.

Not because she expected Mara to need it.

Because she was not ready to move it.

No one asked her to.

---

The station changed after Mara’s death.

Not its procedures.

Not immediately.

Its pauses.

The way people looked toward a chair before remembering.

The way Nima nearly sent a question and then sat with it.

The way a story could no longer be checked by walking three streets.

They became more careful with memory.

Not less.

Corrections remained possible.

Mara’s recordings were preserved with dates and uncertainty notes.

Her mistakes stayed in the archive.

So did her repairs.

No one was allowed to turn her into a person who had always known what to do.

Sella insisted on that.

Nima agreed.

The statue proposal was declined.

The handrail fund was expanded instead.

Mara would have used it.

That seemed reason enough.

---

A month later, the green boat brought a letter addressed to Mara.

Nima held it for a long time.

Then took it to Sella.

The seal was ordinary.

The route valid.

The sender was the younger Mara from Iven’s station.

She wanted to know whether the harbor office had found a better way to label the adjustment screws.

Sella laughed through tears.

Then sat down.

“What do I say?”

Nima waited.

At last Sella took a pen.

She answered the technical question first.

There was a better system.

She enclosed the diagram.

Then she explained that the Mara who had corresponded with them had died.

She did not ask the younger woman to replace her.

She did not ask for a visit.

She wrote one memory.

A clock.

A spring.

A burnt onion.

Then she signed her own name.

---

The reply came three weeks later.

It was careful.

The younger Mara expressed sorrow without pretending to share the same loss.

She thanked Sella for the diagram.

She said their new labels were already easier to read.

At the bottom, she asked whether Sella would like them to continue writing.

Sella held the letter by the window.

For several days, she did not answer.

Then she wrote:

*Yes. But as ourselves.*

The reply was one line.

*That seems the safest place to begin.*

Sella smiled.

Not because it was exactly what Mara would have said.

Because it was not.

---

The correspondence became ordinary.

Repairs.

Weather.

Food.

A troublesome pump.

A child learning to write.

A father who came home before four and sometimes burned the fish.

Sella read that line twice.

Then put the letter down.

There was happiness in it.

And grief.

Neither erased the other.

She went to the workshop.

A clock needed cleaning.

Her hands were stiff.

The younger repairer offered help.

She accepted.

Outside, the station bell rang.

Sella did not look up immediately.

She finished placing the smallest gear in its tray.

Then she listened.

---

Nima’s own hair had gone gray.

The apprentices she had trained were training others.

The Two-Count Agreement had been revised again.

Some revisions improved it.

Some required argument.

The refuge search continued.

The garden flourished.

Willowbank sent reports in a confident hand.

Oren’s village had rebuilt the well wall.

The library carriage acquired a second room.

No one could explain where it fitted.

Ves said that was less important than keeping damp out.

The station received letters.

People.

Questions.

Occasionally a cat.

The manual grew.

The kitchen notebook did not.

Three lines remained three lines.

They were copied at the front desk.

Not as law.

As a reminder.

---

One morning, a new apprentice found the original envelope.

The seven-word note.

*When he returns, ask about the lighthouse.*

She had heard the story.

Everyone had.

But the handwriting surprised her.

It looked ordinary.

No flourish.

No solemnity.

Just someone trying to fit a useful instruction onto paper.

She asked Nima why there was no longer warning.

Nima considered.

“Perhaps there should have been.”

“Then why keep it?”

“Because it is what happened.”

The apprentice looked at the envelope again.

“Not what should have happened.”

“Exactly.”

They placed it back in the archive sleeve.

Then went downstairs.

A visitor was waiting.

The apprentice offered a chair before asking for the name.

---

The visitor declined the chair.

He was too restless.

He wanted a route opened immediately.

He had money.

Documents.

A claim of authority.

The apprentice listened.

Then asked who was waiting at the other end.

He said that was none of the station’s concern.

She looked toward Nima.

Nima remained nearby but did not intervene.

The apprentice returned to the visitor.

“It is necessary for safe receiving confirmation.”

“I told you I have authority.”

“I heard you.”

“Then open it.”

“No.”

The room grew quiet.

The apprentice’s hands trembled under the desk.

Her voice stayed level.

“We can review the request. We cannot open an unverified passage into someone else’s home.”

The man left angrily.

The apprentice sat down.

Nima brought tea.

For a while, they said nothing.

Then Nima asked, “What do you need?”

The apprentice laughed shakily.

“A chair, apparently.”

---

At noon, the man returned.

Not to apologize.

To provide the receiving information.

The person at the far end declined contact.

The route remained closed.

The man argued.

Then stopped.

Then asked whether he could send a letter.

The receiving person agreed to receive one letter through the station, with no obligation to reply.

The man wrote it.

The station delivered it.

No passage opened.

The outcome was not what he wanted.

It was what the other person had consented to.

The apprentice recorded the limits.

Nima checked them.

The case did not become simpler because the man had arrived with authority.

It became manageable because someone had asked who else was affected.

---

In Sella’s workshop, a clock struck thirteen.

She looked up.

Then laughed.

The younger repairer frowned.

“Was it supposed to?”

“No.”

“Then why are you laughing?”

Sella wiped her eyes.

“Because I remember one.”

“Should I fix it?”

“Yes.”

She moved aside so he could reach the mechanism.

He examined the gear train.

Found the fault.

Explained it.

Sella listened.

His hands were steadier than hers.

His explanation clearer in one detail she had always skipped.

She let him work.

Outside, the harbor carried on.

Inside, the clock waited for an ordinary repair.

---

The younger Mara eventually sent a photograph.

Not the one with Ada.

A different one.

A lighthouse.

A group standing beside a safely housed instrument.

Iven, older.

Eli, gray at the temples.

Tomas, pretending not to smile.

A young Mara with a notebook.

The picture had been taken after an inspection.

No catastrophe.

No underwater tower.

No nine-year disappearance.

Sella placed it beside a photograph of the harbor station.

She did not imagine the second canceled the first.

Both had happened to someone.

Both mattered.

She wrote back.

*The housing looks excellent. Tomas appears unconvinced.*

The reply came quickly.

*He says the screws are wrong.*

Sella laughed.

Then sent the wider-headed specification.

---

The station’s next difficult case involved a house that remembered too much.

Every room opened onto a different year.

Its residents had learned to navigate by wallpaper.

Then the wallpaper had been replaced.

Now the children could not reliably find the kitchen.

Nima sent a technical team and a family advocate.

The repair required more than stabilizing doors.

Some rooms contained people at earlier points in their lives.

Some were projections.

Some were unresolved routes.

The team would not know which without careful testing.

The residents wanted their house back.

They also wanted certain rooms left untouched.

The work took months.

There were disagreements.

There were pauses.

There were mornings when everyone wished for a simpler problem.

The station stayed with it.

Not every day.

Not alone.

Long enough to learn what was actually there.

---

In the room with yellow wallpaper, they found a woman sewing.

She looked up when the team entered.

“Are you here about the rent?”

“No.”

“Then you’re in the wrong year.”

The operator did not argue.

She introduced herself.

Asked whether the woman knew the rest of the house had changed.

The woman sighed.

“It always does.”

“Can you leave this room?”

“Yes.”

“Do you want to?”

“Not until I finish the sleeve.”

The operator waited.

The woman sewed three more stitches.

Then set the garment aside.

“All right,” she said. “Tell me what you think you know.”

The operator opened her notebook.

Outside the room, the house shifted.

A door closed.

Another opened.

Someone called for a kettle.

Inside, the woman and the operator began with the rent, because it turned out to explain which part of the house had belonged to whom. 
---

The rent records revealed a divided building.

Not magically at first.

Ordinary inheritance.

Ordinary disputes.

A conversion into lodgings.

An unlicensed route installation added to let tenants travel cheaply.

Then a failed update.

Then years of improvised repairs.

The house had become a palimpsest of incompatible assumptions.

The woman in yellow wallpaper was a tenant from forty years earlier.

She had not realized how long she had been staying.

Her room supplied food.

Her sewing work went out through a collection hatch.

Payment arrived.

The system had continued.

No one had asked whether she wished to continue with it.

The operator explained.

The woman listened.

Then looked at the unfinished sleeve.

“I would like,” she said, “to know who has been wearing all these coats.”

They began tracing the shipments.

It was a reasonable question.

---

Some coats had reached ordinary customers.

Some had entered storage.

Some had been delivered to routes no longer active.

The woman had been paid consistently, but the currency had changed value around her.

Her savings were both substantial and inaccessible.

The station brought an advocate.

Not only an engineer.

The woman chose to leave the room after her property was secured.

She took the sewing machine.

The house grew less complicated by one occupied route.

Not empty.

Less trapped.

The residents’ children helped label the stable doors.

This time, the labels described destinations rather than promises.

Kitchen.

Garden.

Front room.

Street.

No before.

No after.

No try again.

The children could find breakfast.

That was the first success the family celebrated.

---

When the report reached Nima, she read the children’s account first.

It was short.

*We don’t have to knock on three doors to find our mother.*

Nima sat with that sentence for a while.

Then she filed it where the technical team would see it.

Not to make them feel heroic.

To keep the purpose near the procedure.

At the harbor, a new sign was being painted above the chandlery.

The old one had faded.

The words remained the same.

**RETURN INQUIRIES — KNOCK FIRST**

The painter asked whether they wanted something grander.

Nima looked at the doorway.

A traveler stood there, uncertain.

She shook her head.

“No. That still works.”

She went to open the door.

---

The traveler carried no map.

No letter.

No object from home.

She remembered only a song.

Nima brought Leth.

Leth was very old now, but music remained a language her hands knew.

The traveler sang softly.

Four notes.

A pause.

Three more.

Leth did not recognize it.

She played it back.

The traveler corrected the third note.

Then began to cry.

“That’s it.”

They recorded the melody.

Sent it to stations.

Asked musicians, not only archivists.

For weeks, nothing.

Then a baker in a river town wrote that his mother had sung it while kneading bread.

He knew another verse.

The traveler heard it.

Laughed.

Then sang the next.

A route had not yet been found.

But a piece of the world had answered.

Nima wrote that in the progress note.

---

The song led to a valley.

The valley led to a village.

The village had changed.

Its old square was now a reservoir.

The traveler’s house had been moved stone by stone to higher ground.

Her family remained.

They had kept singing.

When the passage opened, the first sound that came through was not speech.

It was the tune.

Someone at the receiving station had asked the family what would help her recognize the place.

They had chosen well.

The traveler crossed.

The two counts matched.

Leth lowered her violin.

Her hands trembled.

Nima took the bow gently.

“Rest?”

Leth nodded.

Then, after a moment, she hummed the fourth note correctly.

Some work stayed with the body after the task was done.

---

At home, Sella heard about the song.

She opened the kitchen drawer.

Inside was an old folded invoice.

On the back, six verses.

The fox in the kitchen.

Anja’s handwriting.

One line crossed out and corrected so the rhyme would work.

Mara had kept it all her life.

Sella read it aloud.

The rhythm was familiar now.

She had learned it from Mara.

The song had outlived the voice that first returned it.

Sella took the paper to the archive.

Not to surrender it.

To make a copy.

The original came home.

The copy went into a collection of songs carried through routes.

No one knew whether it would ever help find a place.

It did not need to prove useful before being worth keeping.

---

The collection grew.

Songs.

Recipes.

Local words for rain.

Children’s games.

The shape of bread.

The sound of a harbor bell.

Information no technical catalogue had thought to record.

Nima made sure the collection did not become another trap.

People could contribute.

Refuse.

Correct.

Remove private material.

A voice was not public property because it had crossed a route.

The archivists argued about access.

The travelers argued back.

The policies improved.

Then required revision.

The work remained human in the least convenient ways.

That was not a defect they could eliminate.

It was the reason the records mattered.

---

One day, the archive received a recording of a bell.

Low.

Cracked.

Familiar.

The old bell from *Tomorrow*.

Sella listened.

Her hands went still.

The recording came from a station founded only six months earlier.

Its operator said the sound had arrived before the boat.

Then the boat had brought a box.

Inside were tools, bread, and a manual.

Sella sat down.

Nima, beside her, said nothing.

The operator’s letter continued:

*We do not know who sent it. The manual says to begin with what we know, what we think, and what we have not tested.*

At the bottom was a question.

*May we write to you if we get stuck?*

Nima smiled through tears.

She took a clean sheet.

*Yes,* she wrote.

Then she added the current contact routes, the emergency procedures, and a reminder that help did not depend on joining an exclusive agreement.

Sella read it.

“Send.”

They did.

---

The new station wrote often.

At first, too often.

Questions already answered in the manual.

Questions not answered anywhere.

Questions whose answers depended on local conditions.

Nima and the training team responded.

Sometimes with instructions.

Sometimes with a request for more information.

Sometimes with the name of someone better suited to help.

The new operator learned.

So did the harbor station.

One day, a diagram arrived showing a simpler way to align the receiving tiles.

Arel tested it.

It worked.

The improvement entered the manual.

No one at the new station knew Mara.

That did not prevent them from adding something useful to work she had begun.

It was how the work remained alive.

---

Sella visited the training station one last time to see the revised manual.

The wider screws were there.

The soup recipe.

The warning about accounting mistakes.

The lessons learned from the blocked maintenance signals.

The new tile alignment.

She turned the pages slowly.

Mara’s handwriting appeared in the facsimiles.

Then other hands.

Then diagrams made by people born long after the green boat first arrived.

Sella closed the book.

“Good.”

The instructor waited.

Sella smiled.

“She would have found something to correct.”

“We have a pencil.”

Sella laughed.

Then pointed to a small error in the clock diagram.

The instructor handed her the pencil.

Her hand was unsteady.

The correction was clear.

---

On the way home, Sella stopped at the harbor steps.

The tide was low.

She counted the stones without meaning to.

The second step.

The third.

The place where Mara had climbed down.

A young operator offered an arm.

Sella accepted.

“Did you know her?” he asked.

“Yes.”

He looked embarrassed at the inadequacy of the question.

Sella spared him.

“For a long time.”

“What was she like?”

Sella watched the boats.

Then smiled.

“She burned onions when she was nervous.”

The young man laughed.

He had expected something else.

Sella was glad.

People became easier to admire and harder to know when all their ordinary failures were removed.

She went home.

The kitchen window opened easily.

The latch still worked.

---

The station’s work continued into another season.

A winter storm damaged the quay.

The handrail held.

A summer drought strained the cistern.

Willowbank sent advice.

A route collapsed.

No one was inside.

The report treated it seriously anyway.

A new operator made an excellent decision and could not explain how she had known.

The review helped her identify the cues.

A traveler refused a reunion.

The refusal was respected.

Another requested one.

The station tried.

Some days, the ledger filled.

Some days, nothing unusual happened.

Those days mattered too.

They were when equipment was cleaned, records corrected, and people rested enough to be careful later.

---

At the far end of the mapped network, the new station received its first impossible request.

A child wanted to return a borrowed afternoon.

The operator did not laugh.

She asked what the child meant.

He explained that a woman had given him more time to say goodbye to his grandmother.

Now he wanted to know whether the time belonged to someone else.

The operator listened.

Then wrote to the harbor.

Nima read the request.

She did not know whether the account was literal.

A misunderstanding.

A local expression.

A real temporal intervention.

She asked for the woman’s name.

The child did not know.

A description.

A coat.

Blue.

Broad pockets.

Buttons easy for stiff fingers.

Nima set the letter down.

For a long time, she looked at the page.

Then she wrote the questions she would have written for any case.

What happened?

Who was present?

What changed?

What evidence remained?

A familiar coat was not an answer.

She could honor memory without surrendering judgment.

---

The investigation found no missing time.

The child’s grandmother had been expected to die quickly.

She had lived through the afternoon.

A visiting nurse in a blue coat had explained that the family could stay.

No rule required them to leave the room.

The child had understood this as a gift of time.

In a sense, it was.

The nurse was found.

Her name was Sera.

She had never met Mara.

She was pleased the child had been able to say goodbye.

The station sent the answer gently.

The afternoon did not need returning.

The child replied with a drawing.

A bed.

A window.

Four people.

A blue coat on a hook.

Nima filed it in the appropriate place.

Not under mysteries.

Under care.

---

Sella heard the story and smiled.

Then cried.

Then asked for a copy of the drawing.

The child agreed.

She placed it near the kitchen window.

The blue coat Mara had worn still hung by the door.

Sella touched the sleeve.

“Good work,” she murmured.

Not to the coat.

Not to a ghost.

To the nurse she had never met, and to the child who had asked a question instead of letting it become a burden.

Outside, the harbor bell sounded the hour.

Inside, a clock answered a little late.

Sella listened.

Then reached for her tools.

There was still time to adjust it.

---

The adjustment was small.

A fraction of a turn.

She let the clock run.

Waited.

Compared it with the station signal.

Better.

Not perfect.

She wrote a note for the younger repairer.

Her hands were too tired for another attempt.

He came the next morning.

Read the note.

Made the final adjustment.

Then sat for tea.

Sella told him about the first clock that struck thirteen.

About Mara holding the spring.

About a supper with burnt onions.

He listened.

The clock kept time between them.

When he left, he took the repair order, not the memories.

Those remained with him without needing a case number.

---

At the station, Nima began training a successor.

Not because she planned to leave immediately.

Because she had learned from Mara that waiting until necessity made the choice urgent was unfair to everyone.

The successor’s name was Reni.

She protested.

Nima asked whether the protest meant no.

Reni thought.

Then said, “It means I’m frightened.”

“That is different.”

“Yes.”

“Would you like to learn more before deciding?”

“Yes.”

They made a plan.

Fixed terms.

Shared duties.

Room to withdraw.

No automatic succession through proximity or gratitude.

Reni read the agreement carefully.

Then signed for the training period only.

Nima smiled.

The first lesson was not about authority.

It was about who needed to be consulted before authority could be used.

---

Reni’s first difficult decision concerned the green boat.

Its hull needed rebuilding.

Not a patch.

Not another coat of paint.

A substantial repair.

The mobile office could be transferred temporarily, but no one knew whether every old route association would survive.

Arel proposed a staged process.

The boatbuilder proposed a simpler one.

The archivists wanted complete preservation.

The operators wanted a safe vessel.

Reni listened to everyone.

Then asked which parts were physically necessary, which carried route functions, and which mattered because people remembered them.

The lists overlapped.

Not completely.

The repair plan preserved what could be preserved, recorded what had to change, and refused to keep rotten timber merely because it was old.

The stern seat remained.

The keyhole remained.

The hull became sound.

When the boat returned to water, the office below was still there.

The stove drew properly.

The window showed a garden.

No one called it unchanged.

They called it ready.

---

Sella did not come to the relaunch.

She was too tired.

Reni visited afterward with a photograph.

The green boat rested bright against the quay.

People stood around it.

Another group.

Another day.

Sella looked carefully.

“Different green.”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

Reni smiled.

“Would Mara have approved?”

Sella considered.

“She would have asked what you checked.”

Reni told her.

The hull.

The routes.

The office.

The emergency exits.

The bell.

Sella nodded.

“Then you don’t need her approval.”

Reni sat with that for a moment.

It was not the answer she had hoped for.

It was better.

---

That winter, Sella’s workshop passed to the younger repairer.

He kept her name on the sign only until she asked him to change it.

“It’s your work now.”

“People know the old name.”

“Then they can learn yours.”

He changed it.

Inside, one bench remained hers for as long as she wanted it.

She used it less often.

The tools stayed sharp.

The clocks continued arriving.

Some could be repaired.

Some could not.

The younger repairer learned to say which without making a promise first.

Sella watched.

She was proud.

She told him.

He looked startled.

Then pleased.

Ordinary praise, given while there was time to hear it, seemed to her increasingly worth the effort.

---

Nima visited Sella on a clear morning.

They sat in the kitchen.

The old bell.

The straight shelf.

The ordinary notebook.

The blue coat.

Nima knew the room as part of her own life now, not only Mara’s.

Sella asked about the station.

Nima answered.

Then asked about the clock on the table.

Sella explained the mechanism.

They spent an hour on it.

No routes.

No governance.

Just gears.

At the end, Nima said, “I should have learned this sooner.”

Sella smiled.

“You’re learning it now.”

Nima laughed softly.

“That sounds like something she’d say.”

“It may have been something I said first.”

They smiled at one another.

The distinction mattered less than the fact that both could remember.

---

The next letter from the younger Mara described an ordinary argument with her father.

He had repaired a shelf without asking.

It was in the wrong place.

She had been unkind.

He had been stubborn.

They had moved it together the next day.

Sella read the letter by the window.

She could see both of them.

Not the people she had known exactly.

People with room to make their own mistakes.

She wrote back about measuring twice.

Then crossed out the advice.

Instead, she told the story of the crooked kitchen shelf.

The younger Mara replied with a drawing of a shelf at a heroic angle.

Sella laughed.

The correspondence continued.

Not as replacement.

As company.

---

At the harbor station, a visitor asked whether the office could show him a life in which he had made no mistakes.

Reni thought he was joking.

He was not.

She asked what he hoped to gain.

“Proof that it was possible.”

“And if you found it?”

“I’d know.”

“What would you do with knowing?”

He looked at the floor.

“I don’t know.”

Reni offered no route.

She offered a conversation.

The man stayed.

They spoke for an hour.

At the end, he did not seem satisfied.

He did seem less urgent.

Sometimes that was the only honest outcome available.

Reni recorded no diagnosis.

No grand lesson.

Only the request, the response, and the fact that no passage had been opened.

---

A week later, the man returned.

Not to ask again.

To bring a broken toy boat.

His daughter’s.

He had snapped the mast while trying to repair the sail.

He wanted to know whether someone could show him how to fix it properly.

Reni directed him to the workshop.

The repairer gave him a sanding block.

He worked carefully.

The daughter watched.

When the mast was sound, she tested it.

Then nodded.

The man smiled with an expression Reni had not seen during the first visit.

No alternate life required.

Only a repair in this one.

She did not tell him that.

He had found it himself.

---

The station’s archive received a new box.

Sella had sent the kitchen notebook for copying.

Not surrender.

The three lines were photographed.

The pages about pears, latches, visits, and screws were copied too, with permission.

The archivist asked whether the ordinary shopping notes belonged in the record.

Nima said yes.

Reni asked why.

Nima thought of the beginning.

The basket.

The market.

The life that had existed before the impossible boat.

“Because otherwise,” she said, “people will think she spent every hour being the person in the stories.”

Reni nodded.

The notebook returned to the kitchen.

The copies remained.

On one page, in a shaky hand, was a single line.

**Blue thread.**

No explanation.

No need for one.

---

The final page was blank.

The archivist wanted to note that.

Nima agreed.

Not every blank needed interpretation.

Some were simply pages not yet used.

At the kitchen table, Sella opened the notebook to that page.

She considered writing a last message.

Something for the station.

For the younger Mara.

For whoever would sort the house one day.

Then she wrote:

**The pantry hinge squeaks. Oil, not force.**

She closed the book.

Practical enough.

True.

Likely to help someone.

Outside, the harbor bell rang.

Inside, she listened to the hinge as she opened the pantry.

It did squeak.

She would ask the younger repairer to bring the oil.

---

He came that afternoon.

Fixed the hinge.

Stayed for tea.

Sella told him to keep the note in the notebook even though the task was done.

“Why?”

“Because someone may wonder whether it was checked.”

He smiled.

Then added the date and his initials.

Sella approved.

The page now held a problem and its repair.

A small complete record.

There would be larger incomplete ones elsewhere.

Both belonged in a world where people tried to leave enough information for the next person.

---

At the station, the live witness from the sealed letter returned in person.

The inquiry had found uncounted arrivals at three offices.

Two had corrected their records.

The third had refused access.

The review group was negotiating.

The witness was tired.

Reni offered a chair.

She accepted.

“Do you think it will work?” she asked.

Reni did not promise.

“I think we have evidence. I think more people are paying attention. I think the travelers involved have representation now.”

“That isn’t yes.”

“No.”

The woman looked at the cup in her hands.

Then nodded.

“It’s more than I had.”

They discussed what she needed for the next stage.

Rest.

Travel costs.

Someone else to hold the documents for a week.

The station arranged all three.

Persistence did not have to mean one person carrying everything without pause.

---

The third office opened its records two months later.

Not willingly.

Not entirely.

Enough to begin.

More names emerged.

More routes.

Some people were found.

Some were not.

The ledger grew.

The work did not become less worthwhile because the totals were painful.

Reni learned to distinguish urgency from panic.

Nima learned to let her.

Arel built better auditing tools.

The travelers’ representatives insisted that numbers be linked to people’s own accounts.

The reports became longer.

They also became harder to use as camouflage.

That was progress of a kind.

Not the kind that rang a bell.

The kind that changed what a future clerk could get away with omitting.

---

Sella asked for the outcome.

Reni told her.

She listened from her chair by the window.

At the end, she said, “Mara would have wanted to know the names.”

“We have them.”

“Good.”

Reni began to offer the list.

Sella shook her head.

“Not for me to read all of them now. Just make sure they remain names.”

Reni understood.

She thought she did.

Later, at the station, she reviewed the report headings.

Affected units.

Pending cases.

Unresolved persons.

She changed the public summary to use people’s names where they had consented.

Where they had not, she preserved their privacy.

Neither anonymity nor exposure would be automatic.

She sent the revision for review.

---

That evening, a child arrived with a question about the stars.

Not a route.

Not a lost family.

The stars.

She had seen a chart at the station showing a sky with unfamiliar constellations.

She wanted to know whether they were real.

Arel brought out the chart.

“Yes.”

“Can we go there?”

“Not from the chart alone.”

“Could someone?”

“Possibly.”

The child traced one bright point.

“Does anyone live there?”

“We don’t know.”

She looked delighted rather than disappointed.

“Then there are still things.”

Arel smiled.

“Yes.”

The child asked for a copy.

They made one.

She left holding it flat so it would not crease.

Reni watched her go.

There were still things.

That was both the difficulty and the invitation.

---

In the training room, the map was added to an exercise.

Students were asked what could be concluded from it.

Positions.

Relative brightness.

A likely observation point.

Not inhabitants.

Not safety.

Not invitation.

The students argued.

Then revised.

One wrote at the bottom:

**Unknown does not mean empty.**

The instructor circled it.

Not as a complete principle.

As a useful caution.

Another student added:

**Known does not mean understood.**

The instructor smiled.

The class continued.

Outside, rain began.

Inside, someone asked whether the roof had been checked before the wet season.

The maintenance log said yes.

The first drip fell anyway.

They moved the books.

Then fetched a bucket.

---

The leak was above the oldest shelf.

The one holding the first field manual.

The books were saved.

The plaster was not.

A repairer climbed onto the roof and found a cracked tile.

Not a route anomaly.

Not sabotage.

Weather and age.

Reni stood below with the bucket.

Nima, visiting rather than working, watched with amusement.

“Every institution,” she said, “eventually returns to the roof.”

Reni looked up.

“Did Mara say that?”

“No. That one is mine.”

They laughed.

The repairer asked for another tile.

Arel brought it.

The rain continued.

The bucket filled slowly.

The manual remained dry.

Good enough for the afternoon.

---

After the repair, they found writing on the underside of the old roof beam.

Not ancient.

Tomas’s hand.

**THIS WILL NEED DOING AGAIN.**

No date.

No prediction.

A practical statement.

Reni photographed it before the plaster went back.

Nima laughed for a long time.

Then wiped her eyes.

They added the photograph to the archive.

The maintenance schedule was updated.

Tomas had been right.

It would need doing again.

Most useful work did.

---

Sella heard about the beam and smiled.

“He always did know where the rot was.”

Nima nodded.

They sat together.

The room was quieter now.

Sella tired easily.

Nima did not stay long.

At the door, Sella called her back.

“The coat.”

Nima looked at the blue wool hanging by the pantry.

“Yes?”

“When I no longer need to see it there, send it somewhere cold.”

Nima swallowed.

“All right.”

“Not a museum.”

“No.”

Sella nodded.

Then smiled faintly.

“It has good pockets.”

Nima touched the sleeve.

“Yes,” she said. “It does.”

---

The coat remained through another winter.

Sella used it sometimes when the house felt cold.

It fit differently.

That did not matter.

In spring, she sat in the garden wearing it while the herb grew between the stones.

A young neighbor came to read aloud.

Letters.

Stories.

Occasionally technical reports, if Sella asked.

One afternoon, she requested the first page of the manual.

The neighbor read.

Then the next.

At the soup recipe, Sella laughed.

“Keep that one.”

“It’s already in every copy.”

“Good.”

The neighbor continued.

Sella closed her eyes.

The words were familiar.

Not all of them.

Enough.

---

When Sella died, the kitchen window was open.

The pantry hinge did not squeak.

The clock kept time.

Nima came.

Reni came.

Ada was told.

The younger Mara received a letter.

The workshop closed for the afternoon.

At the station, someone brought tea to a traveler who did not know why the staff spoke more quietly.

The work continued.

Not because grief was unimportant.

Because someone still needed a chair.

---

The blue coat went north.

To a station where winter was long and fuel scarce.

Its new wearer was an apprentice with cold hands and a habit of carrying too many small tools.

She loved the pockets.

She knew only that the coat had been well cared for.

There was no hidden letter.

No obligation.

No claim on her future.

Inside one pocket, she found a short length of blue thread.
